The law of the circumference of sparse binomial random graphs
Michael Anastos 1, Joshua Erde 2 and Mihyun Kang 2 and Vincent Pfenninger 2

Abstract
. There has been much interest in the distribution of the circumference , the length of the longest cycle, of a random graph G ⁡ ( n , p ) G(n,p) in the sparse regime, when p = Θ ⁡ ( 1 n ) p=\Theta\left(\frac{1}{n}\right) . Recently, the first author and Frieze established a scaling limit for the circumference in this regime, along the way establishing an alternative ‘structural’ approximation for this parameter. In this paper, we give a central limit theorem for the circumference in this regime using a novel argument based on the Efron–Stein inequality, which relies on a combinatorial analysis of the effect of resampling edges on this approximation.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Motivation and results

In this paper we will study the
circumference
, the length of the longest cycle, of a
sparse
random graph
G(n,p)
, where
p=\frac{c}{n}
for some constant
c>0
. Answering a well-known conjecture of Erdős
[
16
]
, Ajtai, Komlós and Szemerédi
[
1
]
showed the existence of a function
\ell\colon(1,\infty)\to(0,\infty)
such that if
c>1
, then whp
1
1

1

With probability tending to
1
as
n
tends to infinity.
the circumference of
G\left(n,\frac{c}{n}\right)
is at least
\ell(c)\cdot n
, where we note that a slightly weaker result was also proved independently by Fernandez de la Vega
[
17
]
.
Since then, the problem of proving more precise results about the distribution of the circumference of sparse random graphs has attracted considerable interest.

Given a graph
G
let us write
L(G)
for the circumference of
G
. We define

\ell_{\textrm{min}}(c)~\coloneqq~\sup\left\{\alpha>0\colon L\left(G\left(n,\frac{c}{n}\right)\right)\geq\alpha n\quad\text{whp}\right\}.

We note that it is easy to show that
\ell\to 1
as
c\to\infty
and
\ell\to 0
as
c\to 1
.
Early work, of Bollobás
[
9
]
, of Bollobás, Fenner and Frieze
[
11
]
and of Frieze
[
19
]
, focused on the asymptotic dependence of the function
1-\ell_{\textrm{min}}
on
c
as
c\to\infty
. In particular, Frieze
[
19
]
determined the value of
1-\ell_{\textrm{min}}(c)
up to a
1+o_{c}(1)
multiplicative factor. On the other hand, the
weakly supercritical regime
, where
\varepsilon\coloneqq c-1
is such that
\varepsilon^{3}n\to\infty
and
\varepsilon
is sufficiently small, has also been studied
[
29
,
32
]
. In particular, the current best known asymptotic bounds are

1.581\varepsilon^{2}\leq\ell_{\textrm{min}}(1+\varepsilon)\leq 1.7395\varepsilon^{2},

where the lower bound is due to the first author
[
2
]
and the upper bound is due to Kemkes and Wormald
[
29
]
.

A recent result of the first author and Frieze
[
5
]
proved that, for large enough
c
, the circumference has a
scaling limit
.

Theorem 1.1

(
[
5
, Theorem 1.6]
)
.

Let
c>1
be a sufficiently large constant. Then there exists a function
f\colon(1,\infty)\to(0,\infty)
such that

\frac{L\left(G\left(n,\frac{c}{n}\right)\right)}{n}\quad\to\quad f(c)\qquad\text{almost surely, as $n\rightarrow\infty$.}

Whilst the function
f
in
Theorem

1.1
is not explicit, the authors gave a method to compute
f
to an arbitrary level of precision. Similarly, the bound on
c
in
Theorem

1.1
was not made explicit, but parts of the arguments require that
c\geq 10^{6}
. However, the result was later shown to hold for all
c\geq 20
by the first author
[
3
, Theorem 1]
.

A natural question to ask, once the existence of a scaling limit has been established, is whether we can determine more precisely the distribution of
L\left(G\left(n,\frac{c}{n}\right)\right)
, and in particular whether it satisfies a
central limit theorem
.
Here, we say that a sequence of random variables
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

satisfies a central limit theorem
if

\frac{X_{n}-\mathbb{E}\left[X_{n}\right]}{\sqrt{\mathbb{V}\left[X_{n}\right]}}\quad\overset{d}{\to}\quad\mathcal{N}(0,1),

as
n\rightarrow\infty
, that is, if
\frac{X_{n}-\mathbb{E}\left[X_{n}\right]}{\sqrt{\mathbb{V}\left[X_{n}\right]}}
converges in distribution to the standard normal distribution.

Establishing central limit theorems for various graph parameters of
G(n,p)
has become an important area of research. A variety of tools have been used including the method of moments
[
26
,
27
,
37
]
, Stein’s method
[
7
]
,
U
-statistics and related methods
[
23
,
34
]
, and martingale methods
[
6
,
14
]
. For example, central limit theorems have been established for various types of subgraph counts
[
20
,
26
,
27
,
37
,
38
]
, for the order of the giant component
[
8
,
12
,
35
,
36
]

and for the order of the
k
-core
[
13
,
24
]
.

All of these parameters are in some sense ‘local’, in that they can be determined by looking at bounded size subgraphs of
G(n,p)
, or have natural ‘local’ approximations. More recently, central limit theorems have been proven for more ‘global’ graph parameters, such as the rank
[
21
]
and the matching number
[
22
,
31
]
by analysing the
Karp–Sipser leaf-removal process

[
28
]
.

Our main result gives a central limit theorem for the circumference
L\left(G\left(n,\frac{c}{n}\right)\right)
, which has a similar ‘global’ nature, in the sparse regime.
In fact, our methods allow us to prove slightly more, and to give quantitative bounds on the variance of
L\left(G\left(n,\frac{c}{n}\right)\right)
.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
c\geq 20
be a fixed constant and let
G\sim G\left(n,\frac{c}{n}\right)
.
Then

\frac{L(G)-\mathbb{E}\left[L(G)\right]}{\sqrt{\mathbb{V}\left[L(G)\right]}}\quad\overset{d}{\to}\quad\mathcal{N}(0,1)

as
n\to\infty
.
Furthermore, the limit
\displaystyle\sigma\coloneqq\lim_{n\to\infty}\left(\frac{\mathbb{V}\left[L(G)\right]}{n}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
exists and there exist constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that
C_{1}e^{-10c}\leq\sigma\leq C_{2}c^{\frac{1}{2}}
.

We note that, as in the result of
[
22
]
on a central limit theorem for the matching number of a sparse random graph, we prove our central limit theorem
non-constructively
, in the sense that the best known bounds on
\mathbb{E}\left[L\left(G\left(n,\frac{c}{n}\right)\right)\right]
are much less accurate than the typical fluctuations of
L\left(G\left(n,\frac{c}{n}\right)\right)
implied by
Theorem

1.2
.

For completeness, we also include the next proposition, whose proof is in
Appendix

A
, which describes how the law of the circumference behaves for larger
p
. The logarithms here and in the remainder of the paper are natural logartithms.

Proposition 1.3
.

Let
\lambda(n)
be a sequence of real numbers,
c\coloneqq\log n+\log\log n+\lambda(n)
, and let
G\sim G\left(n,\frac{c}{n}\right)
.

(1)

If
c\to+\infty
and
\lambda(n)\to-\infty
, then as
n\to\infty

\frac{L(G)-\mathbb{E}\left[L(G)\right]}{\sqrt{\mathbb{V}\left[L(G)\right]}}\quad\overset{d}{\to}\quad\mathcal{N}(0,1).

(2)

If
\lambda(n)\to\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, then as
n\to\infty

n-L(G)\quad\overset{d}{\to}\quad\mathrm{Poisson}\left(e^{-\lambda}\right).

(3)

If
\lambda(n)\to\infty
, then whp
L(G)=n
.

1.2.
Proof methods

Let us fix some large constant
c
and let
G\sim G\left(n,\frac{c}{n}\right)
. In
[
3
]
the first author describes an algorithmic process, similar in nature to the
peeling process
used to produce the
4
-core of a graph, which produces a
3
-colouring
2
2

2

Note, this is not a
proper
colouring of
V(G)
, that is, the colour classes are not necessarily independent.
of
V(G)
, whose colour classes we will later denote by
S
,
P
, and
R
(we use the colours
sapphire
,
purple
, and
red
, respecitvely).
The set
S
is a ‘robust’ version of the
4
-core of
G
, the set
P
is the external neighbourhood of
S
, that is
P\coloneqq N(S)
, and the set
R
is the remaining set of vertices, that is,
R\coloneqq V(G)\setminus(S\cup P)
. He goes on to show that there is some simple function
\Phi(G)
which depends only on the induced subgraph
G[P\cup R]
such that whp
L(G)=n-\Phi(G)
.

Indeed, the rough idea of
[
3
]
is as follows : It can be shown that
G
contains no edges from
S
to
R
, and so given any cycle
\widetilde{C}\subseteq G
, if we delete the vertices of
\widetilde{C}
which lie in
S
, what remains can be split into a vertex-disjoint union of paths, all of whose endpoints lie in
P
, which we call a
family of
P
-paths
. Conversely, it is shown in
[
3
]
that whp the subgraph
G[S\cup P]
has such a rich structure that we can extend
any
family of
P
-paths in
G[P\cup R]
into a cycle which contains every vertex of
S\cup P
. Hence, whp the length of the longest cycle in
G
will be determined by the minimum number of vertices which are left
uncovered
by a family of
P
-paths in
G[P\cup R]
, which is the quantity denoted by
\Phi(G)
. Since this function is determined by the component structure of
G[P\cup R]
(as a coloured graph), it is relatively easy to show that, when
c\to\infty
, the only non-neglible contribution comes from vertices of degree at most one, which
can be controlled precisely using standard techniques, leading to
Proposition

1.3
. When
c
is a fixed constant however, we have to take into account all possible components, which requires more novel ideas.

The colouring process that identifies the sets
S,P
and
R
will also be key to our proof, and will be described in more detail in
Subsection

2.1
. It is shown in
[
3
]
that the probability that
L(G)
is not equal to
n-\Phi(G)=:\widetilde{L}
is sufficiently small that it will be sufficient to prove a central limit theorem for
\widetilde{L}
, which we will do so via a series of approximations.

For the first approximation, since the value of the function
\Phi
can be split additively over the components of
G[P\cup R]
, we can think of each
v\in V(G)
as having a contribution to
\Phi
which is defined by splitting the contribution from each component equally between its vertices, which we denote by
\phi(v)
, so that
\widetilde{L}=n-\sum_{v\in V(G)}\phi(v)
.
We then approximate the colouring process
locally
for each
v\in V
, by running a similar algorithm restricted to the
k
-th neighbourhood of
v
, which we denote by
B(v,k)
. We can consider then the contribution of
v
to the value of
\Phi
on
B(v,k)
with respect to this local colouring, which we denote by
\phi_{k}(v)
. We will show that once
k
is large enough so that the component
C_{v}
of
G[P\cup R]
that contains
v
is a subset of
B(v,k-1)
, then
\phi_{k}(v)=\phi(v)
.
Hence, if we expect the typical component in
G[P\cup R]
to be small, then we should expect that as
k\to\infty
, the quanitity
n-\sum_{v\in V(G)}\phi_{k}(v)\eqqcolon\widetilde{L}_{k}
is a good approximation to
n-\sum_{v\in V(G)}\phi(v)=\widetilde{L}
.

One of the key ideas in the paper will be to show that we can control quite precisely how the typical (global and local) colourings change when we resample an edge in
G\left(n,\frac{c}{n}\right)
. This will allow us to use the Efron–Stein inequality (
Theorem

3.5
) to bound the variance of
\widetilde{L}-\widetilde{L}_{k}
, after which it will suffice to prove a central limit theorem for
\widetilde{L}_{k}
.

In order to prove a central limit theorem for
\widetilde{L}_{k}
, it will be useful to rewrite
\widetilde{L}_{k}
as follows. Since
\phi_{k}(v)
is determined by
G[B(v,k)]
, we can view the random variable
\widetilde{L}_{k}
as a weighted sum of
k
-th neighbourhood counts
. Let
\mathcal{H}
be the set of rooted graphs
(H,r)
of
radius
at most
k
, that is, such that
\mathrm{dist}_{H}(r,v)\leq k
for all
v\in V(H)
. Then for every
(H,r)\in\mathcal{H}
, there is some constant
\alpha_{(H,r)}
such that if
(G[B_{G}(v,k)],v)
and
(H,r)
are isomorphic as rooted graphs, then
1-\phi_{k}(v)=\alpha_{(H,r)}
. Hence, we can rewrite

\displaystyle\widetilde{L}_{k}

\displaystyle\coloneqq n-\sum_{v\in V(G)}\phi_{k}(v)=\sum_{v\in V(G)}(1-\phi_{k}(v))=\sum_{v\in V(G)}\sum_{(H,r)\in\mathcal{H}}\alpha_{(H,r)}\mathds{1}_{\{(G[B_{G}(v,k)],v)\cong(H,r)\}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(H,r)\in\mathcal{H}}\alpha_{(H,r)}\sum_{v\in V(G)}\mathds{1}_{\{(G[B_{G}(v,k)],v)\cong(H,r)\}}

(1)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(H,r)\in\mathcal{H}}\alpha_{(H,r)}\cdot\#\{\text{$k$-th neighbourhoods isomorphic to $(H,r)$}\}.

It is known, see for example
[
37
]
, that for a fixed graph
H
, the number of copies of
H
in
G(n,p)
satisfies a central limit theorem when
p
is sufficiently large and not too close to one, more concretely when
n^{2}(1-p)\to\infty
and
np^{m_{H}}\to\infty
where

m_{H}~\coloneqq~\max\left\{\frac{e(H^{\prime})}{v(H^{\prime})}\colon H^{\prime}\subsetneq H\right\}

is the
maximum density
of
H
.
In fact, it can even be shown (see, e.g.,
[
23
]
) that for any finite collections of graphs which simultaneously satisfy the above conditions, the corresponding subgraph counts satisfy a multidimensional central limit theorem, and hence, any linear combination of them satisfies a central limit theorem. Hence, it is not unreasonable to think that something similar might hold for the weighted sum of neighbourhood counts in (
1
).

However, there are some difficulties here. Firstly the sum in (
1
) is not over a bounded number of rooted graphs,
and secondly there are rooted graphs
(H,r)
which will contribute to this sum for which we should not expect the neighbourhood counts to satisfy a central limit theorem in this regime of
p
as their maximum density
m_{H}
is too large, for example whenever
H
is not a tree.

For this reason we make a second approximation. Letting
\mathcal{T}
be the set of rooted trees
(T,r)\in\mathcal{H}
with
1\leq v(T)\leq(10ck)^{2k}
we consider

(2)

\widehat{L}_{k}~\coloneqq~\sum_{(T,r)\in\mathcal{T}}\alpha_{(T,r)}\cdot\#\{\text{$k$-th neighbourhoods isomorphic to $(T,r)$}\}.

It is reasonable to think that
\widehat{L}_{k}
and
\widetilde{L}_{k}
are close, since our choice of
p=\frac{c}{n}
means that for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
v\in V(G)
it is very likely that
(G[B(v,k)],v)\in\mathcal{T}
. Indeed, standard results imply that whp
G
contains no cycle of length at most
k
, in which case
G[B_{G}(v,k)]
is a tree, and since the expected degree of a vertex in
G
is
\approx c
, the probability that
|B(v,k)|
is significantly larger than
c^{k}
will tend to
0
as a function of
k
. Using this, together with an analysis of how
\widetilde{L}_{k}
and
\widehat{L}_{k}
change under resampling edges, we will again be able to use the Efron–Stein inequality to bound the variance of
\widetilde{L}_{k}-\widehat{L}_{k}
, after which it will suffice to prove a central limit theorem for
\widehat{L}_{k}
.

Finally, we will be able to show, using a version of Stein’s method developed by Barbour, Karoński and Ruciński
[
7
]
, that
\widehat{L}_{k}
satisfies a central limit theorem as
n\to\infty
for any fixed
k\in\mathbb{N}
, from which we will be able to deduce a central limit theorem for the circumference (
Theorem

1.2
) using our sequence of approximations.

However, a small technical issue with the application of Stein’s method is that we will require a lower bound on the variance of
\widehat{L}_{k}
, which turns out to be deceptively difficult to prove. In order to do so we will first prove a lower bound on the variance of
\widetilde{L}_{k}
using a delicate partial exposure argument (see
Section

6
), which we will be able to use to give a sufficient bound on the variance of
\widehat{L}_{k}
, as well as to give the quantitative lower bound on the variance of
L(G)
in
Theorem

1.2
(the upper bound is much simpler to show).

We note that these last two steps, approximating the weighted neighbourhood sum
\widetilde{L}_{k}
by an appropriate truncation
\widehat{L}_{k}
and showing that this truncation satisfies a central limit theorem, do not require much about the specific form of
\widetilde{L}_{k}
beyond that it is a weighted neighbourhood sum whose variance is not too small.

1.3.
Main contributions of the paper

Our aim in this paper is not just to prove a central limit theorem for the circumference (
Theorem

1.2
), but also to demonstrate a novel method for proving central limit theorems for graph parameters in sparse random graphs. We believe this method will have applications more generally. The rough idea is to take a series of finer and finer
local
appromixations to the parameter, each of which can be shown to satisfy a central limit theorem using Stein’s Method, and then to transfer this information ‘through the limit’ to the original parameter. This is achieved using the Efron-Stein inequality (
Theorem

3.5
), which is one of the main novelties in the paper. The Efron–Stein inequality allows us to reduce this problem to a more combinatorial question about the
robustness
of these local approximations under the
resampling
of edges, that is, how do these local approximations change if we add or delete an edge.
This question is addressed then in
Section

4
.

However, deducing a central limit theorem for these local approximations is also not entirely straightforward. In particular, one difficulty that arises here is that proving the lower bound on the variance required by Stein’s method through direct computation does not appear feasible.
This is probably typical for any application that is not amenable to simpler methods. To overcome this difficulty in our case, we show in
Section

6
how we can obtain the desired lower bound through a subtle argument that involves revealing the random edges over several rounds.

1.4.
Structure of the paper

The paper is structured as follows. In the next section we define precisely the global and local colouring process and our sequence of approximations and give a detailed outline of the proof of
Theorem

1.2
. Then, in
Section

3
we introduce various notation and tools that we will use throughout the paper. In
Section

4
we prove some results about how the global and local colourings are affected by resampling an edge. In
Section

5
we apply the Efron–Stein inequality, using the information from
Section

4
, to bound the variances of the differences in our approximation steps.
Section

6
is the dedicated to proving a lower bound on the variance of
\widehat{L}_{k}
. Finally, in
Section

7
we give a central limit theorem for
\widehat{L}_{k}
, together with some quantitative analysis of its variance which will imply the quantitative aspects of
Theorem

1.2
. We finish in
Section

8
with a general discussion of our methods and some open problems and directions for future research.

2.
Proof of Theorem
1.2

In this section we will formally define the three approximations we make for
L\left(G\left(n,\frac{c}{n}\right)\right)
and give a detailed outline of the proof of
Theorem

1.2
. During this section we will state without proof the auxiliary results (
Lemmas

2.5
,
2.3
,
2.7
, and
2.8
) which will allow us to prove
Theorem

1.2
and defer their proofs until later in the paper.

2.1.
The strong
4
-core and a local approximation

Let
G
be a graph. For
A\subseteq V(G)
, we denote by
N_{G}(A)
the
external neighbourhood of
A
in
G
, that is,
N_{G}(A)\coloneqq\{x\in V(G)\setminus A\colon xy\in E(G)\text{ for some }y\in A\}
. For
x\in V(G)
, we also write
N_{G}(x)
for
N_{G}(\{x\})
.
For a subgraph
H
of
G
and a set
A\subseteq V(G)
, we let
H-A
be the subgraph of
H
obtained by deleting all vertices in
V(H)\cap A
.
For
x,y\in V(G)
, we denote by
\mathrm{dist}_{G}(x,y)
the
graph-distance in
G
from
x
to
y
, that is, the length of a shortest path from
x
to
y
(or
\infty
if no such path exists). For
x\in V(G)
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we let
\partial_{G}(x,k)\coloneqq\{y\in V(G)\colon\mathrm{dist}_{G}(x,y)=k\}
and
B_{G}(x,k)\coloneqq\{y\in V(G)\colon\mathrm{dist}_{G}(x,y)\leq k\}
.

We say that a set
A\subseteq V(G)
has the
strong
4
-core property for
G
if

(3)

\displaystyle|N_{G}(v)\cap A|\geq 4\quad\text{ for every }v\in A\cup N_{G}(A).

We call the maximal set
A\subseteq V(G)
that has the strong
4
-core property for
G
the
strong
4
-core of
G
and denote it by
S(G)
. Note that
S(G)
is well-defined, since if two sets
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
have the strong
4
-core property for
G
, then so does
A_{1}\cup A_{2}
.
Moreover, we define

P(G)\coloneqq N_{G}(S(G))\quad\text{ and }\quad R(G)\coloneqq V(G)\setminus(S(G)\cup P(G)).

If
G
is clear from context, we simply write
S
,
P
, and
R
for
S(G)
,
P(G)
, and
R(G)
, respectively. Inspired by the work in
[
5
]
, the first author introduced the strong
4
-core to study long cycles in sparse random graphs
[
3
]
.

The strong
4
-core can also be obtained by a colouring process as follows.
3
3

3

We will call this the
global colouring process
.

Algorithm 1:
Global colouring process (
[
3
, Algorithm 1]
)

1:

A graph
G

2:

S(G)
, the strong
4
-core of
G

3:

Initiate the colour of all vertices to be sapphire.

4:

Set
t\coloneqq 1
.

5:

while
there exists a sapphire or purple vertex
x
with fewer than
4
sapphire neighbours
do

6:

Colour
x
red and all its sapphire neighbours purple.

7:

Set
v_{t}\coloneqq x
and
t\coloneqq t+1
.

8:

end

while

9:

return
the set of sapphire vertices.

Note that the final colouring of
G
is determined by the sequence
v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,
of vertices which are coloured red. Let us show that
Subsection

2.1
does indeed return the strong
4
-core of
G
.
Let
S^{\prime}
be the output of
Subsection

2.1
.
Note that any vertex in the strong
4
-core
S
will never be recoloured. Indeed, suppose for a contradiction that
x
is the first vertex in
S
that is recoloured. Then just before
x
is recoloured either
x
or one of its neighbours has fewer than
4
sapphire neighbours, a contradiction to the fact that
S
has the strong
4
-core property
3
for
G
.
Thus,
S\subseteq S^{\prime}
. Moreover, since at any step in the algorithm the neighbourhood of the set of sapphire vertices is the set of purple vertices, which can be shown inductively,
S^{\prime}
satisfies the strong
4
-core property for
G
and thus
S^{\prime}\subseteq S
. Hence,
S^{\prime}=S
.

We will on occasion make use of this alternative way of finding the strong
4
-core to prove a property about
S
,
P
, or
R
.
In view of
Subsection

2.1
, we call vertices in
S
,
P
, and
R
,
sapphire
,
purple
, and
red
vertices, respectively (see
Figure

1
). This means that these expressions will mean, from now on, the colour of the vertices at the end of the global colouring process, unless otherwise stated.

G
…
…
R

(red vertices)

P

(purple vertices)

S

(sapphire vertices)

Figure 1.
The
3
-colouring of a graph
G
given by
Subsection

2.1
. Note that every vertex in
S\cup P
has at least
4
neighbours in
S
and there is no edge between
R
and
S
.

Given a graph
H
and
W\subseteq V(H)
, we define
\mathrm{uc}(H;W)
to be the minimal number of vertices in
V(H)\setminus W
left
uncovered
by a set
\mathcal{P}
of vertex-disjoint paths in
H
all of whose endpoints are in
W
, which we call a
family of
W
-paths in
H
. Note that, by adding single vertices of
W
(as paths of length
0
) to
\mathcal{P}
if needed, we may assume that
\mathcal{P}
covers all vertices in
W
.
Let

\Phi(G)~\coloneqq~\mathrm{uc}(G[P\cup R];P).

For any cycle
\widetilde{C}
in
G
which contains a vertex of
S
, the components of
\widetilde{C}-S
form a family of
P
-paths in
G[P\cup R]
and thus
\widetilde{C}
leaves at least
\Phi(G)
vertices of
G
uncovered.
Perhaps surprisingly, the first author showed in
[
3
]
that in
G\sim G(n,p)
this is essentially tight, by showing that with very high probability the longest cycle in
G
covers all but
\Phi(G)
many vertices, provided that
np\geq 20
. So, let us define

\widetilde{L}(G)~\coloneqq~n-\Phi(G),

where when
G
is clear from context, we write
L
and
\widetilde{L}
for
L(G)
and
\widetilde{L}(G)
, respectively.

Theorem 2.1

(
[
3
]
, Theorem 11)
.

Let
p\coloneqq p(n)\in[0,1]
be such that
20\leq pn\leq 2\log n
and
G\sim G(n,p)
. Then, the equality
L(G)=\widetilde{L}(G)
holds with probability at least
1-O(n^{-2})
.

Since on occasion it will be convenient to talk about appropriate
rescalings
of these random variables, let us also define

X(G)~\coloneqq~\frac{L(G)-\mathbb{E}\left[L(G)\right]}{n^{\frac{1}{2}}}\quad\text{ and }\quad\widetilde{X}(G)~\coloneqq~\frac{\widetilde{L}(G)-\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{L}(G)\right]}{n^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

If
G
is clear from context, we write
X
and
\widetilde{X}
for
X(G)
and
\widetilde{X}(G)
, respectively. We note that an immediate consequence of
Theorem

2.1
is that
X-\widetilde{X}\overset{p}{\to}0
as
n\to\infty
for large enough
c
.

Corollary 2.2
.

For any
c\geq 20
,
\varepsilon>0
and
\delta>0
there exists
n_{0}\coloneqq n_{0}(c,\varepsilon,\delta)
such that for all
n\geq n_{0}
the following holds. Let
G\sim G(n,\frac{c}{n})
, then

\mathbb{P}\left[|\widetilde{X}(G)-X(G)|>\delta\right]\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{6}.

Proof.

Let
L=L(G)
and
\widetilde{L}=\widetilde{L}(G)
.
By
Theorem

2.1
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\left|\widetilde{X}-X\right|>\delta\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left[\left|\widetilde{L}-L-\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{L}-L\right]\right|>\delta n^{\frac{1}{2}}\right]\leq\mathbb{P}\left[\left|\widetilde{L}-L\right|+\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\widetilde{L}-L\right|\right]>\delta n^{\frac{1}{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left[\left|\widetilde{L}-L\right|>\frac{\delta n^{\frac{1}{2}}}{2}\right]\leq\mathbb{P}\left[L\neq\widetilde{L}\right]=O(n^{-2})\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{6},

where in the second inequality we used that
\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\widetilde{L}-L\right|\right]\leq n\mathbb{P}\left[L\neq\widetilde{L}\right]=O(n^{-1})\leq\frac{\delta n^{\frac{1}{2}}}{2}
.
∎

Since we want to approximate
\Phi(G)
by considering
G

locally
from the point of view of each vertex, it makes sense to rewrite
\Phi(G)
as a sum over
V(G)
as follows.
First, we note that the minimisation giving
\Phi(G)
can be done separately for each connected component of
G[P\cup R]
. Indeed,
\Phi(G)=\sum_{C}\mathrm{uc}(G[C];P\cap C)
where the sum is taken over all components
C
of
G[P\cup R]
.
For a vertex
v\in P\cup R
, we let
C_{v}(G)
be the connected component of
G[P\cup R]
that contains
v
, where we write
C_{v}
for
C_{v}(G)
if
G
is clear from context. For
v\in S
, we set
C_{v}\coloneqq\varnothing
. We can now write

\Phi(G)~=~\sum_{v\in V(G)}\phi_{G}(v),

where

\phi_{G}(v)~\coloneqq~0,\text{ if }v\in S\quad\text{ and }\quad\phi_{G}(v)~\coloneqq~\frac{\mathrm{uc}(G[C_{v}];P\cap C_{v})}{|C_{v}|},\text{ if }v\in P\cup R.

Our aim is now to find a local approximation for
\phi_{G}(v)
.
To that end, we define the local versions of
S,P,R,
and
\phi_{G}(v)
as follows.
Let
v\in V(G)
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Let
A\subseteq B_{G}(v,k-1)
be the maximal set such that

(4)

\displaystyle|N_{G}(v)\cap(A\cup\partial_{G}(v,k))|\geq 4\quad\text{ for every }v\in(A\cup N_{G}(A))\setminus\partial_{G}(v,k).

We then define the
k
-local strong
4
-core

S_{k}(G,v)
to be
A\cup\partial_{G}(v,k)
.

We note that
S_{k}(G,v)
can also be obtained through a colouring process on
B_{G}(v,k)
as follows.
4
4

4

We will call this the
local colouring process
.

Algorithm 2:
Local colouring process

1:

A graph
G
, a vertex
v\in V(G)
, and an integer
k\geq 1
.

2:

S_{k}(G,v)
, the
k
-local strong
4
-core of
G
at
v
.

3:

Initiate the colour of all vertices in
B_{G}(v,k)
to be sapphire.

4:

Set
t\coloneqq 1
.

5:

while
there exists a sapphire or purple vertex
x\in B_{G}(v,k-1)
with fewer than
4
sapphire

neighbours
do

6:

Colour
x
red and all its sapphire neighbours in
B_{G}(v,k-1)
purple.

7:

Set
v_{t}\coloneqq x
and
t\coloneqq t+1
.

8:

end

while

9:

return
the set of sapphire vertices in
B_{G}(v,k)
.

We further define

\displaystyle P_{k}(G,v)

\displaystyle~\coloneqq~N_{G}\Big(S_{k}(G,v)\setminus\partial_{G}(v,k)\Big)\setminus\partial_{G}(v,k)\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\quad R_{k}(G,v)

\displaystyle~\coloneqq~B_{G}(v,k)\setminus\Big(S_{k}(G,v)\cup P_{k}(G,v)\Big).

We can think of this as a ‘local colouring’ of
B_{G}(v,k)
, where vertices in
S_{k}(G,v)
are coloured ‘sapphire’, vertices in
P_{k}(G,v)
are coloured ‘purple’, and vertices in
R_{k}(G,v)
are coloured ‘red’. As with
Subsection

2.1
, we note that the ‘local colouring’ is determined by the sequence
v_{1},v_{2},\ldots
of vertices which are coloured red.

We will later show (
Observation

4.3
) that whenever the component of
G[P\cup R]
containing
v
is a subset of
B_{G}(v,k-1)
, then it will agree with the component of
G[P_{k}(G,v)\cup R_{k}(G,v)]
containing
v
and the global and local colourings on this component will therefore be the same. In this way, we can think of the local colouring as giving a local approximation to the global colouring at
v
as
k\to\infty
.

If
v\in P_{k}(G,v)\cup R_{k}(G,v)
, we define
C_{v}^{k}(G)
to be the component of
G[P_{k}(G,v)\cup R_{k}(G,v)]
that contains
v
. If
v\in S_{k}(G,v)
, we set
C_{v}^{k}(G)\coloneqq\varnothing
. We also write
C_{v}^{k}
for
C_{v}^{k}(G)
if
G
is clear from context. Define
\phi_{G,k}
by

\displaystyle\phi_{G,k}(v)~\coloneqq~0\text{ if }v\in S_{k}(G,v)\quad\text{ and }\quad\phi_{G,k}(v)~\coloneqq~\frac{\mathrm{uc}(G[C_{v}^{k}];P_{k}(G,v))}{|C_{v}^{k}|},\text{ otherwise}.

As above,
Observation

4.3
will imply that
\phi_{G,k}(v)=\phi_{G}(v)
whenever the component of
G[P\cup R]
containing
v
is contained in
B_{G}(v,k)
, and so we should expect that, for large
k
,

\widetilde{L}_{k}(G)~\coloneqq~n-\sum_{v\in V(G)}\phi_{G,k}(v)

will be a good approximation to
\widetilde{L}(G)=n-\sum_{v\in V(G)}\phi_{G}(v)
, which itself should be a good approximation to
L(G)
. Let us also define the rescaling

\widetilde{X}_{k}(G)~\coloneqq~\frac{\widetilde{L}_{k}(G)-\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{L}_{k}(G)\right]}{n^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

When
G
is clear from the context we write
\widetilde{L}_{k}
and
\widetilde{X}_{k}
for
\widetilde{L}_{k}(G)
and
\widetilde{X}_{k}(G)
, respectively.
A key step towards our result will be to bound the variance of
\widetilde{X}-\widetilde{X}_{k}
, by using the Efron–Stein inequality (
Theorem

3.5
). For this step we will need good control over the effect of resampling an edge on
\widetilde{X}-\widetilde{X}_{k}
. We will show the following in
Section

5
.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
k\geq 100
and
c\geq 20
be fixed. Let
G\sim G(n,\frac{c}{n})
. Then,

\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{X}(G)-\widetilde{X}_{k}(G)\right]\leq 64ck^{2}\left(\frac{3}{4}\right)^{k-2}+o(1),

as
n\rightarrow\infty
.

As with
Theorem

2.1
, it is a simple consequence of
Lemma

2.3
that
\widetilde{X}-\widetilde{X}_{k}\overset{p}{\to}0
as
k,n\to\infty
, in the sense that for every
\delta>0
,

\lim_{k\to\infty}\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[|\widetilde{X}-\widetilde{X}_{k}|>\delta\right]=0.

Corollary 2.4
.

For any
c\geq 20
and
\varepsilon>0
there exist
\delta\coloneqq\delta(c,\varepsilon)
and
k_{0}\coloneqq k_{0}(c,\varepsilon,\delta)
such that for all
k\geq k_{0}
there exists
n_{0}\coloneqq n_{0}(c,\varepsilon,\delta,k)
such that for all
n\geq n_{0}
the following holds. Let
G\sim G(n,\frac{c}{n})
, then

\mathbb{P}\left[|\widetilde{X}(G)-\widetilde{X}_{k}(G)|>\delta\right]\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{6}.

Proof.

By Chebyshev’s inequality and
Lemma

2.3
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[|\widetilde{X}-\widetilde{X}_{k}|>\delta\right]\leq\frac{\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{X}-\widetilde{X}_{k}\right]}{\delta^{2}}\leq\frac{64ck^{2}\left(\frac{3}{4}\right)^{k-2}+o(1)}{\delta^{2}}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{6}.

∎

2.2.
Approximation by neighbourhood counts

As in
Subsection

1.2
, we now define an approximation to
\widetilde{L}_{k}
in terms of a weighted sum of neighbourhood counts. A
rooted graph
is a pair
(H,r)
where
H
is a graph and
r\in V(H)
. Two rooted graphs
(H_{1},r_{1})
and
(H_{2},r_{2})
are
isomorphic
(written
(H_{1},r_{1})\cong(H_{2},r_{2})
) if there exists a bijection
f\colon V(H_{1})\rightarrow V(H_{2})
such that
f(r_{1})=r_{2}
and
f(u)f(v)\in E(H_{2})
if and only if
uv\in E(H_{1})
for all
u,v\in V(H_{1})
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be the set of rooted graphs
(H,r)
of radius at most
k
.
Note that

\widetilde{L}_{k}(G)~\coloneqq~\sum_{v\in V(G)}(1-\phi_{G,k}(v))=\sum_{v\in V(G)}\sum_{(H,r)\in\mathcal{H}}\alpha_{(H,r)}\mathds{1}_{\{(G[B_{G}(v,k)],v)\cong(H,r)\}}

where
\alpha_{(H,r)}\in[0,1]
is a constant depending only on
(H,r)
.

We approximate
\widetilde{L}_{k}(G)
by the following ‘truncated’ version.
Define

(5)

\displaystyle t_{k}~\coloneqq~(10ck)^{2k}

and

\widehat{L}_{k}(G)~\coloneqq~\sum_{v\in V(G)}\sum_{(T,r)\in\mathcal{T}}\alpha_{(T,r)}\mathds{1}_{\{(G[B_{G}(v,k)],v)\cong(T,r)\}},

where
\mathcal{T}
is the set of rooted trees
(T,r)\in\mathcal{H}
with
1\leq v(T)\leq t_{k}
. We will sometimes also consider the rescaled analogue of
\widehat{L}_{k}

\widehat{X}_{k}(G)~\coloneqq~\frac{\widehat{L}_{k}(G)-\mathbb{E}\left[\widehat{L}_{k}(G)\right]}{n^{\frac{1}{2}}}

When
G
is clear from the context we write
\widehat{L}_{k}
and
\widehat{X}_{k}
for
\widehat{L}_{k}(G)
and
\widehat{X}_{k}(G)
, respectively.

As with
Lemma

2.3
, we use the Efron–Stein inequality (
Theorem

3.5
) to bound the variance of
\widetilde{L}_{k}-\widehat{L}_{k}
, which we can then use to show that
\widetilde{X}_{k}-\widehat{X}_{k}\overset{p}{\to}0
as
k,n\to\infty
, in the sense that for all
\delta>0
,

\lim_{k\to\infty}\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[|\widetilde{X}_{k}-\widehat{X}_{k}|>\delta\right]=0.

We will show the following in
Section

5
.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
k\geq 100
and
c\geq 20
be fixed. Let
G\sim G(n,\frac{c}{n})
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{X}_{k}(G)-\widehat{X}_{k}(G)\right]\leq\exp\left(-c^{2}k^{2}\right)+O\left(\frac{1}{n}\right),

as
n\rightarrow\infty
.

Corollary 2.6
.

For any
c\geq 20
and
\varepsilon>0
there exist
\delta\coloneqq\delta(c,\varepsilon)
and
k_{0}\coloneqq k_{0}(c,\varepsilon,\delta)
such that for all
k\geq k_{0}
there exists
n_{0}\coloneqq n_{0}(c,\varepsilon,\delta,k)
such that for all
n\geq n_{0}
the following holds. Let
G\sim G(n,\frac{c}{n})
, then

\mathbb{P}\left[|\widetilde{X}_{k}(G)-\widehat{X}_{k}(G)|>\delta\right]\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{6}.

Proof.

By Chebyshev’s inequality and
Lemma

2.5
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[|\widehat{X}_{k}-\widetilde{X}_{k}|>\delta\right]\leq\frac{\mathbb{V}\left[\widehat{X}_{k}-\widetilde{X}_{k}\right]}{\delta^{2}}\leq\frac{\exp\left(-c^{2}k^{2}\right)}{\delta^{2}}+O\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{6}.

∎

Finally, in
Subsection

7.3
we will use Stein’s method to show that
\widehat{L}_{k}
satisfies a central limit theorem as
n\to\infty
.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
k\geq 100
and
c\geq 20
be fixed and
G\sim G(n,\frac{c}{n})
. Then,

\frac{\widehat{L}_{k}(G)-\mathbb{E}\left[\widehat{L}_{k}(G)\right]}{\left(\mathbb{V}\left[\widehat{L}_{k}(G)\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\quad\overset{d}{\to}\quad\mathcal{N}(0,1)\quad\text{ as }n\to\infty.

However, in order to apply Stein’s method, we will require a lower bound on
\mathbb{V}\left[\widehat{L}_{k}\right]
– for Stein’s method,
\mathbb{V}\left[\widehat{L}_{k}\right]=\omega(n^{\frac{2}{3}})
would suffice. Furthermore, in order to prove the quantitative aspects of
Theorem

1.2
, it will be important to have some control over the sequence
\sigma_{k}\coloneqq\lim_{n\to\infty}\left(\frac{\mathbb{V}\left[\widehat{L}_{k}\right]}{n}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
, which we summarise in the following lemma, whose proof is given in
Subsection

7.4
:

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
c\geq 20
and
G\sim G(n,\frac{c}{n})
. Then, there exist constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
and
k_{0}(c)\geq 100
such that

(L1)

the limit
\sigma_{k}\coloneqq\lim_{n\to\infty}\left(\frac{\mathbb{V}\left[\widehat{L}_{k}(G)\right]}{n}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
exists for all fixed
k\geq k_{0}(c)
;

(L2)

the limit
\sigma\coloneqq\lim_{k\to\infty}\sigma_{k}
exists and
\sigma=\lim_{n\to\infty}\left(\frac{\mathbb{V}\left[L(G)\right]}{n}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
; and

(L3)

C_{1}e^{-10c}\leq\sigma\leq C_{2}c^{\frac{1}{2}}
.

2.3.
Proof of Theorem
1.2

Fix
c\geq 20
and recall that we are considering

X(G)~\coloneqq~\frac{L(G)-\mathbb{E}\left[L(G)\right]}{n^{\frac{1}{2}}}

where
G\sim G\left(n,\frac{c}{n}\right)
.

Let us define the approximations
\widetilde{L}
,
\widetilde{L}_{k}
and
\widehat{L}_{k}
and their rescalings
\widetilde{X}
,
\widetilde{X}_{k}
and
\widehat{X}_{k}
as in
Sections

2.1
and
2.2
. Let
\sigma\coloneqq\lim_{k\to\infty}\sigma_{k}
be as in
Lemma

2.8
and let
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
be a standard normal random variable. Our aim is to show that
X
converges in distribution to
\sigma Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
, that is,

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[X\leq x\right]=\mathbb{P}\left[\sigma Z\leq x\right]
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
.

To that end, let
\varepsilon>0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
. It suffices to show that for large enough
n
,

(6)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\sigma Z\leq x\right]-\varepsilon\leq\mathbb{P}\left[X\leq x\right]\leq\mathbb{P}\left[\sigma Z\leq x\right]+\varepsilon.

Let
\delta\coloneqq\delta(c,\varepsilon)
be small enough. Let
k_{0}=k_{0}(c,\varepsilon,\delta)
be large enough and let
k\geq k_{0}(c,\varepsilon,\delta)
. Let
n_{0}=n_{0}(c,\varepsilon,\delta,k)
be large enough and let
n\geq n_{0}(c,\varepsilon,\delta,k)
.

By
Lemma

2.7
,

\frac{\widehat{L}_{k}-\mathbb{E}\left[\widehat{L}_{k}\right]}{\left(\mathbb{V}\left[\widehat{L}_{k}\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\quad\overset{d}{\to}\quad Z\quad\text{ as }n\rightarrow\infty.

Since by
Lemma

2.8

(L1)
,
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\left(\frac{\mathbb{V}\left[\widehat{L}_{k}\right]}{n}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}=\sigma_{k}
, we have by Slutsky’s theorem (
Theorem

3.2
), that

\widehat{X}_{k}\quad\overset{d}{\to}\quad\sigma_{k}Z\quad\text{ as }n\rightarrow\infty.

Moreover, since by
Lemma

2.8

(L2)
,
\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\sigma_{k}=\sigma>0
, we have, again by Slutsky’s theorem (
Theorem

3.2
), that

\sigma_{k}Z\quad\overset{d}{\to}\quad\sigma Z\quad\text{ as }k\rightarrow\infty.

Together with the continuity of the function
x\mapsto\mathbb{P}\left[\sigma Z\leq x\right]
, and our choice of
\delta,k_{0}
and
n_{0}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\widehat{X}_{k}\leq x+3\delta\right]\leq\mathbb{P}\left[\sigma_{k}Z\leq x+3\delta\right]+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\leq\mathbb{P}\left[\sigma Z\leq x+3\delta\right]+\frac{\varepsilon}{3}\leq\mathbb{P}\left[\sigma Z\leq x\right]+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}.

Finally, note that if
X\leq x
, then either
\widehat{X}_{k}\leq x+3\delta
, or
\max\{|\widehat{X}_{k}-X_{k}|,|X_{k}-\widetilde{X}|,|\widetilde{X}-X|\}>\delta
. Thus, together with
Corollaries

2.2
,
2.4
, and
2.6
, it follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[X\leq x\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left[\widehat{X}_{k}\leq x+3\delta\right]+\mathbb{P}\left[|\widehat{X}_{k}-X_{k}|>\delta\right]+\mathbb{P}\left[|X_{k}-\widetilde{X}|>\delta\right]+\mathbb{P}\left[|\widetilde{X}-X|>\delta\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left[\sigma Z\leq x\right]+\varepsilon.

This shows the upper bound in
6
. The lower bound is shown in an analogous way, completing the proof of
6
.

In particular, by
Lemma

2.8

(L2)
and Slutsky’s theorem (
Theorem

3.2
) we have

\frac{L(G)-\mathbb{E}\left[L(G)\right]}{\sqrt{\mathbb{V}\left[L(G)\right]}}\quad\overset{d}{\to}\quad\mathcal{N}(0,1),\quad\text{ as }n\rightarrow\infty.

3.
Notation and Preliminaries

In this section we introduce basic notation and some preliminary lemmas.

For
j\in\mathbb{N}
, we let
[j]\coloneqq\{1,\dots,j\}
. For a set
B
and
j\in\mathbb{N}
, we let
\binom{B}{j}\coloneqq\{A\subseteq B\colon|A|=j\}
. We often abuse notation and write
xy
for the
2
-element set
\{x,y\}
.

Let
G
be a graph. We denote by
V(G)
and
E(G)\subseteq\binom{V(G)}{2}
the set of vertices and the set of edges of
G
, respectively. For
U\subseteq V(G)
and
F\subseteq E(G)
, we let
G-U\coloneqq\left(V(G)\setminus U,E(G)\cap\binom{V(G)\setminus U}{2}\right)
and
G-F\coloneqq(V(G),E(G)\setminus F)
. For disjoint
A,B\subseteq V(G)
, we let
E_{G}(A)\coloneqq E(G)\cap\binom{A}{2}
,
e_{G}(A)\coloneqq|E_{G}(A)|
,
G[A]\coloneqq(A,E_{G}(A))
,
E_{G}(A,B)\coloneqq E(G)\cap\{xy\colon x\in A,y\in B\}
,
e_{G}(A,B)\coloneqq|E_{G}(A,B)|
, and
G[A,B]\coloneqq(A\cup B,E_{G}(A,B))
.

For
A\subseteq V(G)
, we let
N_{G}(A)\coloneqq\{x\in V(G)\setminus A\colon xy\in E(G)\text{ for some }y\in A\}
, which is called the
external neighbourhood
of
A
in
G
. For
x\in V(G)
, we also write
N_{G}(x)
for
N_{G}(\{x\})
. For
x,y\in V(G)
, we denote by
\mathrm{dist}_{G}(x,y)
the
graph-distance in
G
from
x
to
y
, that is, the length of a shortest path from
x
to
y
(or
\infty
if no such path exists). For
x\in V(G)
and
A\subseteq V(G)
, we let
\mathrm{dist}_{G}(x,A)\coloneqq\min_{y\in A}\mathrm{dist}_{G}(x,y)
(and for convenience, we let
\mathrm{dist}_{G}(x,\varnothing)\coloneqq\infty
).
For
x\in V(G)
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we let
\partial_{G}(x,k)\coloneqq\{y\in V(G)\colon\mathrm{dist}_{G}(x,y)=k\}
and
B_{G}(x,k)\coloneqq\{y\in V(G)\colon\mathrm{dist}_{G}(x,y)\leq k\}
.

Given
e\in\binom{V(G)}{2}
, we define

G+e\coloneqq(V(G),E(G)\cup\{e\})\quad\text{ and }\quad G-e\coloneqq(V(G),E(G)\setminus\{e\}).

A
rooted graph
is a pair
(H,r)
where
H
is a graph and
r\in V(H)
. Two rooted graphs
(H_{1},r_{1})
and
(H_{2},r_{2})
are
isomorphic
(written
(H_{1},r_{1})\cong(H_{2},r_{2})
) if there exists a bijection
f\colon V(H_{1})\rightarrow V(H_{2})
such that
f(r_{1})=r_{2}
and
f(u)f(v)\in E(H_{2})
if and only if
uv\in E(H_{1})
for all
u,v\in V(H_{1})
.

We say that a sequence of events
\mathcal{E}_{n}
holds
with high probability
(or whp for short) if
\mathbb{P}\left[\mathcal{E}_{n}\right]\rightarrow 1
as
n\rightarrow\infty
.

We use standard asymptotic notations
o(\cdot),O(\cdot),\Theta(\cdot),\omega(\cdot),\Omega(\cdot)
all of which are taken as
n\rightarrow\infty
(unless otherwise specified) and the implied constants may depend on any parameter other than
n
, and in particular may depend on
c
and
k
. We will also at times use probabilistic asymptotic notations, in particular, given a sequence of random variables
X(n)
and a function
f:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}
, we write
X=o_{\bm{p}}(f)
to mean that for all
\varepsilon,\delta>0
there exists
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
n\geq n_{0}

\mathbb{P}\left[\left|\frac{X(n)}{f(n)}\right|\geq\delta\right]\leq\varepsilon

and
X=O_{\bm{p}}(f)
to mean that for any
\varepsilon>0
there exist
M>0
and
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
n\geq n_{0}

\mathbb{P}\left[\left|\frac{X(n)}{f(n)}\right|\geq M\right]\leq\varepsilon.

3.1.
Probabilistic Preliminaries

In this subsection we collect some probabilistic tools that we will need.

We will use a probabilistic form of the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, see for example
[
33
]
.

Theorem 3.1

(Probabilistic Cauchy–Schwarz inequality)
.

For jointly distributed random variables
Y
and
Z
with
\mathbb{E}\left[Y^{2}\right],\mathbb{E}\left[Z^{2}\right],|\mathbb{E}\left[YZ\right]|<\infty
,

|\mathrm{cov}(Y,Z)|\leq(\mathbb{V}\left[Y\right]\mathbb{V}\left[Z\right])^{\frac{1}{2}}.

In particular,

(7)

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{V}\left[Y\right]-\mathbb{V}\left[Z\right]\right|\leq\mathbb{V}\left[Y-Z\right]+2\left(\mathbb{V}\left[Y-Z\right]\mathbb{V}\left[Z\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

We will also need Slutsky’s theorem, sometimes also known as Cramér’s theorem, which gives conditions under which the product of a pair of real random variables converges in distribution.

Theorem 3.2

(Slutsky’s theorem
[
39
]
)
.

Let
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(Y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be sequences of real random variables. Let
X
be a random variable and let
b\in\mathbb{R}
be a constant. If
X_{n}\overset{d}{\to}X
and
Y_{n}\overset{p}{\to}b
, then

X_{n}\cdot Y_{n}\quad\overset{d}{\to}\quad bX,

that is, if
X_{n}
converges in distribution to
X
and
Y_{n}
converges in probability to
b
, then
X_{n}Y_{n}
converges in distribution to
bX
.

We will also need some concentration results. The first is a bound for the ‘extreme’ upper tail of a binomial distribution

Theorem 3.3

(Extreme tail Chernoff bound
[
25
, Corollary 2.4]
)
.

Let
Z\sim\mathrm{Bin}(n,p)
. Then for any
t\geq 7np
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[Z\geq t\right]\leq\exp(-t).

The second is a version of McDiarmid’s inequality for biased distributions.

Lemma 3.4

(
[
41
, Corollary 1.4]
)
.

Let
p\in(0,1)
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
M>0
.
Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
be independent
\mathrm{Bernoulli}(p)
random variables. Let
f\colon\{0,1\}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a function such that for all
i\in[n]
and
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
,

\displaystyle\max_{x_{i}^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}}\left|f(x_{1},\dots,x_{i-1},x_{i},x_{i+1},\dots,x_{n})-f(x_{1}\dots,x_{i-1},x_{i}^{\prime},x_{i+1},\dots,x_{n})\right|\leq M.

Then, for all
\varepsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\left|f(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})-\mathbb{E}\left[f(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})\right]\right|>\varepsilon\right]\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2np(2-p)M^{2}+\frac{2M\varepsilon}{3}}\right).

Finally, we will make use of the Efron–Stein inequality to bound several variances.

Theorem 3.5

(Efron–Stein inequality
[
15
,
40
]
)
.

Suppose that
X_{1},\dots,X_{m},X_{1}^{\prime},\dots,X_{m}^{\prime}
are independent random variables where
X_{i},X_{i}^{\prime}
have the same distribution for all
i\in[m]
. Let
\textbf{X}=(X_{1},\dots,X_{i-1},X_{i},X_{i+1},\allowbreak\dots,X_{m})

and
\textbf{X}^{(i)}=(X_{1},\dots,X_{i-1},X_{i}^{\prime},X_{i+1},\dots,X_{m})
. Then for any measurable function
g\colon\mathbb{R}^{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,

\mathbb{V}\left[g(\textbf{X})\right]\leq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{E}[(g(\textbf{X})-g(\textbf{X}^{(i)}))^{2}].

In particular, we will apply the Efron–Stein inequality to graph theoretical functions which can be expressed as a sum of ‘local’ quantities, where the expected effect of ‘resampling’ an edge can be controlled. This is expressed in the next lemma which will be essential for the proofs of
Lemmas

2.3
,
5.1
, and
2.5
, the last of which provides an upper bound on
\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{X}_{k}\right]
.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
G\sim G(n,p)
be a random graph, let
(\psi_{G}(v)\colon v\in V(G))
be a collection of random variables and let
Z\coloneqq\sum_{v\in V(G)}\psi_{G}(v)
. Let
f\in\binom{V(G)}{2}
be arbitrary and let
G^{+}=G+f
and
G^{-}=G-f
. Suppose that
K>0
is such that
|\psi_{G}(v)|\leq K
for each
v\in V(G)
and let

D\coloneqq\{v\in V(G)\colon\psi_{G^{+}}(v)\neq\psi_{G^{-}}(v)\}.

Then

\mathbb{V}\left[Z\right]\leq 2K^{2}p(1-p)n^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[|D|^{2}\right].

Proof.

For an edge
e\in\binom{V(G)}{2}
, let
Y_{e}\coloneqq\mathds{1}_{\{e\in E(G)\}}
. Furthermore, let
\textbf{Y}\coloneqq\left(Y_{e}\right)_{e\in\binom{V(G)}{2}}
and
\textbf{Y}^{(e)}
be the vector obtained by replacing
Y_{e}
in
Y
with a random variable
Y_{e}^{\prime}
which has the same distribution as
Y_{e}
and is independent of
Y_{e^{\prime}}
for all
e^{\prime}\in\binom{V(G)}{2}
. The random graph
G
is fully determined by the random vector
Y
and therefore, we can define
g(\textbf{Y})\coloneqq Z(G)
.

Using the Efron–Stein inequality (
Theorem

3.5
) we get

(8)

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}\left[g(\textbf{Y})\right]\leq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{e\in\binom{V(G)}{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(g(\textbf{Y})-g\left(\textbf{Y}^{(e)}\right)\right)^{2}\right].

By symmetry, the value of
\mathbb{E}\left[\left(g(\textbf{Y})-g\left(\textbf{Y}^{(e)}\right)\right)^{2}\right]
is independent of the edge
e
. Let
f=\{u,v\}\in\binom{V(G)}{2}
be a fixed edge as in the statement of the theorem, then, due to (
8
) and the law of total expectation, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}\left[g(\textbf{Y})\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{n^{2}}{4}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(g(\textbf{Y})-g\left(\textbf{Y}^{(f)}\right)\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{n^{2}}{4}\mathbb{P}\left[Y_{f}=Y_{f}^{\prime}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\left(g(\textbf{Y})-g\left(\textbf{Y}^{(f)}\right)\right)^{2}\;\middle|\;Y_{f}=Y_{f}^{\prime}\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{n^{2}}{4}\mathbb{P}\left[Y_{f}\neq Y_{f}^{\prime}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\left(g(\textbf{Y})-g\left(\textbf{Y}^{(f)}\right)\right)^{2}\;\middle|\;Y_{f}\neq Y_{f}^{\prime}\right]

(9)

\displaystyle=\frac{n^{2}}{2}p(1-p)\mathbb{E}\left[\left(g(\textbf{Y})-g\left(\textbf{Y}^{(f)}\right)\right)^{2}\;\middle|\;Y_{f}\neq Y_{f}^{\prime}\right],

where we used in the last equality that if
Y_{f}=Y_{f}^{\prime}
, then
\textbf{Y}=\textbf{Y}^{(f)}
and therefore,

\mathbb{E}\left[\left(g(\textbf{Y})-g\left(\textbf{Y}^{(f)}\right)\right)^{2}\;\middle|\;Y_{f}=Y_{f}^{\prime}\right]=0,

and that
\mathbb{P}\left[Y_{f}\neq Y_{f}^{\prime}\right]=2p(1-p)
.

We note that, under the assumption
Y_{f}\neq Y_{f}^{\prime}
, exactly one of the two vectors
Y
and
\textbf{Y}^{(f)}
‘corresponds’ to
G^{+}
and the other to
G^{-}
.
Hence, by the definition of
g
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(g(\textbf{Y})-g\left(\textbf{Y}^{(f)}\right)\right)^{2}\;\middle|\;Y_{f}\neq Y_{f}^{\prime}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(Z(G^{+})-Z(G^{-})\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum_{w\in V(G)}\psi_{G^{+}}(w)-\psi_{G^{-}}(w)\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 4K^{2}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\left\{w:\psi_{G^{+}}(w)\neq\psi_{G^{-}}(w\right\}\right|^{2}\right]

(10)

\displaystyle=4K^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[|D|^{2}\right].

The result then follows from (
9
) and (
10
).
∎

3.2.
Stein’s Method

We will use the following version of Stein’s method, developed by Barbour, Karoński, and Ruciński
[
7
]
(see
Theorem

3.7
), to deduce
Lemma

3.8
, stated shortly. Barbour, Karoński, and Ruciński applied
Theorem

3.7
in
[
7
]
to prove a cental limit theorem for the number of tree components of size
k\in\mathbb{N}
in
G(n,p)
, in the regime where the expactation of this number tends to infinity. In comparison,
Lemma

3.8
implies a central limit theorem for weighted sums of neighbourhood counts in the sparse regime.
Lemma

3.8
is used in the derivation of
Lemmas

2.7
and
7.6
.

Let
V\coloneqq[n]
and
\mathcal{F}\coloneqq\binom{V}{2}
. We think of
G(n,p)
as a probability space
\mathscr{P}
on
2^{\mathcal{F}}
. For
U\subseteq V
, let
\mathcal{F}(U)\coloneqq\{e\in\mathcal{F}\colon e\cap U\neq\varnothing\}
. We say that for
U\subseteq V
, a random variable
Y\colon 2^{\mathcal{F}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
on
\mathscr{P}
is
semi-induced with respect to
U
if for any
G_{1},G_{2}\in 2^{\mathcal{F}}
with
G_{1}\cap\mathcal{F}(U)=G_{2}\cap\mathcal{F}(U)
we have
Y(G_{1})=Y(G_{2})
.
Let
\mathcal{U}\subseteq 2^{V}
and for each
U\in\mathcal{U}
let
Y_{U}
be a random variable on
\mathscr{P}
that is semi-induced with respect to
U
. For
U\in\mathcal{U}
and
U^{\prime}\subseteq V
with
U^{\prime}\cap U=\varnothing
, we let
Y_{U}^{U^{\prime}}
be the random variable on
\mathscr{P}
defined by
Y_{U}^{U^{\prime}}(G)\coloneqq Y_{U}(G\setminus\mathcal{F}(U^{\prime}))
for all
G\in 2^{\mathcal{F}}
. Let
Y\coloneqq\sum_{U\in\mathcal{U}}Y_{U}
,
\sigma_{Y}\coloneqq(\mathbb{V}\left[Y\right])^{\frac{1}{2}}
, and for every
U\in\mathcal{U}
,
\mu_{U}\coloneqq\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{U}\right]
and
X_{U}\coloneqq\sigma_{Y}^{-1}(Y_{U}-\mu_{U})
. For
U_{1}\in\mathcal{U}
, let
Z_{U_{1}}\coloneqq\sum_{U_{2}\in\mathcal{U}}Z_{U_{1}U_{2}}
, where

Z_{U_{1}U_{2}}~\coloneqq~\begin{cases}\sigma_{Y}^{-1}Y_{U_{2}},\text{ if }U_{1}\cap U_{2}\neq\varnothing\\
\sigma_{Y}^{-1}(Y_{U_{2}}-Y_{U_{2}}^{U_{1}}),\text{ if }U_{1}\cap U_{2}=\varnothing.\end{cases}

For
U_{1},U_{2}\in\mathcal{U}
, let

V_{U_{1}U_{2}}~\coloneqq~\sigma_{Y}^{-1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}U_{3}\in\mathcal{U}\\
U_{3}\cap U_{2}\neq\varnothing\\
U_{3}\cap U_{1}=\varnothing\end{subarray}}Y_{U_{3}}^{U_{1}}+\sigma_{Y}^{-1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}U_{3}\in\mathcal{U}\\
U_{3}\cap U_{2}=\varnothing\\
U_{3}\cap U_{1}=\varnothing\end{subarray}}(Y_{U_{3}}^{U_{1}}-Y_{U_{3}}^{U_{1}\cup U_{2}}).

Let

(11)

\displaystyle\delta\coloneqq\sum_{U\in\mathcal{U}}\mathbb{E}\left[|X_{U}|Z_{U}^{2}\right]+\sum_{U_{1},U_{2}\in\mathcal{U}}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[|X_{U_{1}}Z_{U_{1}U_{2}}V_{U_{1}U_{2}}|\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[|X_{U_{1}}Z_{U_{1}U_{2}}|\right]\mathbb{E}\left[|Z_{U_{1}}+V_{U_{1}U_{2}}|\right]\right).

Theorem 3.7

(Barbour, Karoński, Ruciński
[
7
]
)
.

In the above setting, if
\delta\rightarrow 0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
, then

\frac{Y-\mathbb{E}\left[Y\right]}{\sigma_{Y}}\quad\overset{d}{\to}\quad\mathcal{N}(0,1)\quad\text{ as }n\rightarrow\infty,

that is,
\sigma_{Y}^{-1}(Y-\mathbb{E}\left[Y\right])
converges in distribution to a standard normal random variable as
n\rightarrow\infty
.

The following lemma gives a central limit for a broad class of random variables in
G(n,p)
with
p=\Theta\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)
. Since the proof consists of relatively standard combinatorial estimates to bound the quantity
\delta
as in
11
, we defer the details to
Appendix

B
.

Lemma 3.8
.

Suppose that we are in the above setting and for
U\in\mathcal{U}
, let
T_{U}\coloneqq K\mathds{1}_{\{G[U]\text{ is connected}\}}
where
K>0
is a constant. Let
t>0
be such that
|U|\leq t
for all
U\in\mathcal{U}

and suppose that for all
U\in\mathcal{U}
,
G\in 2^{\mathcal{F}}
, and
e\in\mathcal{F}
with
|e\cap U|=1
, we have

(12)

\displaystyle 0\leq Y_{U}(G)\leq Y_{U}(G\setminus\{e\})\leq T_{U}(G).

Suppose
p=\Theta\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)
and
\sigma_{Y}=\omega(n^{\frac{1}{3}})
. Then

\frac{Y-\mathbb{E}\left[Y\right]}{\sigma_{Y}}\quad\overset{d}{\to}\quad\mathcal{N}(0,1)\quad\text{ as }n\rightarrow\infty,

that is,
\sigma_{Y}^{-1}(Y-\mathbb{E}\left[Y\right])
converges in distribution to a standard normal random variable as
n\rightarrow\infty
.

In
Lemma

3.8
, the inequality in
12
is equivalent to requiring
Y_{U}(G)=0
whenever
G[U]
is not connected, and at most
K
otherwise and moreover that deleting edges
e\in\mathcal{F}
with
|e\cap U|=1
can only increase the value of
Y_{U}
.

4.
The effect of resampling edges on the global and local colourings

In this section, with a view to using the Efron–Stein inequality (
Theorem

3.5
) to control the variance of
\widetilde{X}-\widetilde{X}_{k}
(
Lemma

2.3
), we will consider how resampling an edge of
G\sim G\left(n,\frac{c}{n}\right)
effects the global and local colourings given by
Sections

2.1
and
2.1
. That is, given
e\in\binom{V(G)}{2}
, we are interested in how the global and local colourings in
G-e
and
G+e
can differ. A large difficulty in the analysis here comes from the fact that the strong
4
-core
S(G)
is not a
monotonic
function of the graph
G
— adding edges can either increase or decrease the set
S(G)
.

However, we will be able to show that any changes to the colouring (global or local) when adding an edge
e
are in some sense
localised
. In particular, we will show that only vertices that are in a red-purple component of
G-e
or
G+e
containing an endpoint of
e
contribute to
\widetilde{X}-\widetilde{X}_{k}
(see
Lemma

4.12
). We will show that these components are unlikely to be large in a strong sense (see
Lemma

4.16
) and also that if these components contain fewer than
k
vertices, then
\widetilde{X}=\widetilde{X}_{k}
(see
Lemma

4.13
). These two facts allow us to bound the variance of
\widetilde{X}-\widetilde{X}_{k}
by using the Efron–Stein inequality in the form of
Lemma

3.6
.

Before considering, in
Subsection

4.4
, how resampling an edge affects the global and local colourings in the random graph
G\left(n,\frac{c}{n}\right)
(in order to prove
Lemma

4.16
), we show, in
Subsections

4.1
,
4.2
, and
4.3
, some properties about the global and local colourings for any graph and how adding or removing an edge can affect them.

4.1.
Properties of the global and local colourings

In this section, let us fix a graph
G
, and let
V(G)=S\cup P\cup R
be the partition given by
Section

2.1
.
We start with a few observations about the global colouring given by
Section

2.1
and its relation to the local colouring.

First, we note that in every red-purple component at least a
\frac{1}{4}
-fraction of the vertices are red.

Observation 4.1
.

Let
P_{t}
and
R_{t}
be the sets of red and purple vertices after
t
iterations of the while-loop in
Subsection

2.1
, respectively.
Then for every component
C^{\prime}
of
G[R_{t}\cup P_{t}]
, we have that
R_{t}\cap C^{\prime}
is incident to at most
3|R_{t}\cap C^{\prime}|
edges in
G
. Thus
|R_{t}\cap C^{\prime}|\geq|C^{\prime}|/4
. In particular,
R
is incident to at most
3|R|
edges in
G
and if
C
is a component of
G[R\cup P]
, then there are at least
|C|/4
red vertices in
C
.

Proof.

We first note that a vertex which is coloured red during the process will remain red. Furthermore, whenever a vertex
u
changes its colour to purple, it is adjacent to a vertex
v
which just changed its colour to red. We call
v
a witness for
u
and note that each vertex is a witness for at most
3
vertices.
Note that for every edge
e
incident to
R_{t}\cap C^{\prime}
, the endpoint of
e
that is coloured red first is a witness of the other endpoint. It follows that
R_{t}\cap C^{\prime}
is incident to at most
3|R_{t}\cap C^{\prime}|
edges in
G
. Thereafter as every vertex in
P_{t}\cap C^{\prime}
is adjacent to a vertex in
R_{t}\cap C^{\prime}
in
G
, we have
|P_{t}\cap C^{\prime}|\leq 3|R_{t}\cap C^{\prime}|
. Hence,
|R_{t}\cap C^{\prime}|\geq|C^{\prime}|/4
.
∎

We will also need the following fact, which essentially says that the sets
S_{k}(G,w)
approximate
S
in a ‘monotone-decreasing’ manner, in that, if a vertex is globally sapphire, then it is also locally sapphire from the point of view of every vertex that ‘sees’ it.

Observation 4.2
.

Let
w\in V(G)
, let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and let
S_{k}(G,w)
be the
k
-local strong
4
-core of
G
at
w
. Then
S\cap B_{G}(w,k)\subseteq S_{k}(G,w)
.

Proof.

Let
A\coloneqq S\cap B_{G}(w,k-1)
and let
B=\partial_{G}(w,k)
. Note that since
B\subseteq S_{k}(G,w)
by definition, it suffices to show that
A\subseteq S_{k}(G,w)
. Hence, it will be sufficient to show that
A
satisfies (
4
), that is,
|N_{G}(v)\cap(A\cup B)|\geq 4
for all
v\in(A\cup N_{G}(A))\setminus B
.

By the definition of the strong
4
-core
S
of
G
, we have
|N_{G}(v)\cap S|\geq 4
for every
v\in A\cup N_{G}(A)
. Furthermore, if
v\in(A\cup N_{G}(A))\setminus B
, then
N_{G}(v)\subseteq B_{G}(w,k)
and so
N_{G}(v)\cap S\subseteq N_{G}(v)\cap(A\cup B)
. Hence, for every
v\in(A\cup N_{G}(A))\setminus B
, we have

|N_{G}(v)\cap(A\cup B)|\geq|N_{G}(v)\cap S|\geq 4
as claimed.
∎

Recall that, for a vertex
v\in V(G)
,
C_{v}
is defined to be the component of
G[P\cup R]
which contains
v
(and
C_{v}=\varnothing
if
v\in S
). We next note that if
k\in\mathbb{N}
is such that
C_{v}\subseteq B_{G}(v,k-1)
, then in the local colouring the component
C_{v}^{k}
of
G[P_{k}(G,v)\cup R_{k}(G,v)]
which contains
v
is equal to
C_{v}
, and the global and local colourings agree on this component.

Observation 4.3
.

Let
v\in V(G)
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Suppose
C_{v}\subseteq B_{G}(v,k-1)
. Then
C_{v}^{k}=C_{v}
,
P\cap C_{v}=P_{k}(G,v)\cap C_{v}
, and
R\cap C_{v}=R_{k}(G,v)\cap C_{v}
. In particular,
\phi_{G,k}(v)=\phi_{G}(v)
.

Proof.

Note that
N_{G}(C_{v})\subseteq S
, since
C_{v}
is a component of
G-S
. Moreover, since
C_{v}\subseteq B_{G}(v,k-1)
, we have
N_{G}(C_{v})\subseteq B_{G}(v,k)
. By
Observation

4.2
, we have
N_{G}(C_{v})\subseteq S_{k}(G,v)
and so in particular,
C_{v}^{k}\subseteq C_{v}
. On the other hand, we may assume that
Section

2.1
starts by recolouring the red vertices in
C_{v}
in some order
v_{1},v_{2},\dots
. Since
N_{G}(C_{v})\subseteq S(G,v)
and
C_{v}\subseteq B_{G}(v,k-1)
, we could equally start the local colouring process by recolouring the same vertices in the same order. It follows that
C_{v}^{k}=C_{v}
and furthermore the global and local colourings agree, implying that
\phi_{G,k}(v)=\phi_{G}(v)
.
∎

4.2.
Localising the changes

We will find that the effect of adding or deleting an edge
e\in\binom{V(G)}{2}
on the global and local colourings can be in some sense
localised
by finding a ‘separating’ set
A
of vertices which are sapphire in a particular ‘resilient’ manner, in that they are locally/globally sapphire in both
G-e
and
G+e
. We will show that in this case, the local/global colouring can only change in the component of
G-A
which contains the endpoints of
e
.

So, in slightly more generality, let us suppose we have two graphs
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
on the same vertex set which differ only on some set of vertices
A\cup B
, where
A
is a set of vertices that are in the strong
4
-core for
G_{2}
and
B
is cut off from the rest of the both graphs by
A
in the sense that
N_{G_{1}}(B),N_{G_{2}}(B)\subseteq A
. Since
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
agree outside of
A\cup B
, and the set
A
is fully sapphire in
G_{2}
, it will follow that vertices outside
B
which are sapphire in
G_{1}
must also be sapphire in
G_{2}
.
Figure

2
depicts this configuration.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
G_{1},G_{2}
be two graphs on the same vertex set
V
. Let
A\subseteq S(G_{2})
and
B\subseteq V
be such that
N_{G_{1}}(B)\cup N_{G_{2}}(B)\subseteq A
. Suppose
E(G_{1})\triangle E(G_{2})\subseteq\binom{A\cup B}{2}
.
5
5

5

Here
\triangle
denotes the symmetric difference of sets.
Then
S(G_{1})\setminus B\subseteq S(G_{2})\setminus B
.

Proof.

We will show that
U\coloneqq(S(G_{1})\setminus B)\cup S(G_{2})
has the strong
4
-core property
3
for
G_{2}
, from which the result follows by the maximality of
S(G_{2})
.

By definition, every
v\in S(G_{2})\cup N_{G_{2}}(S(G_{2}))
satisfies

|N_{G_{2}}(v)\cap U|\geq|N_{G_{2}}(v)\cap S(G_{2})|\geq 4.

In particular, since
A\subseteq S(G_{2})
this holds for every
v\in A\cup N_{G_{2}}(A)
.

So, consider a vertex
v\in\big((S(G_{1})\setminus B)\cup N_{G_{2}}(S(G_{1})\setminus B)\big)\setminus(A\cup N_{G_{2}}(A))
. Note that, since
N_{G_{2}}(B)\subseteq A
, it follows that
v\not\in A\cup B
. Hence,
N_{G_{1}}(v)\cap B=\varnothing
and since
E(G_{1})\triangle E(G_{2})\subseteq\binom{A\cup B}{2}
, we have
N_{G_{2}}(v)=N_{G_{1}}(v)
and in particular

v\in S(G_{1})\cup N_{G_{1}}(S(G_{1}))
. Thus,

|N_{G_{2}}(v)\cap U|\geq|N_{G_{2}}(v)\cap(S(G_{1})\setminus B)|=|N_{G_{1}}(v)\cap(S(G_{1})\setminus B)|=|N_{G_{1}}(v)\cap S(G_{1})|\geq 4,

where the last inequality follows from the definition of
S(G_{1})
.
∎

G_{1}-B
G_{2}-B
A
A

Figure 2.
Vertices in
V\setminus(A\cup B)
have the same neighbourhoods in
G_{1}
and in
G_{2}
but in
G_{2}
more of these neighbours will be sapphire.

It follows as a consequence of
Lemma

4.4
that if the set
A
is sapphire in both
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
, then the global colourings will agree outside of
B
.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
G_{1},G_{2}
be two graphs on the same vertex set
V
. Let
A\subseteq S(G_{1})\cap S(G_{2})
and
B\subseteq V
be such that
N_{G_{1}}(B)\cup N_{G_{2}}(B)\subseteq A
. Suppose
E(G_{1})\triangle E(G_{2})\subseteq\binom{A\cup B}{2}
. Then

S(G_{1})\setminus B=S(G_{2})\setminus B,\quad P(G_{1})\setminus B=P(G_{2})\setminus B,\quad\text{and}\quad R(G_{1})\setminus B=R(G_{2})\setminus B.

Moreover, for any vertex
w\in V\setminus B
, we have
C_{w}(G_{1})=C_{w}(G_{2})\subseteq V\setminus(A\cup B)
and thus

G_{1}[C_{w}(G_{1})]=G_{2}[C_{w}(G_{2})]\quad\text{and}\quad R(G_{1})\cap C_{w}(G_{1})=R(G_{2})\cap C_{w}(G_{2}).

Proof.

By
Lemma

4.4
, it follows that
S(G_{1})\setminus B=S(G_{2})\setminus B
. Hence, using that
A\subseteq S(G_{1})\cap S(G_{2})
,
N_{G_{1}}(B)\cup N_{G_{2}}(B)\subseteq A
, and
E(G_{1})\triangle E(G_{2})\subseteq\binom{A\cup B}{2}
, we have

\displaystyle P(G_{1})\setminus B

\displaystyle=N_{G_{1}}(S(G_{1}))\setminus B=N_{G_{1}}(S(G_{1}))\setminus(A\cup B)=N_{G_{2}}(S(G_{1}))\setminus(A\cup B)

\displaystyle=N_{G_{2}}(S(G_{2}))\setminus(A\cup B)=N_{G_{2}}(S(G_{2}))\setminus B=P(G_{2})\setminus B,

and hence, also
R(G_{1})\setminus B=R(G_{2})\setminus B
.

Finally, note that since
A\subseteq S(G_{1})\cap S(G_{2})
and there is no edge from
B
to
V\setminus(A\cup B)
, for any
w\in V\setminus B
, we have
C_{w}(G_{1}),C_{w}(G_{2})\subseteq V\setminus(A\cup B)
and thus
G_{1}[C_{w}(G_{1})]=G_{2}[C_{w}(G_{2})]
.
∎

We will also need a version of
Lemma

4.5
which we can apply to the local colourings.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
G_{1},G_{2}
be two graphs on the same vertex set
V
. Let
k\geq 1
,
w\in V
and
A\subseteq V
be such that
A\subseteq S(G_{1})\cap S(G_{2})
and
B\subseteq V\setminus\{w\}
be such that
N_{G_{1}}(B)\cup N_{G_{2}}(B)\subseteq A
. Suppose
E(G_{1})\triangle E(G_{2})\subseteq\binom{A\cup B}{2}
.
Then
C\coloneqq C_{w}^{k}(G_{1})=C_{w}^{k}(G_{2})\subseteq V\setminus(A\cup B)
,
G_{1}[C]=G_{2}[C]
,
P_{k}(G_{1},w)\cap C=P_{k}(G_{2},w)\cap C
, and
R_{k}(G_{1},w)\cap C=R_{k}(G_{2},w)\cap C
.

Proof.

Since
A
is a vertex-cut that separates
B
and
V(G)\setminus(A\cup B)
in both
G_{i}
,
w\notin A\cup B
, and
E(G_{1})\triangle E(G_{2})\subseteq\binom{A\cup B}{2}

there exists a set
U\subseteq V\setminus A
containing
w
such that
G_{1}[U]=G_{2}[U]
and the graph
F\coloneqq G_{1}[U]
is connected and consists of a connected component of
G_{i}-A
for each
i\in\{1,2\}
. By extension, with
A^{\prime}\coloneqq(A\cap B_{F}(v,k))\cup\partial_{F}(v,k)
, there exists
U^{\prime}\subseteq U
containing
w
such that
F^{\prime}\coloneqq G_{1}[U^{\prime}]=G_{2}[U^{\prime}]
is connected and consists of a connected component of
G_{i}-A^{\prime}
for each
i\in\{1,2\}
. By
Observation

4.2
, we have
A^{\prime}\subseteq B_{G_{i}}(w,k)\cap S_{k}(G_{i},w)
for each
i\in\{1,2\}
. Thus
C_{w}^{k}(G_{1}),C_{w}^{k}(G_{2})\subseteq U^{\prime}
.

Now note that if the local colouring process in
G_{1}
recolours the red vertices in
U^{\prime}
in some order
v_{1},v_{2},\dots
, then we could equally start the local colouring process in
G_{2}
by recolouring the same vertices in the same order, and vice versa.
It follows that
C\coloneqq C_{w}^{k}(G_{1})=C_{w}^{k}(G_{2})\subseteq U^{\prime}
,
P_{k}(G_{1},w)\cap C=P_{k}(G_{2},w)\cap C
, and
R_{k}(G_{1},w)\cap C=R_{k}(G_{2},w)\cap C
.
∎

4.3.
The effect of resampling an edge

Throughout this subsection, let us fix a graph
G
and a potential edge
e=\{u,v\}\in\binom{V(G)}{2}
. We let
G^{+}=G+e
and
G^{-}=G-e
. Moreover, for
x\in V(G)
and
\circ\in\{+,-\}
, we let
C_{x}^{\circ}\coloneqq C_{x}(G^{\circ})
,
W^{\circ}\coloneqq C_{u}^{\circ}\cup C_{v}^{\circ}
, and we abbreviate
C_{x}^{k,\circ}\coloneqq C_{x}^{k}(G^{\circ})
,
\phi^{\circ}\coloneqq\phi_{G^{\circ}}
,
\phi_{k}^{\circ}\coloneqq\phi_{G^{\circ},k}
,
S^{\circ}\coloneqq S(G^{\circ})
,
P^{\circ}\coloneqq P(G^{\circ})
, and
R^{\circ}\coloneqq R(G^{\circ})
.
Further, we define

\displaystyle I^{*}\coloneqq\{w\in V(G)\colon G^{+}[C_{w}^{+}]=G^{-}[C_{w}^{-}]\text{ and }R^{+}\cap C_{w}^{+}=R^{-}\cap C_{w}^{-}\},

and

\displaystyle I^{*}_{k}\coloneqq\{

\displaystyle w\in V(G)\colon G^{+}[C_{w}^{k,+}]=G^{-}[C_{w}^{k,-}]\text{ and }

\displaystyle R_{k}(G^{+},w)\cap C_{w}^{k,+}=R_{k}(G^{-},w)\cap C_{w}^{k,-}\}.

Suppose that
w\in I^{*}
. By definition, we have
G^{+}[C_{w}^{+}]=G^{-}[C_{w}^{-}]
which implies that
C_{w}^{+}=C_{w}^{-}
. Since
R^{+}\cap C_{w}^{+}=R^{-}\cap C_{w}^{-}
,
C_{w}^{+}\subseteq P^{+}\cup R^{+}
, and
C_{w}^{-}\subseteq P^{-}\cup R^{-}
, we also have
P^{-}\cap C_{w}^{-}=P^{+}\cap C_{w}^{+}
.
Thus
I^{*}
is the set of vertices whose red-purple components in the global colouring are ‘indifferent’ to the change in the edge
e=\{u,v\}
and
I^{*}_{k}
is the corresponding set of vertices for the local colouring. In particular, this implies that the value of
\phi
does not change for vertices in
I^{*}
when flipping the edge
e
.

Observation 4.7
.

For
w\in I^{*}
, we have
\phi^{+}(w)=\phi^{-}(w)
and for
w\in I^{*}_{k}
, we have
\phi^{+}_{k}(w)=\phi^{-}_{k}(w)
.

Proof.

For a graph
H
and
w\in V(H)
, the value of
\phi_{H}(w)
only depends on
H[C_{w}(H)]
and the global colouring on
C_{w}(H)
. If
w\in I^{*}
, then these are identical for
G^{+}
and
G^{-}
, thus
\phi^{+}(w)=\phi^{-}(w)
. The second statement follows analogously.
∎

Let us now consider how the global colourings in
G^{+}
and
G^{-}
can differ, which will depend on the colours that
u
and
v
receive in
G^{+}
and
G^{-}
.
Firstly, we note that if
u
and
v
are both sapphire in
G^{-}
, then the global colourings in
G^{+}
and
G^{-}
agree.

Observation 4.8
.

Suppose that
\{u,v\}\subseteq S^{-}
. Then
S^{-}=S^{+}
,
P^{-}=P^{+}
, and
R^{-}=R^{+}
. In particular,
I^{*}=V(G)
.

Proof.

We first show that
S^{-}\subseteq S^{+}
.
Since
e=\{u,v\}\subseteq S^{-}
, we have
N_{G^{+}}(S^{-})=N_{G^{-}}(S^{-})
.
By the definition of
S^{-}
, we have
|N_{G^{-}}(w)\cap S^{-}|\geq 4
for every
w\in S^{-}\cup N_{G^{-}}(S^{-})
. Therefore,
|N_{G^{+}}(w)\cap S^{-}|\geq|N_{G^{-}}(w)\cap S^{-}|\geq 4
for every
w\in S^{-}\cup N_{G^{-}}(S^{-})=S^{-}\cup N_{G^{+}}(S^{-})
. Hence,
S^{-}
has the strong
4
-core property
3
for
G^{+}
and it follows that
S^{-}\subseteq S^{+}
.

We now show that
S^{+}\subseteq S^{-}
.
To that end, let
x\in S^{+}\cup N_{G^{-}}(S^{+})
. Since
N_{G^{-}}(S^{+})\subseteq N_{G^{+}}(S^{+})
, we have
|N_{G^{+}}(x)\cap S^{+}|\geq 4
, by the definition of
S^{+}
. If
x\notin\{u,v\}
, then
N_{G^{+}}(x)=N_{G^{-}}(x)
and so
|N_{G^{-}}(x)\cap S^{+}|=|N_{G^{+}}(x)\cap S^{+}|\geq 4
. If
x\in\{u,v\}
, then, since
\{u,v\}\subseteq S^{-}\subseteq S^{+}
, we have
|N_{G^{-}}(x)\cap S^{+}|\geq|N_{G^{-}}(x)\cap S^{-}|\geq 4
, by the definition of
S^{-}
.
It follows that
S^{+}
has the strong
4
-core property
3
for
G^{-}
. Hence,
S^{+}\subseteq S^{-}
.

Therefore,
S^{-}=S^{+}
. Finally note that since
\{u,v\}\subseteq S^{-}
, we have
P^{-}=N_{G^{-}}(S^{-})=N_{G^{+}}(S^{+})=P^{+}
and thus
R^{-}=V(G)\setminus(S^{-}\cup P^{-})=V(G)\setminus(S^{+}\cup P^{+})=R^{+}
.

Now note that
G^{-}[R^{-}\cup P^{-}]
and
G^{+}[R^{+}\cup P^{+}]
have the same components and for any such component
C
, we have
G^{-}[C]=G^{+}[C]
. Hence
I^{*}=V(G)
.
∎

Whilst the global colourings do not agree in every case, we will find that one of
S^{+}
or
S^{-}
will always be a subset of the other, and the same is true for
W^{+}
and
W^{-}
.

Lemma 4.9
.

The following hold.

(i)

If
\{u,v\}\cap S^{+}=\varnothing
, then
N_{G^{-}}(W^{+})\subseteq N_{G^{+}}(W^{+})\subseteq S^{+}\subseteq S^{-}
,
R^{-}\subseteq R^{+}
, and
W^{-}\subseteq W^{+}
.

(ii)

If
\{u,v\}\cap S^{+}\neq\varnothing
, then
N_{G^{-}}(W^{-})\subseteq N_{G^{+}}(W^{-})\subseteq S^{-}\subseteq S^{+}
,
R^{+}\subseteq R^{-}
, and
W^{+}\subseteq W^{-}
.

Proof.

We deal with the two cases separately.

Case 1:

\{u,v\}\cap S^{+}=\varnothing
.

We first show that
S^{+}\subseteq S^{-}
.
By the definition of
S^{+}
, we have
|N_{G^{+}}(x)\cap S^{+}|\geq 4
for every
x\in S^{+}\cup N_{G^{+}}(x)
.
Since
\{u,v\}\cap S^{+}=\varnothing
, we have
N_{G^{+}}(S^{+})=N_{G^{-}}(S^{+})
and for any vertex
x\in V(G)
, we have
N_{G^{+}}(x)\cap S^{+}=N_{G^{-}}(x)\cap S^{+}
.
Thus, it follows that
|N_{G^{-}}(x)\cap S^{+}|\geq 4
for every
x\in S^{+}\cup N_{G^{-}}(S^{+})
. That is,
S^{+}
satisfies the strong
4
-core property
3
for
G^{-}
. Hence,
S^{+}\subseteq S^{-}
, and so

(13)

\displaystyle N_{G^{-}}(W^{+})\subseteq N_{G^{+}}(W^{+})\subseteq S^{+}\subseteq S^{-},

where we used that
G^{-}\subseteq G^{+}
and the definition of
W^{+}
.

Furthermore, since
N_{G^{+}}(S^{+})=N_{G^{-}}(S^{+})
and
S^{+}\subseteq S^{-}
, we have
S^{+}\cup N_{G^{+}}(S^{+})=S^{+}\cup N_{G^{-}}(S^{+})\subseteq S^{-}\cup N_{G^{-}}(S^{-})
. Hence,

R^{-}=V(G)\setminus(S^{-}\cup N_{G^{-}}(S^{-}))\subseteq V(G)\setminus(S^{+}\cup N_{G^{+}}(S^{+}))=R^{+}.

Finally, by
13
, any component of
G^{-}[P^{-}\cup R^{-}]=G^{-}-S^{-}
is contained in
W^{+}
or
V(G^{-})\setminus(W^{+}\cup N_{G^{-}}(W^{+}))
. Since
\{u,v\}\cap S^{+}=\varnothing
, it follows from the definition of
W^{+}
that
\{u,v\}\subseteq W^{+}
. Hence, the components
C_{u}^{-}
and
C_{v}^{-}
of
G^{-}-S^{-}
are contained in
W^{+}
.

Case 2:

\{u,v\}\cap S^{+}\neq\varnothing
.

Note that, in this case, since
\{u,v\}\in E(G^{+})
, we have
\{u,v\}\subseteq S^{+}\cup N_{G^{+}}(S^{+})
. Thus, by the definition of
S^{+}
, we have

(14)

\displaystyle|N_{G^{+}}(u)\cap S^{+}|,|N_{G^{+}}(v)\cap S^{+}|\geq 4.

We now show that
S^{+}\cup S^{-}
satisfies the strong
4
-core property
3
for
G^{+}
. To that end, let
x\in S^{+}\cup S^{-}\cup N_{G^{+}}(S^{+}\cup S^{-})
. If
x\in S^{+}\cup N_{G^{+}}(S^{+})
, then
|N_{G^{+}}(x)\cap(S^{+}\cup S^{-})|\geq|N_{G^{+}}(x)\cap S^{+}|\geq 4
by the definition of
S^{+}
. Similarly, if
x\in S^{-}\cup N_{G^{-}}(S^{-})
, then
|N_{G^{+}}(x)\cap(S^{+}\cup S^{-})|\geq|N_{G^{-}}(x)\cap S^{-}|\geq 4
by the definition of
S^{-}
. Thus, we may assume that

x\in N_{G^{+}}(S^{+}\cup S^{-})\setminus(N_{G^{+}}(S^{+})\cup N_{G^{-}}(S^{-}))=N_{G^{+}}(S^{-})\setminus N_{G^{-}}(S^{-})\subseteq\{u,v\}

and so
|N_{G^{+}}(x)\cap(S^{+}\cup S^{-})|\geq 4
by
14
.
Hence,
S^{+}\cup S^{-}
satisfies the strong
4
-core property
3
for
G^{+}
. It follows that
S^{+}\cup S^{-}\subseteq S^{+}
, and so
S^{-}\subseteq S^{+}
.

Next we show that
N_{G^{-}}(W^{-})\subseteq N_{G^{+}}(W^{-})\subseteq S^{-}
, where the first inclusion is apparent. Furthremore, since
W^{-}
is a union of components of
G^{-}-S^{-}
, we have
N_{G^{-}}(W^{-})\subseteq S^{-}
. We split into three cases.
If
\{u,v\}\cap S^{-}=\varnothing
, then
\{u,v\}\subseteq W^{-}
by the definition of
W^{-}
and thus
N_{G^{+}}(W^{-})=N_{G^{-}}(W^{-})\subseteq S^{-}
. If
\{u,v\}\subseteq S^{-}
, then by
Observation

4.8
,
S^{-}=S^{+}
and
W^{-}=W^{+}=\varnothing
and thus the conclusion trivially holds.
So, we may assume without loss of generality that
u\in V(G^{-})\setminus S^{-}

and
v\in S^{-}
. Then
u\in W^{-}
and thus
N_{G^{+}}(W^{-})\subseteq N_{G^{-}}(W^{-})\cup\{v\}\subseteq S^{-}
.

Next we show that
R^{+}\subseteq R^{-}
. Indeed, since
S^{-}\subseteq S^{+}
and
G^{-}\subseteq G^{+}
, we have

R^{+}=V(G)\setminus(S^{+}\cup N_{G^{+}}(S^{+}))\subseteq V(G)\setminus(S^{-}\cup N_{G^{+}}(S^{-}))\subseteq V(G)\setminus(S^{-}\cup N_{G^{-}}(S^{-}))=R^{-}.

Finally, we show that
C_{x}^{+}\subseteq W^{-}
for each
x\in\{u,v\}
, and hence
C_{u}^{+}\cup C_{v}^{+}=W^{+}\subseteq W^{-}
. Note that, if
x\in S^{-}\subseteq S^{+}
, then
C_{x}^{+}=\varnothing\subseteq W^{-}
vacuously. Hence we may assume that
x\not\in S^{-}
, and so
x\in W^{-}
. In particular,
C_{x}^{+}
meets
W^{-}
. On the other hand, recalling that
N_{G^{+}}(W^{-})\subseteq S^{-}\subseteq S^{+}
, we see that every component of
G^{+}-S^{+}
is contained in
W^{-}
or
V(G^{+})\setminus(W^{-}\cup N_{G^{+}}(W^{-}))
. Hence, since
C_{x}^{+}
meets
W^{-}
, it follows that
C_{x}^{+}\subseteq W^{-}
as claimed.
∎

Using this information, we can get some control over the set of vertices
w
whose global or local red-purple components are indifferent to flipping the edge
e
.

We start by showing that, if
u
and
v
are both sapphire in
G^{-}
, then all local colourings for
G^{-}
and
G^{+}
agree.

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
If
\{u,v\}\subseteq S^{-}
, then
I^{*}_{k}=V(G).

Proof.

By
Observation

4.8
,
S^{+}=S^{-}\eqqcolon A
.
Since
\{u,v\}\subseteq A
, we have
E(G^{-})\triangle E(G^{+})\subseteq\binom{A}{2}
.
By
Lemma

4.6
, with
G^{-}
,
G^{+}
,
A
, and
\varnothing
playing the roles of
G_{1}
,
G_{2}
,
A
, and
B
, respectively, we have
I^{*}_{k}=V(G)
.
∎

Next we show that vertices not in
W^{+}
or
W^{-}
are ‘indifferent’ to flipping the edge
e
.

Lemma 4.11
.

The following hold for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

(i)

If
\{u,v\}\cap S^{+}=\varnothing
, then
V(G)\setminus W^{+}\subseteq I^{*}\cap I^{*}_{k}
.

(ii)

If
\{u,v\}\cap S^{+}\neq\varnothing
, then
V(G)\setminus W^{-}\subseteq I^{*}\cap I^{*}_{k}
.

Proof.

We deal with the two cases separately.

Case 1:

\{u,v\}\cap S^{+}=\varnothing
.

Let
w\in V(G)\setminus W^{+}
. Note that by
Lemma

4.9
, we have
N_{G^{-}}(W^{+})\subseteq N_{G^{+}}(W^{+})\subseteq S^{+}=S^{+}\cap S^{-}
. Since
\{u,v\}\cap S^{+}=\varnothing
, we have
\{u,v\}\subseteq W^{+}
. Hence,
E(G^{+})\triangle E(G^{-})\subseteq\binom{W^{+}}{2}
. By
Lemma

4.5
and
Lemma

4.6
with
G^{+}
,
G^{-}
,
N_{G^{+}}(W^{+})
, and
W^{+}
playing the roles of
G_{1}
,
G_{2}
,
A
, and
B
, respectively, we have
w\in I^{*}\cap I^{*}_{k}
.

Case 2:

\{u,v\}\cap S^{+}\neq\varnothing
.

Let
w\in V(G)\setminus W^{-}
. By
Lemma

4.9
, we have
N_{G^{-}}(W^{-})\subseteq N_{G^{+}}(W^{-})\subseteq S^{-}=S^{-}\cap S^{+}
.

First suppose that
\{u,v\}\subseteq S^{-}
. Then by
Lemma

4.10
, we have
I^{*}_{k}=V(G)
and by
Observation

4.8
, we have
I^{*}=V(G)
. Hence,
w\in I^{*}\cap I^{*}_{k}
.

Thus, we may assume without loss of generality that
u\in P^{-}\cup R^{-}
. Hence,
u\in W^{-}
and thus
\{u,v\}\subseteq W^{-}\cup N_{G^{+}}(W^{-})
. Let
A\coloneqq N_{G^{+}}(W^{-})
. It follows that
E(G^{+})\triangle E(G^{-})\subseteq\binom{A\cup W^{-}}{2}
.
Hence, by
Lemma

4.5
with
G^{+}
,
G^{-}
,
A
, and
W^{-}
playing the roles of
G_{1}
,
G_{2}
,
A
, and
B
, respectively, we have
w\in I^{*}
.

Since
A\subseteq S^{-}\cap S^{+}
, we have, by
Observation

4.2
, that
A\cap B_{G^{-}}(w,k)\subseteq S_{k}(G^{-},w)
and
A\cap B_{G^{+}}(w,k)\subseteq S_{k}(G^{+},w)
. Hence, by
Lemma

4.6
with
G^{+}
,
G^{-}
,
A
, and
W^{+}
playing the roles of
G_{1}
,
G_{2}
,
A
, and
B
, respectively, we have
w\in I^{*}_{k}
.

It follows that
w\in I^{*}\cap I^{*}_{k}
. ∎

To bound the variances of
\widetilde{X}_{k}
and
\widetilde{X}-\widetilde{X}_{k}
using the Efron–Stein inequality (in the form of
Lemma

3.6
), we will be interested in the set

(15)

\displaystyle\widetilde{D}\coloneqq\{w\in V(G)\colon\phi_{k}^{+}(w)\neq\phi_{k}^{-}(w)\}

of vertices whose local contribution changes and the set

(16)

\displaystyle D\coloneqq\{w\in V(G)\colon\phi^{+}(w)-\phi_{k}^{+}(w)\neq\phi^{-}(w)-\phi_{k}^{-}(w)\}

of vertices for which the difference between the local and the global contribution changes. We can now show that the vertices in
D\cup\widetilde{D}
are in some sense local to the edge
e
, in that they are contained in
W^{+}
or
W^{-}
.

Lemma 4.12
.

The following hold.

(i)

If
\{u,v\}\cap S^{+}=\varnothing
, then
D\cup\widetilde{D}\subseteq W^{+}
.

(ii)

If
\{u,v\}\cap S^{+}\neq\varnothing
, then
D\cup\widetilde{D}\subseteq W^{-}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\{u,v\}\cap S^{+}=\varnothing
(the proof in the other case is analgous). Let
w\in V(G)\setminus W^{+}
. By
Lemma

4.11
, we have
w\in I^{*}\cap I^{*}_{k}
. By
Observation

4.7
, we have that
\phi^{+}(w)=\phi^{-}(w)
and
\phi^{+}_{k}(w)=\phi^{-}_{k}(w)
. Hence
w\notin D\cup\widetilde{D}
. Thus we have shown that
D\cup\widetilde{D}\subseteq W^{+}
.
∎

The following lemma shows that
D=\varnothing
if both
W^{+}
and
W^{-}
have size smaller than
k
.

Lemma 4.13
.

If
\max\{|W^{+}|,|W^{-}|\}<k
, then
D=\varnothing
.

Proof.

We make the usual case distinction according to
Lemma

4.9
.

Case 1:

\{u,v\}\cap S^{+}=\varnothing
.

By
Lemma

4.12
, we have
D\subseteq W^{+}
. Let
w\in W^{+}
and note that, by definition,
C_{w}(G^{+})\subseteq W^{+}
. Since
|W^{+}|<k
, this implies that
C_{w}(G^{+})\subseteq B_{G^{+}}(w,k-1)
. Hence, by
Observation

4.3
, we have
\phi_{k}^{+}(w)=\phi^{+}(w)
.

On the other hand, by
Lemma

4.9
, we have
N_{G^{-}}(W^{+})\subseteq S^{-}
.
Since
w\in W^{+}
and
C_{w}(G^{-})
is the component of
G^{-}-S^{-}
that contains
w
, this implies that
C_{w}(G^{-})\subseteq W^{+}
. Since
|W^{+}|<k
, this implies that
C_{w}(G^{-})\subseteq B_{G^{-}}(w,k-1)
. Hence, by
Observation

4.3
, we have
\phi_{k}^{-}(w)=\phi^{-}(w)
.

Therefore,
w\notin D
and so
D=\varnothing
.

Case 2:

\{u,v\}\cap S^{+}\neq\varnothing
.

This case is proved in an analogous way to Case
1
, noting that
Lemma

4.12
implies that
D\subseteq W^{-}
. ∎

Finally, we will prove some structural properties about
W^{-}\cup W^{+}\cup\{u,v\}
which will later use in the proof of
Lemma

4.16
to show that this set is unlikely to be large in
G\left(n,\frac{c}{n}\right)
.

Lemma 4.14
.

Let
W^{*}\coloneqq W^{-}\cup W^{+}\cup\{u,v\}
and
W^{*}_{R}\coloneqq(R^{-}\cap W^{*})\cup(R^{+}\cap W^{*})
. Then the following hold.

(i)

G^{+}[W^{*}]
is connected.

(ii)

N_{G}(x)\subseteq W^{*}
for all
x\in W^{*}_{R}
.

(iii)

|W^{*}_{R}|\geq\frac{|W^{*}|-2}{4}
.

Proof.

Recall that
W^{-}\coloneqq C_{u}^{-}\cup C_{v}^{-}
where for
x\in\{u,v\}
,
C_{x}^{-}
is the component of
G^{-}-S^{-}
that contains
x
(or
C_{x}^{-}=\varnothing
if
x\in S^{-}
). Since
G^{+}\coloneqq G+uv
, this implies that
G^{+}[W^{-}]
is connected. Similarly,
G^{+}[W^{+}]
is connected. Hence, since the union of two non-disjoint connected sets is connected,
(i)
follows.

Let
x\in R^{-}\cap W^{*}
. By the definition of
R^{-}
, we have
N_{G^{-}}(x)\subseteq R^{-}\cup P^{-}
.
By the definition of
W^{-}
, we have
x\in W^{-}\cup W^{+}
. Note that by
Lemma

4.9
, we have
N_{G^{+}}(W^{-}\cup W^{+})\subseteq S^{-}
. Thus
N_{G}(x)\subseteq N_{G^{+}}(x)\subseteq W^{-}\cup W^{+}\subseteq W^{*}
.
Arguing along similar lines, we can conclude that for each
x\in W^{*}_{R}
, we have
N_{G}(x)\subseteq W^{*}
