. Hence we have proved
(ii)
.

To prove
(iii)
, suppose first that
\{u,v\}\cap S^{+}=\varnothing
. Then, by
Lemma

4.9
, we have
R^{-}\subseteq R^{+}
and
W^{-}\subseteq W^{+}
. Hence,
R^{-}\cap W^{-}\subseteq R^{+}\cap W^{+}
and so
W^{*}=W^{+}\cup\{u,v\}
and
W^{*}_{R}\supseteq R^{+}\cap W^{+}=(R^{+}\cap C_{u}^{+})\cup(R^{+}\cap C_{v}^{+})
. Since
C_{u}^{+}
and
C_{v}^{+}
are components of
G^{+}-S^{+}
, we have
C_{u}^{+}\cap C_{v}^{+}=\varnothing
or
C_{u}^{+}=C_{v}^{+}
. Thus, by
Observation

4.1
, we have
|W^{*}_{R}|\geq|R^{+}\cap W^{+}|\geq\frac{1}{4}|W^{+}|\geq\frac{1}{4}|W^{*}\setminus\{u,v\}|=\frac{|W^{*}|-2}{4}
. In the case where
\{u,v\}\cap S^{+}\neq\varnothing
,
|W^{*}_{R}|\geq\frac{|W^{*}|-2}{4}
follows analogously.
∎

4.4.
Typical changes in the colourings after resampling

So far we have only considered the deterministic effects of adding or removing an edge to a fixed
G
on the global and local colourings. Let us now show what these deterministic results imply for the ‘typical’ changes when resampling an edge in a
random
graph.

In what follows we let
G\sim G\left(n,\frac{c}{n}\right)
,
e=\{u,v\}\in\binom{V(G)}{2}
be arbitrary and let
G^{\circ},S^{\circ},P^{\circ},R^{\circ}
and
W^{\circ}
for
\circ\in\{+,-\}
be as in the previous section.

The proof of the following lemma is an adaption of the proof of
[
3
, Lemma 7 (b)]
. It states that whp there are no large red-purple components in
G^{+}
or
G^{-}
. For completeness, the proof is given in
Appendix

C
.

Lemma 4.15
.

Let
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{comp}}
be the event that every component in
G^{+}-S^{+}
or in
G^{-}-S^{-}
has size at most
\log^{4}n
. Then
\mathbb{P}\left[\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{comp}}^{c}\right]=o(n^{-2})
.

The following lemma states that

W^{*}\coloneqq W^{-}\cup W^{+}\cup\{u,v\}

is unlikely to be large in a strong way. Together with
Lemma

4.12
, we will use this to prove
Lemma

2.3
via
Lemma

3.6
.

Lemma 4.16
.

If
k\geq 100
is fixed, then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[|W^{*}|^{2}\mathds{1}_{\{|W^{*}|\geq k\}}\right]\leq 32k^{2}\left(\frac{3}{4}\right)^{k-2}+o(1)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{E}\left[|W^{*}|^{2}\right]\leq\widetilde{B}+o(1),

as
n\rightarrow\infty
, where
\widetilde{B}>0
is an absolute constant.

Proof.

Recall that by
Lemma

4.15
,
\mathbb{P}\left[\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{comp}}^{c}\right]=o(n^{-2})
. Hence, by linearity of expectation

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[|W^{*}|^{2}\mathds{1}_{\{|W^{*}|\geq k\}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[|W^{*}|^{2}\mathds{1}_{\{|W^{*}|\geq k\}}\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{comp}}}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[|W^{*}|^{2}\mathds{1}_{\{|W^{*}|\geq k\}}\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{comp}}^{c}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[|W^{*}|^{2}\mathds{1}_{\{|W^{*}|\geq k\}}\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{comp}}}\right]+\mathbb{P}\left[\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{comp}}^{c}\right]n^{2}

(17)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[|W^{*}|^{2}\mathds{1}_{\{|W^{*}|\geq k\}}\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{comp}}}\right]+o(1).

Since, by
Lemma

4.14
,
W^{*}
is connected in
G^{+}
and contains at least
\frac{|W^{*}|-2}{4}
vertices with no neighbour outside of
W^{*}
, it follows that for any
k\geq 100
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[|W^{*}|^{2}\mathds{1}_{\{|W^{*}|\geq k\}}\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{comp}}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=k}^{4\log^{4}n}j^{2}\binom{n-2}{j-2}j^{j-2}p^{j-2}\binom{j}{\lceil\frac{j-2}{4}\rceil}(1-p)^{\frac{(j-2)(n-j)}{4}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=k}^{\infty}8j^{2}\left(\frac{2ejnp}{j-2}e^{-\frac{c}{4}}\right)^{j-2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=k}^{\infty}8j^{2}\left(\frac{k}{k-2}\cdot 2ce^{1-\frac{c}{4}}\right)^{j-2}

\displaystyle\leq 8k^{2}\left(\frac{k}{k-2}\cdot 2ce^{1-\frac{c}{4}}\right)^{k-2}\sum_{j=k}^{\infty}\left(\frac{j}{k}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{k}{k-2}\cdot 2ce^{1-\frac{c}{4}}\right)^{j-k}.

Now note that
\left(\frac{j}{k}\right)^{2}=\left(1+\frac{j-k}{k}\right)^{2}\leq e^{\frac{2(j-k)}{k}}
and that, since
k\geq 100
and
c\geq 20
, we have

e^{\frac{2}{k}}\cdot\frac{k}{k-2}\cdot 2ce^{1-\frac{c}{4}}\leq e^{\frac{2}{100}}\cdot\frac{100}{98}\cdot 40e^{-4}\leq\frac{3}{4}
.
Hence, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[|W^{*}|^{2}\mathds{1}_{\{|W^{*}|\geq k\}}\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{comp}}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 8k^{2}\left(\frac{3}{4}\right)^{k-2}\sum_{j=k}^{\infty}\left(e^{\frac{2}{k}}\frac{k}{k-2}\cdot 2ce^{1-\frac{c}{4}}\right)^{j-k}

\displaystyle\leq 8k^{2}\left(\frac{3}{4}\right)^{k-2}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{3}{4}\right)^{j}

(18)

\displaystyle=32k^{2}\left(\frac{3}{4}\right)^{k-2}.

The first part of the statement now follows from (
17
) and (
18
).

Furthermore, it follows from (
18
) that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[|W^{*}|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[|W^{*}|^{2}\mathds{1}_{\{|W^{*}|<100\}}\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{comp}}}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[|W^{*}|^{2}\mathds{1}_{\{|W^{*}|\geq 100\}}\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{comp}}}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[|W^{*}|^{2}\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{comp}}^{c}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=2}^{99}j^{2}+32\cdot 100^{2}\cdot\left(\frac{3}{4}\right)^{98}+o(1).

Hence, the second part follows by setting
\widetilde{B}\coloneqq\sum_{j=2}^{99}j^{2}+32\cdot 100^{2}\cdot\left(\frac{3}{4}\right)^{98}
.
∎

5.
Variance bounds via Efron–Stein inequality

In this section we will use the results in
Section

4
together with the Efron–Stein inequality (in the form of
Lemma

3.6
) to bound the variance of various random variables.
We prove upper bounds on the variance of
\widetilde{X}-\widetilde{X}_{k}
(
Lemma

2.3
), the variance of
\widetilde{X}_{k}-\widehat{X}_{k}
(
Lemma

2.5
), and the variance of
\widetilde{X}_{k}
(
Lemma

5.1
).
The upper bounds on
\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{X}-\widetilde{X}_{k}\right]
and
\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{X}_{k}-\widehat{X}_{k}\right]
will together imply that
\widehat{X}_{k}
is a good approximation for
\widetilde{X}
. The upper bound on
\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{X}_{k}\right]
will be used to show that the bounds on
\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{X}-\widetilde{X}_{k}\right]
and
\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{X}_{k}-\widehat{X}_{k}\right]
imply bounds on
\left|\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{X}\right]-\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{X}_{k}\right]\right|
and
\left|\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{X}_{k}\right]-\mathbb{V}\left[\widehat{X}_{k}\right]\right|
via
Theorem

3.1
.

Throughout this section let
G\sim G\left(n,\frac{c}{n}\right)
, let
f=\left\{u,v\right\}\in\binom{V(G)}{2}
be a fixed edge, and let
G^{+}\coloneqq G+f
and
G^{-}\coloneqq G-f
. Let
L,\widetilde{L},\widetilde{L}_{k},\widehat{L}_{k}
and
X,\widetilde{X},\widetilde{X}_{k},\widehat{X}_{k}
be defined as in
Section

2
.

We start by giving a proof of
Lemma

2.3
, which bounds the variance of
\widetilde{X}-\widetilde{X}_{k}
.

Proof of
Lemma

2.3
.

Recall that, by definition

\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{X}-\widetilde{X}_{k}\right]=\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{L}-\widetilde{L}_{k}\right],

and
\widetilde{L}-\widetilde{L}_{k}=\sum_{w\in V(G)}\left(\phi_{G}(w)-\phi_{G,k}(w)\right)
. Note further that, since
\phi_{G}(w),\phi_{G,k}(w)\in[0,1]
, it follows that
|\phi_{G}(w)-\phi_{G,k}(w)|\leq 1
for all
w\in V(G)
. Hence, if we define

D\coloneqq\{w\in V(G)\colon\phi^{+}(w)-\phi_{k}^{+}(w)\neq\phi^{-}(w)-\phi_{k}^{-}(w)\}

as in (
16
), then by
Lemma

3.6

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{X}-\widetilde{X}_{k}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{L}-\widetilde{L}_{k}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2p(1-p)n\mathbb{E}\left[|D|^{2}\right]

(19)

\displaystyle\leq 2c\mathbb{E}\left[|D|^{2}\right].

By
Lemmas

4.12
and
4.13
, we have
|D|\leq|W^{*}|\mathds{1}_{\{|W^{*}|\geq k\}}
, where
W^{*}\coloneqq W^{-}\cup W^{+}\cup\{u,v\}
. Together with
Lemma

4.16
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[|D|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[|W^{*}|^{2}\mathds{1}_{\{|W^{*}|\geq k\}}\right]\leq 32k^{2}\left(\frac{3}{4}\right)^{k-2}+o(1).

Hence, by
19
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{X}-\widetilde{X}_{k}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 64ck^{2}\left(\frac{3}{4}\right)^{k-2}+o(1),

as desired.
∎

We also need the following bound on the variance of
\widetilde{X}_{k}
which is derived in a similar way.

Lemma 5.1
.

There exists an absolute constant
B^{*}>0
such that for all fixed
k\geq 100
and
c\geq 20
, we have

\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{X}_{k}\right]\leq cB^{*}+o(1),

as
n\rightarrow\infty
.

Proof.

Fix
k\geq 100
.
Recall that
\widetilde{D}\coloneqq\{w\in V(G)\colon\phi_{k}^{+}(w)\neq\phi_{k}^{-}(w)\}
as in
15
, where we note that
\phi_{G,k}(w)\in[0,1]
for all
w\in V(G)
. By applying
Lemma

3.6
to
\widetilde{L}
, as in the proof of
Lemma

2.3
, we get that

(20)

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{X}_{k}\right]\leq 2c\mathbb{E}\left[|\widetilde{D}|^{2}\right].

By
Lemma

4.12
, we have
|\widetilde{D}|\leq|W^{*}|
, where
W^{*}\coloneqq W^{-}\cup W^{+}\cup\{u,v\}
. Together with
Lemma

4.16
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[|\widetilde{D}|^{2}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[|W^{*}|^{2}\right]\leq\widetilde{B}+o(1),

where
\widetilde{B}>0
is an absolute constant.
Together with
20
, it follows that
\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{X}_{k}\right]\leq 2c\widetilde{B}+o(1)
. Hence, we are done by setting
B^{*}\coloneqq 2\widetilde{B}
.
∎

Finally, as mentioned in the introduction, our bounds on the variance of
\widetilde{X}_{k}-\widehat{X}_{k}
will hold for a much broader class of random variables, coming from the difference between (rescalings of) a weighted neighbourhood sum and an appropriate truncation thereof. Let us define a
weighted (
k
th)-neighbourhood sum
to be a random variable of the form

\widetilde{M}_{k}=\sum_{v\in V(G)}\sum_{(H,r)\in\mathcal{H}}\beta_{(H,r)}\mathds{1}_{\{(G[B_{G}(v,k)],v)\cong(H,r)\}},

where
\mathcal{H}
is the set of rooted graphs
(H,r)
of radius at most
k
and
\beta_{(H,r)}\in[0,1]
is a constant depending only on
(H,r)
. The
truncation
of
\widetilde{M}_{k}
is the random variable

(21)

\widehat{M}_{k}~\coloneqq~\sum_{v\in V(G)}\sum_{(T,r)\in\mathcal{T}}\beta_{(T,r)}\mathds{1}_{\{(G[B_{G}(v,k)],v)\cong(T,r)\}},

where
\mathcal{T}
is the set of rooted trees
(T,r)\in\mathcal{H}
with
1\leq v(T)\leq t_{k}=(10ck)^{2k}
. We will show the following lemma, which clearly implies
Lemma

2.5
.

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
\widetilde{M}_{k}
be a weighted neighbourhood sum and let
\widehat{M}_{k}
be its truncation. Then for fixed
k\geq 100
and
c>1

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{M}_{k}-\widehat{M}_{k}\right]\leq n\exp\left(-c^{2}k^{2}\right)+O\left(1\right),

as
n\rightarrow\infty
.

In order to prove
Lemma

5.2
we will require the following technical lemma about the typical neighbourhood structure in sparse random graphs, which will imply that the effect of moving to a truncation is neglible, whose proof is given in
Appendix

D
.

Lemma 5.3
.

For fixed
k\geq 100
,
c>1
and
t_{k}\coloneqq(10ck)^{2k}
, let

\displaystyle U_{N}

\displaystyle~\coloneqq~\{w\in V(G)\colon|B_{G^{+}}(w,k)|>t_{k}\},

\displaystyle U_{C}

\displaystyle~\coloneqq~\{w\in V(G)\colon G^{+}[B_{G^{+}}(w,k)]\text{ contains a cycle}\},\text{ and}

\displaystyle N^{*}

\displaystyle~\coloneqq~B_{G}(u,k)\cup B_{G}(v,k).

Then

(i)

\mathbb{E}\left[|N^{*}|^{2}\mathds{1}_{\{|N^{*}|>t_{k}\}}\right]\leq 2k\exp\left(-2c^{2}k^{2}\right)
;

(ii)

\mathbb{E}\left[|N^{*}\cap(U_{N}\cup U_{C})|\right]\leq 10k\exp\left(-2c^{2}k^{2}\right)+O\left(\frac{1}{n}\right).

We are now ready to prove
Lemma

5.2
.

Proof of
Lemma

5.2
.

Let us suppose that

\widetilde{M}_{k}=\widetilde{M}_{k}(G)=\sum_{v\in V(G)}\sum_{(H,r)\in\mathcal{H}}\beta_{(H,r)}\mathds{1}_{\{(G[B_{G}(v,k)],v)\cong(H,r)\}},

where
\beta_{(H,r)}\in[0,1]
and let us define for
w\in V(G)

\psi_{G,k}(w)=\sum_{(H,r)\in\mathcal{H}}\beta_{(H,r)}\mathds{1}_{\{(G[B_{G}(w,k)],v)\cong(H,r)\}},

so that
\widetilde{M}_{k}=\sum_{w\in V(G)}\psi_{G,k}(w)
. Note that, since
\beta_{(H,r)}\in[0,1]
, it follows that
\psi_{G,k}(w)\in[0,1]
. Then

\widehat{M}_{k}=\widehat{M}_{k}(G)=\sum_{w\in V(G)}\psi_{G,k}(w)\mathds{1}_{\{|B_{G}(w,k)|\leq t_{k}\}}\mathds{1}_{\{G[B_{G}(w,k)]\text{ is a tree}\}}:=\sum_{w\in V(G)}\widehat{\psi}_{G,k}(w),

where similarly
\widehat{\psi}_{G,k}\in[0,1]
.

Hence, by applying
Lemma

3.6
to
\widetilde{M}_{k}-\widehat{M}_{k}
, it follows that

(22)

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{M}_{k}-\widehat{M}_{k}\right]\leq 2cn\mathbb{E}\left[|D^{*}|^{2}\right],

where

\displaystyle D^{*}\coloneqq\{w\in V(G)\colon\psi_{G^{+},k}(w)-\widehat{\psi}_{G^{+},k}(w)\neq\psi_{G^{-},k}(w)-\widehat{\psi}_{G^{-},k}(w)\}.

It remains to bound
\mathbb{E}\left[|D^{*}|^{2}\right]
. To this end let us define, as in
Lemma

5.3
,

\displaystyle U_{N}

\displaystyle~\coloneqq~\{w\in V(G)\colon|B_{G^{+}}(w,k)|>t_{k}\},

\displaystyle U_{C}

\displaystyle~\coloneqq~\{w\in V(G)\colon G^{+}[B_{G^{+}}(w,k)]\text{ contains a cycle}\},\text{ and }

\displaystyle N^{*}

\displaystyle~\coloneqq~B_{G}(u,k)\cup B_{G}(v,k).

We claim that

(23)

\displaystyle D^{*}\subseteq N^{*}\cap(U_{N}\cup U_{C}).

Indeed, suppose that
w\in V(G)\setminus(U_{N}\cup U_{C})
. Then
|B_{G^{-}}(w,k)|\leq|B_{G^{+}}(w,k)|\leq t_{k}
and
G^{-}[B_{G^{-}}(w,k)]
and
G^{+}[B_{G^{+}}(w,k)]
are trees. Hence,
\psi_{G^{+},k}(w)=\widehat{\psi}_{G^{+},k}(w)
and
\psi_{G^{-},k}(w)=\widehat{\psi}_{G^{-},k}(w)
. Thus,
w\notin D^{*}
. It follows that
D^{*}\subseteq U_{N}\cup U_{C}
.

On the other hand, suppose that
w\in V(G)\setminus N^{*}
.
Then
G^{+}[B_{G^{+}}(w,k)]=G^{-}[B_{G^{-}}(w,k)]
and thus
\psi_{G^{+},k}(w)=\psi_{G^{-},k}(w)
and
\widehat{\psi}_{G^{+},k}(w)=\widehat{\psi}_{G^{-},k}(w)
. Hence,
w\notin D^{*}
. It follows that
D^{*}\subseteq N^{*}
. Hence, (
23
) follows.

From (
23
) we can conclude that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[|D^{*}|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[|N^{*}\cap(U_{N}\cup U_{C})|^{2}\mathds{1}_{\{|N^{*}|>t_{k}\}}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[|N^{*}\cap(U_{N}\cup U_{C})|^{2}\mathds{1}_{\{|N^{*}|\leq t_{k}\}}\right]

(24)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[|N^{*}|^{2}\mathds{1}_{\{|N^{*}|>t_{k}\}}\right]+t_{k}\mathbb{E}\left[|N^{*}\cap(U_{N}\cup U_{C})|\right].

Hence, by (
22
), (
24
), and
Lemma

5.3
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{M}_{k}-\widehat{M}_{k}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2cn\mathbb{E}\left[|D^{*}|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq n\left(4ck\exp\left(-2c^{2}k^{2}\right)+20ck(10ck)^{2k}\exp\left(-2c^{2}k^{2}\right)\right)+O\left(1\right)

\displaystyle\leq n\exp\left(-c^{2}k^{2}\right)+O\left(1\right),

since
t_{k}\coloneqq(10ck)^{2k}
,
k\geq 100
and
c>1
, as desired.
∎

6.
A lower bound on
\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{L}_{k}\right]

In this subsection we will prove that the variance of
\widetilde{L}_{k}
is linear in
n
, where we recall that
\widetilde{L}_{k}=\widetilde{L}_{k}(G)\coloneqq n-\sum_{v\in V(G)}\phi_{G,k}(v)
.

The heuristic idea is relatively simple, but surprisingly hard to implement rigorously due to the non-monotonicity of the strong
4
-core. Let us briefly sketch our approach.
Suppose we expose
G\sim G(n,p)
via two rounds with probabilities
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
where
p_{1},p_{2}=\Theta\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)
but
p_{2}
is much smaller than
p_{1}
. Let
G_{1}\sim G(n,p_{1})
and
G_{2}\sim G(n,p_{2})
, so that
G=G_{1}\cup G_{2}
.

Since
p_{1}\approx\frac{c}{n}
, for large
c
we expect the strong
4
-core
S_{1}=S(G_{1})
to cover a large proportion of the vertices, but equally we expect there to be a linearly sized set
A^{\prime}
of isolated vertices in
G_{1}
. Furthermore, since
p_{2}=\Theta\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)
it is relatively easy to verify that we expect there to be some linear-sized subset
A\subseteq A^{\prime}
with
N_{G_{2}}(A):=B
which satisfies the following properties:

•

A
is independent in
G_{1}
;

•

B\subseteq S(G_{1})
;

•

m\coloneqq e_{G_{2}}(A,B)=\Omega(n)
;

•

|A|=\Omega(n)
;

•

\frac{m}{|A|}<\frac{1}{100}
.

•

Each
b\in B
is incident to at most one edge in
E_{G_{2}}(A,B)
.

If we condition on this likely event, and the set of edges of
G_{1}\cup G_{2}
outside of
E_{G}(A,B)
,
then by symmetry the number of edges incident to each vertex
a\in A
can be modelled by placing
m
balls into
|A|
bins.

Let us assume for simplicity’s sake that
B
is so
robustly
sapphire in
G_{1}
that exposing the edges
E_{G_{2}}(A,B)
will not change the colouring outside of
A\cup B
. In this case, the contribution to
\Phi(G)
from vertices outside of
A\cup B
will not be changed by the edges
E_{G_{2}}(A,B)
. Furthermore, it can be seen (see
Proposition

6.6
) that the contribution to
\Phi(G)
from the red-purple component containing a vertex
a\in A_{2}
depends only on the number of edges of
E_{G_{2}}(A,B)
which are incident to
a
. If this number is at most
1
the contribution is
1
, and if this number is at least
2
then the contribution is
0
.

However, standard calculations (see
Lemma

6.3
) will show that the variance of the random variable which counts the number of bins containing at least two balls when
m
balls are placed in
|A|
bins at random is at least
\frac{m^{2}}{3|A|}=\Omega(n)
. Due to the likely existence of sets
A,B
as above, this will be enough to bound the variance of
\widetilde{L}
from below by a linear function of
n
.

Of course, there are many simplifications in the strategy above. Firstly, although this is not a big difference, in the above sketch we are working with
\widetilde{L}
rather than
\widetilde{L}_{k}
. Secondly, and more crucially, it is not clear that we can guarantee the existence of sets
A
and
B
with the desired properties to make this sketch work, or even what properties we should require in terms of the robustness of the colouring of
B
.

Let us make the above sketch precise. Throughout this section we will assume that
c\geq 20
,
k\geq 100
, and that
n
is large enough. We will prove the following lower bound on the variance of
\widetilde{L}_{k}
.

Lemma 6.1
.

For every
c\geq 20
,
k\geq 100
, and
n
large enough,

\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{L}_{k}\right]\geq 2A_{c}n,

where
A_{c}\coloneqq\frac{1}{12}e^{-20c}>0
.

The proof of
Lemma

6.1
, which we give in
Subsection

6.1
, will rely on the following notion of a
robustly
sapphire set. Given a graph
H
and a subset
B\subseteq V(H)
, we say that
B
has the
robust sapphire property for
H
if the following hold.

(RS1)

For every
x\in B\cup N_{H}(B)
, we have
\{x\}\cup N_{H}(x)\subseteq S(H)
and
d_{H}(x)\geq 5
.

(RS2)

For every
b_{1},b_{2}\in B
with
b_{1}\neq b_{2}
, we have
\mathrm{dist}_{H}(b_{1},b_{2})\geq 5
.

Given a graph
H
and disjoint subsets
A,B\subseteq V(H)
, we say that
(H,A,B)
has the property
\mathcal{P}
(written
(H,A,B)\in\mathcal{P}
) if the following hold, where
H^{*}\coloneqq H-E_{H}(A,B)
.

(P1)

E_{H}(A)=E_{H}(B)=\varnothing
.

(P2)

H[A\cup B]
is a union of stars and
|N_{H}(b)\cap A|\leq 1
for each
b\in B
.

(P3)

A
consists of isolated vertices in
H^{*}
.

(P4)

B
has the robust sapphire property for
H^{*}
.

The next lemma states that if
(H,A,B)\in\mathcal{P}
, then
L_{k}(H)-L_{k}(H^{*})
is precisely the number of stars in
H[A,B]
with at least
2
edges.
This will allow us to show that if we condition on
E(H^{*})
, then the remaining variance will be the same as the variance of the number of bins with at least
2
balls in a balls in bins model.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
k\geq 2
. Let
H
be a graph and let
A,B\subseteq V(H)
be disjoint such that
(H,A,B)\in\mathcal{P}
. Let
Y
be the number of stars in
H[A,B]
with at least
2
edges. Let
H^{*}\coloneqq H-E_{H}(A,B)
. Then,

(25)

\widetilde{L}_{k}(H)=\widetilde{L}_{k}(H^{*})+Y.

We prove
Lemma

6.2
in
Subsection

6.2
.
In order to use this to prove
Lemma

6.1
, we will need the following bound on the variance of the number of bins with at least
2
balls in a balls in bins model. The proof of this is given in
Appendix

E
.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
N
and
m
be positive integers such that
m=\Theta(N)
as
N\rightarrow\infty
and
\frac{m}{N}<\frac{1}{100}
. Consider a balls and bins configuration with
N
bins and
m
balls, that is, each of the
m
balls is assigned independently and uniformly at random to one of the
N
bins.
Let
Z
be the number of bins with at least
2
balls. Then, for
N
large enough,

\mathbb{V}\left[Z\right]=h\left(\frac{m}{N}\right)N+O(1)\geq\frac{1}{3}\left(\frac{m}{N}\right)^{2}N,

as
N\rightarrow\infty
, where
h(x)\coloneqq(1+x)e^{-x}-((1+x)^{2}+x^{3})e^{-2x}
.

As mentioned in the sketch proof above, we will expose the edges of
G
in two rounds.
Let
p_{2}\coloneqq\frac{e^{-5c}}{n}
and let
p_{1}\in(0,1)
be such that
1-p=(1-p_{1})(1-p_{2})
. Let
G_{1}\sim G(n,p_{1})
and
G_{2}\sim G(n,p_{2})
be independent random graphs. Then, we can couple
G
and
(G_{1},G_{2})
such that
G=G_{1}\cup G_{2}
.
To bound the variance of
\widetilde{L}_{k}
we will implement an
edge revealing process
, that is, a process that reveals partial information about the random graphs
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
to find two vertex disjoint sets, which will play the roles of
A
and
B
from the sketch proof above. The next lemma makes precise the properties we want these sets to have. We will prove this in
Subsection

6.3
.

Lemma 6.4
.

There exists an edge revealing process that outputs two vertex disjoint sets
A_{4},B_{4}\subseteq V(G)
such that the following hold, where
G^{*}\coloneqq G-E_{G}(A_{4},B_{4}))
.

(R1)

(G,A_{4},B_{4})\in\mathcal{P}
.

(R2)

E(G_{1}[A_{4},B_{4}])=\varnothing
.

(R3)

The process reveals precisely the edges of
G^{*}
, that
E(G_{1}[A_{4},B_{4}])=\varnothing
, that
|N_{G_{2}}(b)\cap A_{4}|\leq 1
for each
b\in B_{4}
, and the number of edges in
G_{2}[A_{4},B_{4}]
, which we denote by
m
.

(R4)

\mathbb{P}\left[|A_{4}|\geq\frac{e^{-c}n}{2}\text{ and }e^{-10c}n\leq m\leq e^{-5c}n\right]\geq\frac{1}{2}
.

Note that
(R2)
implies that
G^{*}=G_{1}\cup(G_{2}-E_{G_{2}}(A_{4},B_{4}))
, that is,
G^{*}
consists of all edges of
G
except the ones from
A_{4}
to
B_{4}
that belong to
G_{2}
.

6.1.
Proof of Lemma
6.1

By
Lemma

6.4
, there exists an edge revealing process such that
(R1)
,
(R2)
,
(R3)
, and
(R4)
hold. Let
A_{4}
and
B_{4}
be the two vertex disjoint subsets of
V(G)
that the process outputs.
For disjoint
U,W\subseteq V(G)
let
\mathcal{F}(U,W)
be the set of subsets of edges of
\binom{V(G)}{2}\setminus\{\{x,y\}\colon x\in U,y\in W\}
. Furthermore for disjoint
U,W\subseteq V(G)
,
F\in\mathcal{F}(U,W)
, and
0\leq m\leq|U||W|
we let

\mathcal{E}(U,W,F,m)
be the event that

\displaystyle A_{4}=U,\quad B_{4}=W,\quad E(G)\setminus E(G[U,W])=F,\quad\text{and}\quad e(G_{2}[U,W])=m.

In addition, we let
Y(U,W,m)
be the number of stars in
G[U,W]
with at least
2
edges, conditioned on the event
\mathcal{E}(U,W,F,m)
. Note that if
\mathcal{E}(U,W,F,m)
holds, then
F=E(G^{*})
and thus
\widetilde{L}_{k}(G^{*})
is determined by
F
.
Hence,
Lemma

6.2
implies that there exists a deterministic
x(F)
such that in the event
\mathcal{E}(U,W,F,m)
one has

(26)

\displaystyle\widetilde{L}_{k}(G)=x(F)+Y(U,W,m).

Let
\mathcal{Q}
be the set of
4
-tuples
(U,W,F,m)
, where
U,W\subseteq V(G)
are disjoint,
F\in\mathcal{F}(U,W)
, and
0\leq m\leq|U||W|
are such that
\mathbb{P}\left[\mathcal{E}(U,W,F,m)\right]>0
,
|U|\geq\frac{e^{-c}n}{2}
, and
e^{-10c}n\leq m\leq e^{-5c}n
. By the law of total expectation, it follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{L}_{k}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\widetilde{L}_{k}-\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{L}_{k}\right]\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\quad\overset{\mathclap{\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{eq:x_F}}}{=}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(U,W,F,m)\\
\mathbb{P}\left[\mathcal{E}(U,W,F,m)\right]>0\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}\left[\mathcal{E}(U,W,F,m)\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\left(x(F)+Y(U,W,m)-\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{L}_{k}\right]\right)^{2}\bigg|\mathcal{E}(U,W,F,m)\right]

\displaystyle\quad\geq\sum_{(U,W,F,m)\in\mathcal{Q}}\mathbb{P}\left[\mathcal{E}(U,W,F,m)\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\left(x(F)+Y(U,W,m)-\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{L}_{k}\right]\right)^{2}\bigg|\mathcal{E}(U,W,F,m)\right]

\displaystyle\quad\geq\sum_{(U,W,F,m)\in\mathcal{Q}}\mathbb{P}\left[\mathcal{E}(U,W,F,m)\right]\mathbb{V}\left[Y(U,W,m)\bigg|\mathcal{E}(U,W,F,m)\right],

where the second inequality follows from the fact that, conditioned on
\mathcal{E}(U,W,F,m)
,
\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{L}_{k}\right]-x(F)
is a constant and that the constant
a
that minimises

\mathbb{E}\left[(Y(U,W,m)-a)^{2}\bigg|\mathcal{E}(U,W,F,m)\right]

is
a=\mathbb{E}\left[Y(U,W,m)\bigg|\mathcal{E}(U,W,F,m)\right]
.
By
(R4)
, we have

(27)

\sum_{(U,W,F,m)\in\mathcal{Q}}\mathbb{P}\left[\mathcal{E}(U,W,F,m)\right]\geq\frac{1}{2}.

Thus, it now suffices to prove that for all
(U,W,F,m)\in\mathcal{Q}
,

(28)

\mathbb{V}\left[Y(U,W,m)\bigg|\mathcal{E}(U,W,F,m)\right]\geq 4A_{c}n,

where
A_{c}\coloneqq\frac{1}{12}e^{-20c}>0
.
Observe that
\mathbb{V}\left[Y(U,W,m)\bigg|\mathcal{E}(U,W,F,m)\right]
equals the variance of the number of bins with at least
2
balls in a balls in bins configuration where each of the
m
balls is placed in one of the
|U|
many bins uniformly at random.
Using the bounds on
m
given by
(U,W,F,m)\in\mathcal{Q}
, we have by
Lemma

6.3
that the variance of this quantity can be lower bounded by
\frac{m^{2}}{3|U|}\geq 4A_{c}n
, where
A_{c}\coloneqq\frac{1}{12}e^{-20c}>0
.
This completes the proof of
Lemma

6.1
.

6.2.
The robust sapphire property

In this subsection we will prove
Lemma

6.2
. In order to do so we need to understand how adding the edges in
E(H)\setminus E(H^{*})
to
H^{*}
changes its strong
4
-core. We first prove the following proposition which tells us how a set of vertices with the robust sapphire property can limit changes to the strong
4
-core when adding more edges.

Proposition 6.5
.

Let
H^{*}
be a graph and
A,B\subseteq V(H^{*})
be disjoint such that the vertices in
A
are isolated in
H^{*}
and
B
has the robust sapphire property for
H^{*}
. Let
F\subseteq\{ab\colon a\in A,b\in B\}
and
H\coloneqq(V(H^{*}),E(H^{*})\cup F)
.
Let
B_{=1}\coloneqq\{b\in B\colon|N_{H}(b)\cap A|=1\}
,
A^{\prime}\coloneqq\{a\in A\colon d_{H}(a)\geq 4,N_{H}(a)\subseteq B_{=1}\}
, and
B^{\prime}\coloneqq N_{H}(A\setminus A^{\prime})
.
Then
(S(H^{*})\setminus B^{\prime})\cup A^{\prime}\subseteq S(H)
. In addition,
B\setminus N_{H}(A)
has the robust sapphire property for
H
.

Proof.

Let
\widetilde{S}\coloneqq(S(H^{*})\setminus B^{\prime})\cup A^{\prime}
. We first show that
\widetilde{S}\subseteq S(H)
. It suffices to show that
\widetilde{S}
has the strong
4
-core property
3
for
H
. Observe that
B\subseteq S(H^{*})
, since
B
has the robust sapphire property for
H^{*}
. Note that, since
B^{\prime}\cup N_{H}(A^{\prime})\subseteq B\subseteq S(H^{*})
,

\displaystyle N_{H}(\widetilde{S})\subseteq N_{H}(S(H^{*})\setminus B^{\prime})\cup N_{H}(A^{\prime})\subseteq N_{H^{*}}(S(H^{*}))\cup A^{\prime}\cup B\subseteq N_{H^{*}}(S(H^{*}))\cup S(H^{*})\cup A^{\prime}.

Hence,

(29)

\displaystyle\widetilde{S}\cup N_{H}(\widetilde{S})\subseteq S(H^{*})\cup N_{H^{*}}(S(H^{*}))\cup A^{\prime}.

Let
x\in\widetilde{S}\cup N_{H}(\widetilde{S})
. We show that
|N_{H}(x)\cap\widetilde{S}|\geq 4
. First suppose that
x\in A^{\prime}
. By the definition of
A^{\prime}
, we have
N_{H}(A^{\prime})\subseteq B_{=1}
. Thus, by the definition of
B_{=1}
, we have
N_{H}(A^{\prime})\subseteq B
is disjoint from
N_{H}(A\setminus A^{\prime})\eqqcolon B^{\prime}\subseteq B\setminus B_{=1}
. By the definition of
A^{\prime}
, it now follows that
x
has at least
4
neighbours in
B_{=1}\setminus B^{\prime}\subseteq B\setminus B^{\prime}\subseteq S(H^{*})\setminus B^{\prime}\subseteq\widetilde{S}
, and so
|N_{H}(x)\cap\widetilde{S}|\geq 4
.

Now assume that
x\notin A^{\prime}
and hence,
x\in S(H^{*})\cup N_{H^{*}}(S(H^{*}))
by
29
.
By the definition of
S(H^{*})
, we have

(30)

\displaystyle|N_{H^{*}}(x)\cap S(H^{*})|\geq 4.

If
x\notin B\cup N_{H^{*}}(B)
, then
N_{H^{*}}(x)=N_{H^{*}}(x)\setminus B
and so

\displaystyle|N_{H}(x)\cap\widetilde{S}|\geq|N_{H}(x)\cap(S(H^{*})\setminus B)|\geq|(N_{H^{*}}(x)\setminus B)\cap S(H^{*})|=|N_{H^{*}}(x)\cap S(H^{*})|\geq 4.

So, we may assume that
x\in B\cup N_{H^{*}}(B)
. Note that, since
B
has the robust sapphire property for
H^{*}
, we have
|N_{H^{*}}(x)\cap S(H^{*})|\geq 5
and
|N_{H^{*}}(x)\cap B|\leq 1
.
Hence,

\displaystyle|N_{H}(x)\cap\widetilde{S}|

\displaystyle\geq|N_{H}(x)\cap(S(H^{*})\setminus B)|\geq|N_{H^{*}}(x)\cap(S(H^{*})\setminus B)|

\displaystyle\geq|N_{H^{*}}(x)\cap S(H^{*})|-|N_{H^{*}}(x)\cap B|\geq 5-1=4.

We have shown that
\widetilde{S}
has the strong
4
-core property
3
for
H
and hence,
\widetilde{S}\subseteq S(H)
.

It remains to show that
\widetilde{B}\coloneqq B\setminus N_{H}(A)
has the robust sapphire property for
H
. We first note that
\mathrm{dist}_{H}(b,b^{\prime})\geq 5
for all
b\in B
and
b^{\prime}\in\widetilde{B}
. Indeed, since
\widetilde{B}\subseteq B
, by
(RS2)

\mathrm{dist}_{H^{*}}(b,b^{\prime})\geq 5
. So, let
P
be a path from
b
to
b^{\prime}
in
H
that uses an edge of
F
. Note that,
P
contains a path in
H^{*}
from
b
to a vertex in
B\setminus\widetilde{B}
. Since
B
has the robust sapphire property for
H^{*}
, this implies that
P
has length at least
5
. Hence,
\mathrm{dist}_{H}(b,b^{\prime})\geq 5
. In particular, since
\widetilde{B}\subseteq B
,
(RS2)
is satisfied.

Furthermore, it follows that
(\widetilde{B}\cup N_{H}(\widetilde{B})\cup N_{H}(N_{H}(\widetilde{B})))\cap B^{\prime}=\varnothing
and, since
B
has the robust sapphire property for
H^{*}
,
\widetilde{B}\cup N_{H}(\widetilde{B})\cup N_{H}(N_{H}(\widetilde{B}))\subseteq B\cup N_{H^{*}}(B)\cup N_{H^{*}}(N_{H^{*}}(B))
. Hence,
\widetilde{B}\cup N_{H}(\widetilde{B})\cup N_{H}(N_{H}(\widetilde{B}))\subseteq S(H^{*})\setminus B^{\prime}\subseteq S(H)
.

Finally, since
B
has the robust sapphire property for
H^{*}
, we have
d_{H}(x)=d_{H^{*}}(x)\geq 5
for all
x\in\widetilde{B}\cup N_{H}(\widetilde{B})\subseteq(B\cup N_{H^{*}}(B))\setminus B^{\prime}
. Hence,
(RS1)
is satisfied.
∎

Next, we show precisely how the strong
4
-core changes when going from
H^{*}
to
H
(in the setting of
Lemma

6.2
).

Proposition 6.6
.

Let
H
be a graph and let
A,B\subseteq V(H)
be disjoint such that
(H,A,B)\in\mathcal{P}
. Let
H^{*}\coloneqq H-E_{H}(A,B)
,
A^{\prime}\coloneqq\{x\in A\colon d_{H}(x)\geq 4\}
and
B^{\prime}\coloneqq N_{H}(A\setminus A^{\prime})\subseteq B
. Then the following hold.

(i)

S(H)=(S(H^{*})\setminus B^{\prime})\cup A^{\prime}
.

(ii)

P(H)=P(H^{*})\cup B^{\prime}
.

(iii)

R(H)=R(H^{*})\setminus A^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Let
\widetilde{S}\coloneqq(S(H^{*})\setminus B^{\prime})\cup A^{\prime}
.
Note that, since
(H,A,B)\in\mathcal{P}
, by
(P2)
, we have
B_{=1}\coloneqq\{b\in B\colon|N_{H}(b)\cap A|=1\}=B
. Thus,

A^{\prime}\coloneqq\{a\in A\colon d_{H}(a)\geq 4\}=\{a\in A\colon d_{H}(a)\geq 4,N_{H}(a)\subseteq B_{=1}\}.

Hence, by
Proposition

6.5
, we have
\widetilde{S}\subseteq S(H)
.

Next, we show that
S(H)\subseteq\widetilde{S}
. Let
W\coloneqq A\cup B^{\prime}
and
U\coloneqq S(H^{*})\setminus B^{\prime}
. Note that
B\subseteq S(H^{*})
and so
B\subseteq(S(H^{*})\setminus B^{\prime})\cup B^{\prime}\subseteq W\cup U
. Since also
A\subseteq W\cup U
, we have
E(H)\triangle E(H^{*})\subseteq\binom{A\cup B}{2}\subseteq\binom{W\cup U}{2}
. Note that
U\subseteq S(H)\cap S(H^{*})
, since
U\coloneqq S(H^{*})\setminus B^{\prime}\subseteq\widetilde{S}\subseteq S(H)
. Observe that

\displaystyle N_{H^{*}}(W)\subseteq N_{H^{*}}(B)\subseteq S(H^{*})\setminus B\subseteq U

and

\displaystyle N_{H}(W)\subseteq(B\setminus B^{\prime})\cup(N_{H}(B)\setminus A)\subseteq(S(H^{*})\setminus B^{\prime})\cup N_{H^{*}}(B)\subseteq S(H^{*})\setminus B^{\prime}=U.

Hence, by
Lemma

4.5
with
U
and
W
playing the roles of
A
and
B
, respectively, we have
S(H)\setminus W=S(H^{*})\setminus W=S(H^{*})\setminus B^{\prime}
. Thus,
S(H)\subseteq(S(H^{*})\setminus B^{\prime})\cup W
. It follows by the definition of
A^{\prime}
, that
A\setminus A^{\prime}\subseteq R(H)
and thus
B^{\prime}\subseteq P(H)
.
Hence,
W\setminus(R(H)\cup P(H))\subseteq A^{\prime}
.
It follows that

\displaystyle S(H)\subseteq(S(H^{*})\setminus B^{\prime})\cup A^{\prime}\eqqcolon\widetilde{S}.

Together with the reverse inclusion established above, we have proved
(i)
.

To prove
(ii)
, note that

\displaystyle P(H)\coloneqq N_{H}(S(H))=N_{H}((S(H^{*})\setminus B^{\prime})\cup A^{\prime})=N_{H^{*}}(S(H^{*}))\cup B^{\prime}=P(H^{*})\cup B^{\prime}.

To prove
(iii)
, note that

\displaystyle R(H)

\displaystyle\coloneqq V(H)\setminus(S(H)\cup P(H))=(V(H^{*})\setminus(S(H^{*})\cup P(H^{*})))\setminus(A\setminus A^{\prime})

\displaystyle=R(H^{*})\setminus A^{\prime}.

This completes the proof.
∎

Finally, we are ready to prove
Lemma

6.2
.

Proof of
Lemma

6.2
.

By
Proposition

6.6
, we have

(31)

\displaystyle S(H)=(S(H^{*})\setminus B^{\prime})\cup A^{\prime},\quad P(H)=P(H^{*})\cup B^{\prime},\quad\text{and}\quad R(H)=R(H^{*})\setminus A^{\prime},

where
A^{\prime}\coloneqq\{x\in A\colon d_{H}(x)\geq 4\}
and
B^{\prime}\coloneqq N_{H}(A\setminus A^{\prime})
.
Note that, using
(P4)
,

U\coloneqq N_{H}(A\cup B)=N_{H^{*}}(A\cup B)=N_{H^{*}}(B)\subseteq S(H^{*})\setminus B\subseteq S(H).

and hence,

(32)

\displaystyle U\subseteq S(H)\cap S(H^{*}).

Let
w\in V(H)\setminus(A\cup B)
. Since
E(H)\triangle E(H^{*})\subseteq\binom{A\cup B\cup U}{2}
, by
Lemma

4.6
with
U
and
A\cup B
, playing the roles of
A
and
B
, respectively, we have

(33)

\displaystyle\phi_{H,k}(w)=\phi_{H^{*},k}(w),\text{ for }w\in V(H)\setminus(A\cup B).

Note that, since by
(P3)

A
is a set of isolated vertices in
H^{*}
, we have
A\subseteq R(H^{*})
. Moreover, by
(P4)
, it follows that
B\subseteq S(H^{*})
.
For
x\in A\cup B
, we denote by
C_{x}
and
C_{x}^{*}
the components in
H[P(H)\cup R(H)]
and
H^{*}[P(H^{*})\cup R(H^{*})]
that contain
x
, respectively.
Note that for
x\in A
,
C_{x}^{*}=\{x\}
and
x\in R(H^{*})
and thus, by
Observation

4.3
,
\phi_{H^{*},k}(x)=1
. Moreover, for
x\in B
,
x\in S(H^{*})
and by
Observation

4.2
,
x\in S_{k}(H^{*},x)
implying
\phi_{H^{*},k}(x)=0
. It follows that

(34)

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in A\cup B}\phi_{H^{*},k}(x)=|A|.

By
(P1)
and
(P2)
and by
31
and
32
, for each
x\in A\cup B
we either have
x\in S(H)
or
H[C_{x}]
is a star with centre at a vertex in
A
and all leaves in
B
. Moreover, since
k\geq 2
, we have by
Observation

4.3
, that
\phi_{H,k}(x)=\phi_{H}(x)
for each
x\in A\cup B
. Let
A^{\prime}_{0}\coloneqq\{x\in A^{\prime}\colon d_{H}(x)=0\}
,
A^{\prime}_{1}\coloneqq\{x\in A^{\prime}\colon d_{H}(x)=1\}
, and
B^{\prime}_{1}\coloneqq N_{H}(A^{\prime}_{1})
.
Now, note that for
x\in A_{0}^{\prime}
, we have
C_{x}=\{x\}
and
x\in R(H)
and thus
\phi_{H,k}(x)=\phi_{H}(x)=1
.
For
x\in A^{\prime}_{1}\cup B^{\prime}_{1}
, we see that
H[C_{x}]
is a single edge with one endpoint in each of
R(H)
and
P(H)
and thus
\phi_{H,k}(x)=\phi_{H}(x)=\frac{1}{2}
. Let
x\in(A^{\prime}\cup B^{\prime})\setminus(A^{\prime}_{0}\cup A^{\prime}_{1}\cup B^{\prime}_{1})
. Then
H[C_{x}]
is a
K_{1,2}
or a
K_{1,3}
whose central vertex is in
R(H)
and whose leaves are in
P(H)
. Hence,
\phi_{H,k}(x)=\phi_{H}(x)=0
as the vertex in
R(H)
can be covered by a path of length
2
with endpoints in
P(H)
. Finally, for
x\in(A\cup B)\setminus(A^{\prime}\cup B^{\prime})
, we have
x\in S(H)
and thus
\phi_{H,k}(x)=\phi_{H}(x)=0
.
It follows that

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in A\cup B}\phi_{H,k}(x)=|A^{\prime}_{0}|+\frac{|A^{\prime}_{1}|}{2}+\frac{|B^{\prime}_{1}|}{2}=|A^{\prime}_{0}|+|A^{\prime}_{1}|.

Note that
|A|-|A^{\prime}_{0}|-|A^{\prime}_{1}|=Y
, and thus

(35)

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in A\cup B}\phi_{H,k}(x)=|A|-Y.

Hence, we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{L}_{k}(H)

\displaystyle\coloneqq n-\sum_{x\in V(H)}\phi_{H,k}(x)=n-\sum_{x\in V(H)\setminus(A\cup B)}\phi_{H,k}(x)-\sum_{x\in A\cup B}\phi_{H,k}(x)

\displaystyle{=}n-\sum_{x\in V(H)\setminus(A\cup B)}\phi_{H^{*},k}(x)-|A|+Y

\displaystyle{=}n-\sum_{x\in V(H^{*})}\phi_{H^{*},k}(x)+Y=\widetilde{L}_{k}(H^{*})+Y,

where the penultimate equality also uses that
V(H^{*})=V(H)
.
This completes the proof.
∎

6.3.
Proof of Lemma
6.4

We first describe the edge revealing process and then show that it satisfies
(R1)
,
(R2)
,
(R3)
, and
(R4)
. For ease of notation, given a graph
G
and
v\in V(G)
, we let
N_{G}[v]:=\{v\}\cup N_{G}(v)
.

Algorithm 3:
Edge revealing process

1:

c\geq 20
,
k\geq 100
, and
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

2:

Random sets
A_{4}
and
B_{4}
such that
(G,A_{4},B_{4})\in\mathcal{P}
.

3:

Reveal
E(G_{1})
and set
A_{0}\coloneqq\{v\in V(G)\colon d_{G_{1}}(v)=0\}
.

4:

Reveal all edges of
G_{2}[A_{0}]
and set
A_{1}\coloneqq\{v\in V(G)\colon N_{G}(v)\cap A=\varnothing\}
.

5:

Reveal all edges of
G_{2}[V(G)\setminus A_{1}]
.

6:

Set
G_{0}\coloneqq(V(G),E(G_{1})\cup E(G_{2}[V(G)\setminus A_{1}])\cup E(G_{2}[A_{1}]))
.
\triangleright

G_{0}
is the graph of all edges revealed so far.

7:

Use
Subsection

2.1
to determine
S(G_{0})
.

8:

Set
B_{0}\coloneqq\{b\in V(G)\colon\text{Each }x\in N_{G_{0}}[b]\text{ satisfies }N_{G_{0}}[x]\subseteq S(G_{0})\text{ and }d_{G_{0}}(x)\geq 5\}
.

9:

\triangleright

Note that
B_{0}
is the maximal set of vertices in
G_{0}
such that
(RS1)
holds with
G_{0}
and
B_{0}
playing the roles of
H
and
B
, respectively.

\triangleleft

10:

Set
B_{1}\coloneqq\varnothing
.

11:

while
There exists
x\in B_{0}\setminus B_{1}
such that
\mathrm{dist}_{G_{0}}(x,B_{1})\geq 5
.
do

12:

Add
x
to
B_{1}
.

13:

end

while

14:

\triangleright

Note that
B_{1}
is a maximal subset of
B_{0}
, with the property that no two vertices in
B_{1}
are within distance at most
4
in
G_{0}
. Observe that
B_{1}
has the robust sapphire property for
G_{0}
.

\triangleleft

15:

Set
A_{2}\coloneqq A_{1}
,
B_{2}\coloneqq B_{1}
, and
G^{*}\coloneqq G_{0}
.

16:

while
True.
do

17:

Reveal
E(G_{2}[A_{2},V(G)\setminus B_{2}])
.

18:

G^{*}\coloneqq G^{*}\cup G_{2}[A_{2},V(G)\setminus B_{2}]
.
\triangleright

G^{*}
is the graph of all edges revealed so far.

19:

A^{*}\coloneqq\{a\in A_{2}\colon d_{G^{*}}(a)=0\}
.

20:

Use
Subsection

2.1
to determine
S(G^{*})
.

21:

B_{0}^{*}\coloneqq\{b\in B_{2}\colon\text{Each }x\in N_{G^{*}}[b]\text{ satisfies }N_{G^{*}}[x]\subseteq S(G^{*})\text{ and }d_{G^{*}}(x)\geq 5\}
.

22:

B^{*}\coloneqq\varnothing
.

23:

while
There exists
x\in B_{0}^{*}\setminus B^{*}
such that
\mathrm{dist}_{G^{*}}(x,B^{*})\geq 5
.
do

24:

Add
x
to
B^{*}
.

25:

end

while

26:

\triangleright

Note that
B^{*}
is a maximal subset of
B_{2}
that has the robust sapphire property for the graph of all edges revealed so far.

\triangleleft

27:

if

A^{*}=A_{2}
and
B^{*}=B_{2}

then

28:

Break.
\triangleright
Ends the while-loop once
A_{2}
and
B_{2}
have ‘stabilised’.

29:

end

if

30:

Set
A_{2}\coloneqq A^{*}
and
B_{2}\coloneqq B^{*}
.

31:

end

while

32:

Set
A_{3}\coloneqq A_{2}
,
B_{3}\coloneqq B_{2}
, and
B_{3}^{-}\coloneqq\varnothing
.

33:

for

b\in B_{3}

do

34:

Query if
|N_{G}(b)\cap A_{3}|\geq 2
.

35:

\triangleright

We only reveal if
|N_{G}(b)\cap A_{3}|\geq 2
, not the corresponding edges.

\triangleleft

36:

if

|N_{G}(b)\cap A_{3}|\geq 2

then

37:

Add
b
to
B_{3}^{-}
.

38:

end

if

39:

end

for

40:

Reveal
E(G[A_{3},B_{3}^{-}])
.

41:

Set
G^{*}\coloneqq G^{*}\cup G[A_{3},B_{3}^{-}]
.
\triangleright

G^{*}
is the graph of all edges revealed so far.

42:

Set
A_{4}\coloneqq A_{3}\setminus N_{G^{*}}(B_{3}^{-})
.

43:

\triangleright

Note that
A_{4}=A_{3}\setminus N_{G}(B_{3}^{-})
since all edges incident to
B_{3}^{-}
have been revealed.

\triangleleft

44:

Reveal
E(G[A_{3}\setminus A_{4},B_{3}])
.

45:

Set
G^{*}\coloneqq G^{*}\cup G[A_{3}\setminus A_{4},B_{3}]
.
\triangleright

G^{*}
is the graph of all edges revealed so far.

46:

Set
B_{4}\coloneqq B_{3}\setminus N_{G^{*}}(A_{3}\setminus A_{4})
.

47:

\triangleright

Note that
B_{4}=B_{3}\setminus N_{G}(A_{3}\setminus A_{4})
, since all edges incident to
A_{3}\setminus A_{4}
have been revealed.

\triangleleft

48:

Reveal the number of edges
m
in
G_{2}[A_{4},B_{4}]
.
\triangleright
We do not reveal the corresponding edges.

49:

return

A_{4}
,
B_{4}
, and
m
.

We first show that
(R1)
holds, that is,
(G,A_{4},B_{4})\in\mathcal{P}
. Note that
(P1)
,
(P2)
, and
(P3)
clearly hold by construction. We now show that
(P4)
holds, that is, that
B_{4}
has the robust sapphire property for
G^{*}\coloneqq G-E_{G}(A_{4},B_{4})
.

By construction,
B_{3}
has the robust sapphire property for
G-E_{G}(A_{3},B_{3})
and
A_{3}
is isolated in
G-E_{G}(A_{3},B_{3})
. Hence, by
Proposition

6.5
with
G-E_{G}(A_{3},B_{3})
,
G^{*}
,

A_{3}
, and
B_{3}
playing the roles of
H^{*}
,
H
,
A
, and
B
, respectively, it follows that
B_{3}\setminus N_{G^{*}}(A_{3})
has the robust sapphire property for
G^{*}
. We claim that
B_{3}\setminus N_{G^{*}}(A_{3})=B_{4}
, and hence,
B_{4}
has the robust sapphire property for
G^{*}
as claimed.
Indeed, since
B_{4}=B_{3}\setminus N_{G}(A_{3}\setminus A_{4})
, we have
E_{G}(A_{3}\setminus A_{4},B_{4})=\varnothing
. We now show that
E_{G}(A_{4},B_{3}\setminus B_{4})=\varnothing
. Suppose for a contradiction, that there is an edge
ab\in E(G)
with
a\in A_{4}
and
b\in B_{3}\setminus B_{4}
. Since
b\in B_{3}\setminus B_{4}
, we have
b\in N_{G}(A_{3}\setminus A_{4})
. Let
a^{\prime}\in A_{3}\setminus A_{4}
such that
a^{\prime}b\in E(G)
. Hence,
|N_{G}(b)\cap A_{3}|\geq 2
, so
b\in B_{3}^{-}
. This implies that
a\in N_{G}(B_{3}^{-})
, a contradiction to our assumption that
a\in A_{4}=A_{3}\setminus N_{G}(B_{3}^{-})
.
Since
E_{G}(A_{3}\setminus A_{4},B_{4})=\varnothing
and
E_{G}(A_{4},B_{3}\setminus B_{4})=\varnothing
, we have
G^{*}\coloneqq G-E_{G}(A_{4},B_{4})
contains all the edges of
G
incident to
A_{3}\setminus A_{4}
but none of the edges of
G
incident to
A_{4}
. It follows that
B_{4}=B_{3}\setminus N_{G}(A_{3}\setminus A_{4})=B_{3}\setminus N_{G^{*}}(A_{3})
.

Now, note that
(R2)
is satisfied, since
A_{4}
is contained in
A_{0}
, the set of isolated vertices of
G_{1}
.
Moreover, note that by construction
Subsection

6.3
satisfies
(R3)
.

It remains to prove that
(R4)
holds.
Every isolated vertex of
G
belongs to
A_{4}
. Thus, by a simple application of McDiarmid’s inequality (
Lemma

3.4
), whp
|A_{4}|\geq\frac{e^{-c}n}{2}
.
Moreover,
m\leq e(G_{2})\leq 2p_{2}\frac{n^{2}}{2}\leq e^{-5c}n
holds with high probability by a Chernoff bound.
To show that
(R4)
holds, it now suffices to prove that
m\geq e^{-10c}n
with probability at least
\frac{7}{10}
. To that end, we first prove that whp
|B_{1}|\geq\frac{n}{c^{7}}
, and then that
|B_{3}|\geq\frac{n}{2c^{7}}
with probability at least
\frac{4}{5}
. We start with the following (standard) bound on the size of the neighbourhood of a set of vertices.

Claim 6.7
.

Whp for every set
W\subseteq V(G)
, we have
|N_{G}(W)|\leq\max\{e^{-2c}n,(2c\log c)|W|\}
.

Proof of Claim.

The probability that there exists
W\subseteq V(G)
of size at least
e^{-3c}n
with
|N_{G}(W)|>(2c\log c)|W|
is at most

\displaystyle\sum_{j\geq e^{-3c}n}\binom{n}{j}\binom{n}{(2c\log c)j}j^{(2c\log c)j}p^{(2c\log c)j}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j\geq e^{-3c}n}\bigg(\frac{en}{j}\left(\frac{enp}{2c\log c}\right)^{2c\log c}\bigg)^{j}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j\geq e^{-3c}n}\bigg(e^{3c+1}\left(\frac{e}{2\log c}\right)^{2c\log c}\bigg)^{j}=o(1).

Thus, whp
|N_{G}(W)|\leq(2c\log c)|W|
for every
W\subseteq V(G)
with
|W|\geq e^{-3c}n
. Now, suppose this event holds and let
W\subseteq V(G)
with
|W|<e^{-3c}n
. Let
W^{\prime}\subseteq V(G)
be such that
W\subseteq W^{\prime}
and
|W^{\prime}|=\lceil e^{-3c}n\rceil
. Then, since
N_{G}(W)\subseteq W\cup N_{G}(W)\subseteq W^{\prime}\cup N_{G}(W^{\prime})
,

\displaystyle|N_{G}(W)|

\displaystyle\leq|W|+|N_{G}(W)|\leq|W^{\prime}|+|N_{G}(W^{\prime})|\leq(2c\log c+1)|W^{\prime}|

\displaystyle=(2c\log c+1)\lceil e^{-3c}n\rceil\leq e^{-2c}n.

This completes the proof of the claim.
∎

We also need a bound on the number of vertices of a given degree in
G_{0}
.

Claim 6.8
.

Let
0\leq i\leq 6
. Then whp the number of vertices of degree
i
in
G_{0}
is at most

\left(1+\frac{1}{10}\right)\frac{c^{i}e^{-c}}{i!}n.

Proof of Claim.

Fix
0\leq i\leq 6
and set
Y_{i}
and
\widetilde{Y}_{i}
to be the number of vertices of degree
i
in
G_{0}
and
G
, respectively. Let
Z
be the number of vertices that are adjacent to at least one edge of
G_{2}
. Note that
Y_{i}\leq\widetilde{Y}_{i}+Z
. Observe that
\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{Y}_{i}\right]=n\binom{n-1}{i}p^{i}(1-p)^{n-1-i}=(1+o(1))\frac{c^{i}e^{-c}}{i!}n
and
\mathbb{E}\left[Z\right]\leq n(n-1)p_{2}\leq e^{-5c}n
. Since adding or removing an edge changes the value of
\widetilde{Y}_{i}
and
Z
by at most
2
, by McDiarmid’s inequality (
Lemma

3.4
), whp
\widetilde{Y}_{i}\leq\left(1+\frac{1}{20}\right)\frac{c^{i}e^{-c}}{i!}n
for all
i\in[6]
and
Z\leq\left(1+\frac{1}{20}\right)e^{-5c}n
. Hence, whp
Y_{i}\leq\left(1+\frac{1}{10}\right)\frac{c^{i}e^{-c}}{i!}n
for all
i\in[6]
.
∎

Next, we prove a lower bound on
|B_{1}|
. We aim to show that

(36)

\displaystyle\textit{whp}\qquad|B_{1}|\geq\frac{n}{c^{7}}.

Let
V_{4}
be the set of vertices of degree
4
in
G_{0}
. The key observation is that

(37)

V(G)\setminus\left(R(G_{0})\cup P(G_{0})\cup N_{G_{0}}(P(G_{0}))\cup N_{G_{0}}(N_{G_{0}}(P(G_{0})))\cup V_{4}\cup N_{G_{0}}(V_{4})\right)\subseteq B_{0}.

Indeed, suppose that

x\in V(G)\setminus\left(R(G_{0})\cup P(G_{0})\cup N_{G_{0}}(P(G_{0}))\cup N_{G_{0}}(N_{G_{0}}(P(G_{0})))\cup V_{4}\cup N_{G_{0}}(V_{4})\right).

Since
x\notin R(G_{0})\cup P(G_{0})\cup N_{G_{0}}(P(G_{0}))\cup N_{G_{0}}(N_{G_{0}}(P(G_{0})))
, we have
N_{G_{0}}[y]\subseteq S(G_{0})
for every
y\in N_{G_{0}}[x]
.
Since
x\notin V_{4}\cup N_{G_{0}}(V_{4})
and since all vertices of degree
3
or fewer in
G_{0}
are in
R(G_{0})
, we have
d_{G_{0}}(y)\geq 5
for all
y\in N_{G_{0}}[x]
.
It follows that
x\in B_{0}
. Thus, we have proved
37
.

Claim 6.9
.

Whp

|R(G_{0})|+|P(G_{0})|+|N_{G_{0}}(P(G_{0}))|+|N_{G_{0}}(N_{G_{0}}(P(G_{0})))|+|V_{4}|+|N_{G_{0}}(V_{4})|\leq\frac{3}{5}n.

Proof of Claim.

Note that if
R(G_{0})=\varnothing
, then also
P(G_{0})=\varnothing
. So we may assume without loss of generality that
R(G_{0})\neq\varnothing
. Let
r\coloneqq|P(G_{0})|/|R(G_{0})|
. Note that by
Observation

4.1
,
|P(G_{0})|\leq 3|R(G_{0})|
and so
0<r\leq 3
.
Note further that
P(G_{0})=N_{G_{0}}(R(G_{0}))
and thus
r|R(G_{0})|=|P(G_{0})|=|N_{G_{0}}(R(G_{0}))|\leq e_{G_{0}}(R(G_{0}),V(G)\setminus R(G_{0}))
.
Moreover, note that
R(G_{0})
is incident with at most
3|R(G_{0})|
edges in
G_{0}
by
Observation

4.1
. Hence,

\displaystyle e_{G_{0}}(R(G_{0}))+r|R(G_{0})|\leq e_{G_{0}}(R(G_{0}))+e_{G_{0}}(R(G_{0}),V(G)\setminus R(G_{0}))\leq 3|R(G_{0})|.

It follows that
e_{G_{0}}(R(G_{0}))\leq(3-r)|R(G_{0})|
. We deduce that

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in R(G_{0})}d_{G_{0}}(x)

\displaystyle=2e_{G_{0}}(R(G_{0}))+e_{G_{0}}(R(G_{0}),V(G)\setminus R(G_{0}))

(38)

\displaystyle\leq(3-r)|R(G_{0})|+3|R(G_{0})|=(6-r)|R(G_{0})|.

For
0\leq i\leq 7
, we let
d_{i}
and
d_{\geq i}
be the number of vertices in
R(G_{0})
with degree equal to
i
and at least
i
in
G_{0}
, respectively.
Note that

(39)

\displaystyle|R(G_{0})|=\sum_{i=0}^{5}d_{i}+d_{\geq 6}\quad\text{and}\quad|R(G_{0})|=\sum_{i=0}^{6}d_{i}+d_{\geq 7}

and

(40)

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in R(G_{0})}d_{G_{0}}(x)\geq\sum_{i=0}^{5}id_{i}+6d_{\geq 6}\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{x\in R(G_{0})}d_{G_{0}}(x)\geq\sum_{i=0}^{6}id_{i}+7d_{\geq 7}.

Combining
40
and
38
, we obtain

(41)

\displaystyle 6d_{\geq 6}\leq(6-r)|R(G_{0})|-\sum_{i=0}^{5}id_{i}\quad\text{and}\quad 7d_{\geq 7}\leq(6-r)|R(G_{0})|-\sum_{i=0}^{6}id_{i}.

Combining
41
and
39
gives

\displaystyle 6|R(G_{0})|=\sum_{i=0}^{5}6d_{i}+6d_{\geq 6}\leq\sum_{i=0}^{5}(6-i)d_{i}+(6-r)|R(G_{0})|

and

\displaystyle 7|R(G_{0})|=\sum_{i=0}^{6}7d_{i}+7d_{\geq 7}\leq\sum_{i=0}^{6}(7-i)d_{i}+(6-r)|R(G_{0})|\leq\sum_{i=0}^{6}(7-i)d_{i}+6|R(G_{0})|.

From these inequalities, we deduce

\displaystyle|R(G_{0})|\leq\frac{1}{r}\sum_{i=0}^{5}(6-i)d_{i}\quad\text{and}\quad|R(G_{0})|\leq\sum_{i=0}^{6}(7-i)d_{i}.

Since
|P(G_{0})|=r|R(G_{0})|
, we obtain

\displaystyle|P(G_{0})|\leq\sum_{i=0}^{5}(6-i)d_{i}\quad\text{and}\quad|R(G_{0})|\leq\sum_{i=0}^{6}(7-i)d_{i}.

Together with
Claim

6.8
, we have whp

(42)

\displaystyle|P(G_{0})|\leq\sum_{i=0}^{5}(6-i)\left(1+\frac{1}{10}\right)\frac{c^{i}e^{-c}}{i!}n\leq\frac{2c^{5}e^{-c}}{5!}n

and

(43)

\displaystyle|R(G_{0})|\leq\sum_{i=0}^{6}(7-i)\left(1+\frac{1}{10}\right)\frac{c^{i}e^{-c}}{i!}n\leq\frac{2c^{6}e^{-c}}{6!}n.

Combining
Claim

6.7
with
42
, we obtain that whp

(44)

\displaystyle|N_{G_{0}}(P(G_{0}))|\leq|N_{G}(P(G_{0}))|\leq\frac{2c^{6}(\log c)e^{-c}}{5!}n

and

(45)

\displaystyle|N_{G_{0}}(N_{G_{0}}(P(G_{0})))|\leq|N_{G}(N_{G}(P(G_{0})))|\leq\frac{2c^{7}(\log c)^{2}e^{-c}}{5!}n.

Moreover, by
Claim

6.8
, whp

(46)

\displaystyle|V_{4}|+|N_{G_{0}}(V_{4})|\leq 5|V_{4}|\leq 5\left(1+\frac{1}{10}\right)\frac{c^{4}e^{-c}}{4!}n.

It follows from
43
,
42
,
44
,
45
, and
46
that whp

\displaystyle|R(G_{0})|+|P(G_{0})|+|N_{G_{0}}(P(G_{0}))|+|N_{G_{0}}(N_{G_{0}}(P(G_{0})))|+|V_{4}|+|N_{G_{0}}(V_{4})|

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{2c^{6}e^{-c}}{6!}n+\frac{2c^{5}e^{-c}}{5!}n+\frac{2c^{6}(\log c)e^{-c}}{5!}n+\frac{2c^{7}(\log c)^{2}e^{-c}}{5!}n+5\left(1+\frac{1}{10}\right)\frac{c^{4}e^{-c}}{4!}n

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{3c^{7}(\log c)^{2}e^{-c}}{5!}n\leq\frac{3}{5}n,

as desired.
∎

By
Claim

6.9
and
37
, we have whp

\displaystyle|B_{0}|

\displaystyle\geq n-\left(|R(G_{0})|+|P(G_{0})|+|N_{G_{0}}(P(G_{0}))|+|N_{G_{0}}(N_{G_{0}}(P(G_{0})))|+|V_{4}|+|N_{G_{0}}(V_{4})|\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{2}{5}n.

Since
\mathrm{dist}_{G}(b,B_{1})\leq 4
for all
b\in B_{0}
, whp the set
B_{1}
has the property that

|B_{1}|+|N_{G}(B_{1})|+|N_{G}(N_{G}(B_{1}))|+|N_{G}(N_{G}(N_{G}(B_{1})))|+|N_{G}(N_{G}(N_{G}(N_{G}(B_{1}))))|\geq\frac{2}{5}n.

As
\frac{n}{c^{7}}\geq e^{-3c}n
and

\frac{1}{c^{7}}\sum_{i=0}^{4}(2c\log c)^{i}<\frac{2}{5}
for
c\geq 20
,
Claim

6.7
implies that
|B_{1}|\geq\frac{n}{c^{7}}
, proving
36
.

We are now ready to prove the bound on
|B_{3}|
. We will show that

(47)

\displaystyle|B_{3}|\geq\frac{n}{2c^{7}}\qquad\text{ with probability at least }\frac{4}{5}.

Let
U
be the set of vertices in red/purple components of size at least 2 of
\widetilde{G}\coloneqq G-E_{G}(A_{3},B_{3})
that contain a vertex in
A_{0}
(and hence, also an edge of
G_{2}
).

Claim 6.10
.

Let
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{comp}}
be the event that every component in
\widetilde{G}[R(\widetilde{G})\cup P(\widetilde{G})]
has size at most
\log^{4}n
.
Then
\mathbb{P}\left[\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{comp}}^{c}\right]=o(n^{-2})
.

The proof of this claim is very similar to the proof of
Lemma

4.15
(except that we upper bound the probability that an edge is not present by
1-p_{1}
instead of
1-p
), so we omit it.

Note that each component
C
of
\widetilde{G}[R(\widetilde{G})\cup P(\widetilde{G})]
with
C\cap A_{0}\neq\varnothing
of size
k\geq 2
contains at least
\frac{k}{4}
vertices of
R(\widetilde{G})
by
Observation

4.1
. Those vertices have no neighbours in
\widetilde{G}
outside of
V(C)
. Moreover, since
C\cap A_{0}\neq\varnothing
, we have that
C
contains an edge of
G_{2}
.
It follows that,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[|U|\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[|U|\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{comp}}}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[|U|\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{comp}}^{c}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=2}^{\log^{4}n}k\binom{n}{k}k^{k-2}p^{k-2}(kp_{2})\binom{k}{\lceil\frac{k}{4}\rceil}(1-p_{1})^{\lceil\frac{k}{4}\rceil(n-k)}+n\mathbb{P}\left[\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{comp}}^{c}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=2}^{\log^{4}n}(1+o(1))\left(\frac{en}{k}\right)^{k}k^{k}p^{k}\left(\frac{p_{2}}{p^{2}}\right)2^{k}e^{-p_{1}\lceil\frac{k}{4}\rceil n}+o(1)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=2}^{\log^{4}n}(1+o(1))\left(\frac{p_{2}}{p^{2}}\right)\left(2enpe^{-\frac{n}{4}(p-p_{2})}\right)^{k}+o(1)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k\geq 2}(1+o(1))c^{-2}e^{-5c}\left(\frac{4}{5}\right)^{k}n+o(1)\leq\frac{1}{10}e^{-5c}n.

Markov’s inequality implies that
|U|\leq e^{-5c}n
with probability at least
\frac{9}{10}
.
Let
U_{\geq 1}^{*}
be the set of vertices adjacent to at least one edge of
G_{2}
.
Note that
\mathbb{E}\left[|U_{\geq 1}^{*}|\right]\leq n^{2}p_{2}=e^{-5c}n
. A simple application of McDiarmid’s inequality (
Lemma

3.4
) gives that whp
|U_{\geq 1}^{*}|\leq 2e^{-5c}n
.

Claim 6.11
.

Let
x\in B_{1}
with
\mathrm{dist}_{G}(x,U\cup U_{\geq 1}^{*})\geq 4
. Then
x\in B_{3}
.

Proof of Claim.

Let
y\in B_{1}
with
y\neq x
. By the definition of
B_{1}
, we have
\mathrm{dist}_{G_{0}}(x,y)\geq 5
. Since
\mathrm{dist}_{G}(x,U_{\geq 1}^{*})\geq 4
, any path from
x
to
y
that is in
G
but not in
G_{0}
(and thus contains an edge of
G_{2}
) is of length at least
5
. Hence,
\mathrm{dist}_{\widetilde{G}}(x,y)\geq 5
.
Note that since
\mathrm{dist}_{G}(x,U_{\geq 1}^{*})\geq 4
, we have

\widetilde{G}\left[N_{\widetilde{G}}[x]\cup N_{\widetilde{G}}(N_{\widetilde{G}}(x))\right]=G_{0}\left[N_{G_{0}}[x]\cup N_{G_{0}}(N_{G_{0}}(x))\right].

Thus, in particular, since
B_{1}
has the robust sapphire property for
G_{0}
, we have

(48)

\displaystyle N_{\widetilde{G}}[y]\subseteq S(G_{0})\quad\text{and}\quad d_{\widetilde{G}}(y)\geq 5,\quad\text{ for every }y\in N_{\widetilde{G}}[x].

Hence, it suffices to show that
N_{\widetilde{G}}[x]\cup N_{\widetilde{G}}(N_{\widetilde{G}}(x))\subseteq S(\widetilde{G})
. To that end, let
z\in N_{\widetilde{G}}[x]\cup N_{\widetilde{G}}(N_{\widetilde{G}}(x))
. Note that by
48
, we have
z\in S(G_{0})
. Moreover, since
\mathrm{dist}_{G}(x,z)\leq 2
and
\mathrm{dist}_{G}(x,U)\geq 4
, we have
z\notin U
. By the definition of
U
and by
z\notin A_{0}
,
z
is contained in a component
C
of
\widetilde{G}[R(\widetilde{G})\cup P(\widetilde{G})]
that does not contain a vertex of
A_{0}
. Let
W\coloneqq V(G)\setminus(C\cup N_{\widetilde{G}}(C))
. Since all edges in
E(G_{0})\triangle E(\widetilde{G})
are incident to
A_{0}
, we have
N_{\widetilde{G}}(W)=N_{G_{0}}(W)=N_{G_{0}}(C)=N_{\widetilde{G}}(C)
and
E(G_{0})\triangle E(\widetilde{G})\subseteq\binom{W\cup N_{\widetilde{G}}(C)}{2}
. Furthermore, since
C
is a component of
\widetilde{G}[R(\widetilde{G})\cup P(\widetilde{G})]
, we have
N_{\widetilde{G}}(C)\subseteq S(\widetilde{G})
. Hence, by
Lemma

4.4
with
N_{\widetilde{G}}(C)
and
W
playing the roles of
A
and
B
, respectively, we have
S(G_{0})\cap C\subseteq S(\widetilde{G})\cap C
. Finally, since
z\in S(G_{0})\cap C
,
z\in S(\widetilde{G})
.
∎

Thus,
Claims

6.7
and
36
imply that with probability at least
\frac{4}{5}
, we have

\displaystyle|B_{3}|

\displaystyle\geq|B_{1}|-(|U\cup U_{\geq 1}^{*}|+|N_{G}(U\cup U_{\geq 1}^{*})|+|N_{G}(N_{G}(U\cup U_{\geq 1}^{*}))|

\displaystyle\phantom{{}\geq|B_{1}|-(|U\cup U_{\geq 1}^{*}|}+|N_{G}(N_{G}(N_{G}(U\cup U_{\geq 1}^{*})))|)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{n}{c^{7}}-e^{-2c}n\sum_{i=0}^{3}(2c\log c)^{i}\geq\frac{n}{2c^{7}}.

This proves
47
.

Finally, we will show that

(49)

\displaystyle m\geq e^{-10c}n\qquad\text{ with probability at least }\frac{7}{10}.

Note that
m\coloneqq e_{G_{2}}(A_{4},B_{4})
is bounded below by the number
m^{\prime}
of edge-components
6
6

6

By ‘edge-components’ we mean components isomorphic to
K_{2}
.
in
G_{2}[A_{3},B_{3}]
. Indeed, any edge that forms such a component in
G_{2}[A_{3},B_{3}]
is also in
G[A_{4},B_{4}]
by the definition of
A_{4}
and
B_{4}
.
Observe that, by the time we define
A_{3}
and
B_{3}
, we have not revealed anything about the edges in
G[A_{3},B_{3}]
. Hence, for each
x\in A_{3}
and
y\in B_{3}
, the edge
xy
is in
G_{2}[A_{3},B_{3}]
independently with probability
p_{2}
.
Note further, that
|A_{3}|
is bounded from below by the number of isolated vertices of
G
, thus whp
|A_{3}|\geq\frac{e^{-c}n}{2}
. Thereafter,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[m^{\prime}\;\middle|\;|A_{3}|\geq\frac{e^{-c}n}{2},|B_{3}|\geq\frac{n}{2c^{7}}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{e^{-c}n}{2}\frac{n}{2c^{7}}p_{2}(1-p)^{2n}\geq\frac{e^{-c}n}{2}\frac{n}{2c^{7}}\frac{e^{-5c}}{n}\frac{e^{-2c}}{2}\geq\frac{e^{-9c}n}{100}.

Observe that, adding or removing an edge from
G_{2}[A_{3},B_{3}]
changes
m^{\prime}
by at most
2
. Thus, a simple application of McDiarmid’s inequality (
Lemma

3.4
) gives that whp
m\geq m^{\prime}\geq e^{-10c}n
conditioned on the events
|A_{3}|\geq\frac{e^{-c}n}{2}
and
|B_{3}|\geq\frac{n}{2c^{7}}
.
Thus, by
47
,
m\geq e^{-10c}n
with probability at least
\frac{7}{10}
.

This proves
49
and thus
Lemma

6.4
.

7.
Properties of
\widehat{L}_{k}

In this section, we will prove that
\left(\frac{\mathbb{V}\left[\widehat{L}_{k}\right]}{n}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
converges to a constant
\sigma_{k}>0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
and the the sequence
\sigma_{k}
converges as
k\rightarrow\infty
. This will allow us to prove
Lemma

2.8
. Moreover, we will show using Stein’s method that
\widehat{L}_{k}
satisfies a central limit theorem (
Lemma

2.7
). As we showed in the proof of
Theorem

1.2
, this then carries over into a central limit theorem for
L\left(G\left(n,\frac{c}{n}\right)\right)
.

7.1.
The form of
\mathbb{V}\left[\widehat{L}_{k}\right]
and the convergence of
\frac{\mathbb{V}\left[\widehat{L}_{k}\right]}{n}
in
n

It will be convenient in what follows to view
\widehat{L}_{k}
not as a weighted sum of neighbourhood counts, but rather by considering the contribution from each set
U\subseteq V(G)
coming from vertices whose neighbourhood is
U
.

More concretely, we can rewrite
\widehat{L}_{k}
as follows, where we recall that
\mathcal{T}
is the set of rooted trees with
1\leq v(T)\leq t_{k}\coloneqq(10ck)^{2k}
and radius at most
k
.

\displaystyle\widehat{L}_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{v\in V(G)}\sum_{(T,r)\in\mathcal{T}}\alpha_{(T,r)}\mathds{1}_{\{(G[B_{G}(v,k)],v)\cong(T,r)\}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}U\subseteq V(G)\\
1\leq|U|\leq t_{k}\end{subarray}}\sum_{v\in U}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(T,r)\in\mathcal{T}\\
v(T)=|U|\end{subarray}}\alpha_{(T,r)}\mathds{1}_{\{B_{G}(v,k)=U\}}\mathds{1}_{\{(G[U],v)\cong(T,r)\}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}U\subseteq V(G)\\
1\leq|U|\leq t_{k}\end{subarray}}\sum_{v\in U}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(T,r)\in\mathcal{T}\\
v(T)=|U|\end{subarray}}\alpha_{(T,r)}I(U,v,T,r),

where we have defined
I(U,v,T,r)\coloneqq\mathds{1}_{\{B_{G}(v,k)=U\}}\mathds{1}_{\{(G[U],v)\cong(T,r)\}}
as this term will occur frequently.
Letting

(50)

\displaystyle Y_{U}\coloneqq\sum_{v\in U}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(T,r)\in\mathcal{T}\\
v(T)=|U|\end{subarray}}\alpha_{(T,r)}I(U,v,T,r),

and
\mathcal{U}_{k}\coloneqq\{U\subseteq V(G)\colon 1\leq|U|\leq t_{k}\}
,
we have
\widehat{L}_{k}=\sum_{U\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}Y_{U}
.

We start by showing that
\mathbb{V}\left[\widehat{L}_{k}\right]=O(n)
and that the limit
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\mathbb{V}\left[\widehat{L}_{k}\right]}{n}
exists.

Lemma 7.1
.

There exists a constant
K(k,c)\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\mathbb{V}\left[\widehat{L}_{k}\right]=K(k,c)n+O(1)
.

In fact, we show that this hold more generally for any truncated weighted neighbourhood sum.

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
\widehat{M}_{k}\coloneqq\sum_{v\in V(G)}\sum_{(T,r)\in\mathcal{T}}\beta_{(T,r)}\mathds{1}_{\{(G[B_{G}(v,k)],v)\cong(T,r)\}}
be a truncated weighted neighbourhood sum as in
21
with weights
\bm{\beta}\coloneqq\left(\beta_{(T,r)}\right)_{(T,r)\in\mathcal{T}}
.
Then there exists a constant
K(k,c,\bm{\beta})\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\mathbb{V}\left[\widehat{M}_{k}\right]=K(k,c,\bm{\beta})n+O(1)
.

Since the proof consists of standard, albeit reasonably involved, combinatorial estimates, we defer the details to
Appendix

F
.

However, despite the fact that we can give an explicit formula for the constant
K(k,c)
in
Lemma

7.1
, it is not obvious that
K(k,c)\neq 0
. Since it will be important for the application of Stein’s method that
\mathbb{V}\left[\widehat{L}_{k}\right]
is not too small, we will also want to establish a lower bound on
\mathbb{V}\left[\widehat{L}_{k}\right]
, which we will do by relating it to
\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{L}_{k}\right]
, which we have already bounded from below in
Lemma

6.1
.

Lemma 7.3
.

For fixed
c\geq 20
, there exists
k_{0}(c)\geq 100
such that for all fixed
k\geq k_{0}(c)
the following hold, where
A_{c}
and
B^{*}
are as in
Lemma

6.1
and
Lemma

5.1
, respectively.

(i)

\mathbb{V}\left[\widehat{L}_{k}\right]=\Theta(n)
.

(ii)

The limit
\sigma_{k}\coloneqq\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\left(\frac{\mathbb{V}\left[\widehat{L}_{k}\right]}{n}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
exists.

(iii)

0<\left(A_{c}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\sigma_{k}\leq\left(2cB^{*}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
.

Proof.

By
Lemma

7.1
, we have
\mathbb{V}\left[\widehat{L}_{k}\right]=K(k,c)n+O(1)=O(n)
and

\sigma_{k}\coloneqq\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\left(\frac{\mathbb{V}\left[\widehat{L}_{k}\right]}{n}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}=\sqrt{K(k,c)}.

Note however, that
Lemma

7.1
does not rule out the possibility that
K(k,c)=0
. So, let us give quantitative bounds on
\mathbb{V}\left[\widehat{L}_{k}\right]
.

By
Lemmas

6.1
and
5.1
,

(51)

\displaystyle 2A_{c}n\leq\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{L}_{k}\right]=n\mathbb{V}\left[X_{k}\right]\leq cB^{*}n+o(n),

and by
Lemma

2.5
, we have

(52)

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{L}_{k}-\widehat{L}_{k}\right]\leq\exp\left(-c^{2}k^{2}\right)n+O(1).

Hence, by (
51
), (
52
) and
Theorem

3.1
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{V}\left[\widehat{L}_{k}\right]-\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{L}_{k}\right]\right|

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{V}\left[\widehat{L}_{k}-\widetilde{L}_{k}\right]+2\left(\mathbb{V}\left[\widehat{L}_{k}-\widetilde{L}_{k}\right]\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{L}_{k}\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-c^{2}k^{2}\right)n+O(1)+2\left(cB^{*}\exp\left(-c^{2}k^{2}\right)+o(1)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}n

(53)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\min\left\{A_{c},cB^{*}\right\}+o(1)\right)n,

for all
k\geq k_{0}(c)
if
k_{0}(c)
is sufficiently large. In particular, by (
51
) and (
53
), it follows that

\mathbb{V}\left[\widehat{L}_{k}\right]=\Theta(n)\text{ and }\left(A_{c}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\sigma_{k}\leq\left(2cB^{*}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

for all
k\geq k_{0}(c)
if
k_{0}(c)
is sufficiently large.
∎

7.2.
The convergence of
\sigma_{k}
in
k

We first prove the following lemma which roughly speaking states that
\mathbb{V}\left[\widehat{L}_{k}\right]
and
\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{L}\right]
are close to each other. This will allow us to show that the sequence of real numbers
(\sigma_{k})_{k\geq 1}
given by
Lemma

7.3
has a limit.

Lemma 7.4
.

For every fixed
c\geq 20
, there exists
\widetilde{k}_{0}(c)\geq 100
such that for all
k\geq\widetilde{k}_{0}(c)
,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{n}\left|\mathbb{V}\left[\widehat{L}_{k}\right]-\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{L}\right]\right|\leq 2\left(\frac{4}{5}\right)^{\frac{k}{2}}.

Proof.

Fix
c\geq 20
, let
\widetilde{k}_{0}(c)
be large enough, and let
k\geq\widetilde{k}_{0}(c)
.
Note that

(54)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{n}\left|\mathbb{V}\left[\widehat{L}_{k}\right]-\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{L}\right]\right|\leq\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{n}\left|\mathbb{V}\left[\widehat{L}_{k}\right]-\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{L}_{k}\right]\right|+\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{n}\left|\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{L}_{k}\right]-\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{L}\right]\right|.

We bound these two terms as follows. For the second term, we observe that, by

Theorem

3.1
,
Lemma

2.3
, and
Lemma

5.1
,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{n}\left|\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{L}\right]-\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{L}_{k}\right]\right|

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\left(\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{X}-\widetilde{X}_{k}\right]+2\left(\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{X}-\widetilde{X}_{k}\right]\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{X}_{k}\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 64ck^{2}\left(\frac{3}{4}\right)^{k-2}+2\left(64ck^{2}\left(\frac{3}{4}\right)^{k-2}cB^{*}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

(55)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{4}{5}\right)^{\frac{k}{2}},

if
\widetilde{k}_{0}(c)
is large enough compared to
B^{*}
.

We bound the first term in
54
similarly. By
Theorem

3.1
,
Lemma

2.5
, and
Lemma

5.1
,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{n}\left|\mathbb{V}\left[\widehat{L}_{k}\right]-\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{L}_{k}\right]\right|

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\left(\mathbb{V}\left[\widehat{X}_{k}-\widetilde{X}_{k}\right]+2\left(\mathbb{V}\left[\widehat{X}_{k}-\widetilde{X}_{k}\right]\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{X}_{k}\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)

(56)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{4}{5}\right)^{\frac{k}{2}}.

By
54
,
56
, and
55
we obtain the desired conclusion.
∎

We now show that
(\sigma_{k})_{k\geq 1}
has a limit.

Lemma 7.5
.

For every fixed
c\geq 20
, the sequence of real numbers
(\sigma_{k})_{k\geq 1}
given by
Lemma

7.3
has a limit
\sigma
as
k\rightarrow\infty
with
0<\left(A_{c}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\sigma\leq\left(2cB^{*}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
, where
A_{c}
and
B^{*}
are as in
Lemma

6.1
and
Lemma

5.1
, respectively.

Proof.

We show that
(\sigma_{k})_{k\geq 1}
converges by showing that
(\sigma_{k}^{2})_{k\geq 1}
is a Cauchy sequence. Let
k^{*}\geq\widetilde{k}_{0}(c)
, where
\widetilde{k}_{0}(c)
is as in
Lemma

7.4
.
Let
k_{1},k_{2}\geq k^{*}
. Note that by
Lemma

7.4
,

\displaystyle|\sigma_{k_{1}}^{2}-\sigma_{k_{2}}^{2}|

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{n}\left|\mathbb{V}\left[\widehat{L}_{k_{1}}\right]-\mathbb{V}\left[\widehat{L}_{k_{2}}\right]\right|=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{n}\left|\mathbb{V}\left[\widehat{L}_{k_{1}}\right]-\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{L}\right]+\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{L}\right]-\mathbb{V}\left[\widehat{L}_{k_{2}}\right]\right|

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{n}\left|\mathbb{V}\left[\widehat{L}_{k_{1}}\right]-\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{L}\right]\right|+\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{n}\left|\mathbb{V}\left[\widehat{L}_{k_{2}}\right]-\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{L}\right]\right|

\displaystyle\leq 2\left(\frac{4}{5}\right)^{\frac{k_{1}}{2}}+2\left(\frac{4}{5}\right)^{\frac{k_{2}}{2}}\leq 4\left(\frac{4}{5}\right)^{\frac{k^{*}}{2}}.

It follows that
(\sigma_{k}^{2})_{k\geq 1}
is a Cauchy sequence.
The bounds on
\sigma
follow from the bounds on
\sigma_{k}
in
Lemma

7.3
.
∎

7.3.
A central limit theorem for
\widehat{L}_{k}

We first note that
Lemma

3.8
easily implies the following central limit theorem for truncations of weighted neighbourhood sums whose variance is not too small, which in particular, by
Lemma

7.3

(i)
, implies
Lemma

2.7
.

Lemma 7.6
.

Let
c>1
, let
p=\frac{c}{n}
and let
G\sim G(n,p)
. Let
\widehat{M}_{k}
be the truncation of a weighted neighbourhood sum. If
\mathbb{V}\left[\widehat{M}_{k}\right]=\omega\left(n^{\frac{2}{3}}\right)
, then

\frac{\widehat{M}_{k}-\mathbb{E}\left[\widehat{M}_{k}\right]}{\left(\mathbb{V}\left[\widehat{M}_{k}\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\quad\overset{d}{\to}\quad\mathcal{N}(0,1)\quad\text{ as }n\to\infty.

Proof.

Suppose
\widehat{M}_{k}
is the truncation (as in (
21
)) of a neighbourhood sum

\widetilde{M}_{k}=\sum_{v\in V(G)}\sum_{(H,r)\in\mathcal{H}}\beta_{(H,r)}\mathds{1}_{\{(G[B_{G}(v,k)],v)\cong(H,r)\}}.

As in
50
, we can write
\widehat{M}_{k}=\sum_{U\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}Y_{U}
, where
\mathcal{U}\coloneqq\{U\subseteq V(G)\colon 1\leq|U|\leq t_{k}\}
,

Y_{U}~\coloneqq~\sum_{v\in U}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(T,r)\in\mathcal{T}\\
v(T)=|U|\end{subarray}}\beta_{(T,r)}I(U,v,T,r),

and
I(U,v,T,r)\coloneqq\mathds{1}_{\{B_{G}(v,k)=U\}}\mathds{1}_{\{(G[U],v)\cong(T,r)\}}
. Observe that for each
U\in\mathcal{U}
, we have that
Y_{U}
is semi-induced with respect to
U
.

Thus, it is easy to see that the conditions of
Lemma

3.8
are satisfied for
Y=\widehat{M}_{k}
with
t_{k}
playing the roles of both
t
and
K
. Hence, by
Lemma

3.8
, we have

\frac{\widehat{M}_{k}-\mathbb{E}\left[\widehat{M}_{k}\right]}{\left(\mathbb{V}\left[\widehat{M}_{k}\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\quad\overset{d}{\to}\quad\mathcal{N}(0,1)\quad\text{ as }n\rightarrow\infty,

as desired.
∎

7.4.
Proof of Lemma
2.8

We first note that
(L1)
follows immediately from
Lemma

7.3
. Furthermore,
(L3)
and the first part of
(L2)
follow from
Lemma

7.5
.

For the second part of
(L2)
we argue as follows. By
Theorem

2.1
,
Lemma

2.3
, and
Lemma

2.5
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}\left[L-\widetilde{L}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[(L-\widetilde{L})^{2}\right]=O(1);

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{L}-\widetilde{L}_{k}\right]\leq\left(64ck^{2}\left(\frac{3}{4}\right)^{k-2}+o(1)\right)n;\text{ and }

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{L}_{k}-\widehat{L}_{k}\right]\leq\exp\left(-c^{2}k^{2}\right)n+O(1).

Furthermore, since by
Theorem

3.1
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}\left[X\right]\leq\mathbb{V}\left[X-Y\right]+\mathbb{V}\left[Y\right]+2\left(\mathbb{V}\left[X-Y\right]\mathbb{V}\left[Y\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

we can deduce from the above, and the fact that

\mathbb{V}\left[\widehat{L}_{k}\right]=O(n)
(see
Lemma

7.1
), that also
\mathbb{V}\left[L\right],\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{L}\right]
, and
\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{L}_{k}\right]
are
O(n)
.

It then follows by repeated applications of
Theorem

3.1
that

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\mathbb{V}\left[L(G)\right]}{n}=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{L}\right]}{n}=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\mathbb{V}\left[\widehat{L}_{k}\right]}{n}=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\sigma_{k}^{2}=\sigma^{2},

establishing the second part of
(L2)
.

8.
Discussion

In this paper, we prove a central limit theorem for the circumference of a sparse random graph.
Our proof
has essentially two main parts: we first establish a ‘local’ approximation to the circumference, for which a central limit theorem can be proven using standard techniques; and we then use the Efron–Stein inequality to control the accuracy of our approximation and in this way deduce a central limit theorem from the local version.

We complement our main result by proving that the circumference of a random graph exhibits the same behaviour up to the Hamiltonicity threshold. In addition, we show that at the Hamiltonicity threshold the difference between the number of vertices and the circumference of a random graph follows the Poisson paradigm.

Unlike many other graph parameters for which a central limit theorem has been established, for example, subgraph counts
[
20
,
26
,
27
,
37
]
, the order of the giant component
[
12
,
35
,
36
]
or the order of the
k
-core
[
24
]
, there is not an obvious ‘local’ approximation to the circumference, however the main novelty in this regard comes from earlier work of the first author
[
3
]
and the first author and Frieze
[
5
]
, see for example
Theorem

2.1
, where they established an explicit structural parameter which has a ‘local’ approximation and which is close in distribution to the circumference.

Our main contribution is then the application of the Efron–Stein inequality in this context to ‘lift’ properties of these approximations by analysing local changes under the resampling of edges, together with the non-trivial combinatorial analysis of the local changes of this approximation under resampling.

It seems likely that these methods, both the local approximations coming from
[
3
,
5
]
and the use of the Efron–Stein inequality to transfer a central limit theorem from an approximation, can be applied to prove central limit theorems in other contexts.

As a simple example, since the
k
-core can be defined via a similar peeling process as in
Subsection

2.1
, there is a natural ‘local’ approximation to the order of the
k
-core in the vein of
Subsection

2.1
, for which a central limit theorem can be established via
Lemma

3.8
. It is reasonable to believe that the changes in the difference between the
k
-core and this approximation under resampling can be shown to be small and ‘local’ as in
Section

4
, and so these methods should lead to an alternative proof of a central limit theorem for the order of the
k
-core for a suitable range of
p
, which we intend to address in future work
[
4
]
.

As a more complex example, it seems likely that similar methods should give an alternative proof that the matching number of
G\left(n,\frac{c}{n}\right)
satisfies a central limit theorem for
c
sufficiently large, which was recently proved for
c>e
by Kreacic
[
31
]
and for
c\leq e
by Glasgow, Kwan, Sah and Sawhney
[
22
]
.

Indeed, similar arguments as in
[
3
]
should show that for
c
sufficiently large, if we take the colouring
V(G)=S\cup P\cup R
given by
Section

2.1
, then
G[S\cup P]
is robust enough that any matching on
G[P\cup R]
can be extended to a matching which covers all but at most one vertex of
S\cup P
. In particular, if a vertex is contained in a component of
G[P\cup R]
of size at most
k
, then the ‘contribution’ of
v
towards the matching number can already be ‘recognised’ in
B_{G}(v,k)
. This would lead to a similar ‘local’ approximation to the matching number, for which most of the analysis in this paper would follow verbatim.
Of course, there are many technical details left to check to verify that the approximations in these cases are appropriately ‘robust’ under the resampling of edges, and that the variances involved are large enough to apply Stein’s method (c.f.
Lemma

3.8
).

Whilst
Theorem

1.2
goes slightly beyond a central limit theorem, giving some quantitative control on the variance
\mathbb{V}\left[L\right]
of the circumference
L\coloneqq L\left(G\left(n,\frac{c}{n}\right)\right)
, our upper and lower bounds on
\sigma=\lim_{n\to\infty}\left(\frac{\mathbb{V}\left[L\right]}{n}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
are quite far apart as functions of
c
. It would be interesting to know what the actual asymptotic dependence of
\sigma
on
c
is, where we would expect the answer to be closer to the upper bound than the lower bound. Furthermore, given
Theorem

1.2
, we find ourselves in the situation that, for
c\geq 20
, we know that
L
is concentrated in a reasonably small interval about its mean
\mu:=\mathbb{E}\left[L\right]
, in that
L=\mu+O_{\bm{p}}(\sqrt{n})
, but the best known estimate for
\mu
, which follows from
Theorem

1.1
, only gives
\mu=f(c)n+o_{\bm{p}}(n)
. Hence, it would be interesting to obtain a more precise estimate for
\mu
. More ambitiously, it would be interesting to determine if
L
satisfies a
local limit theorem
, in the sense that, for any compact interval
I\subseteq\mathbb{R}
and
\delta>0
, there exists
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
n\geq n_{0}
, we have that if
k=k(n)\in\mathbb{N}
is such that
\frac{k-\mu}{\sqrt{n}}\in I
, then

(1-\delta)\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi n}\sigma}\exp\left(-\frac{(k-\mu)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}n}\right)\leq\mathbb{P}\left[L=k\right]\leq(1+\delta)\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi n}\sigma}\exp\left(-\frac{(k-\mu)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}n}\right).

Finally, as mentioned in the introduction, whilst the distribution of the circumference of a sparse random graph
G\left(n,\frac{c}{n}\right)
is well understood now when
c
is large, there is still a large gap in our knowledge for smaller
c
, where even a scaling limit has not yet been established. It would be interesting to know if the lower bound
c\geq 20
can be improved upon significantly, where we note that for small enough values of
c
, for example below the threshold for the existence of a
3
-core, there does not seem to be a way to apply the methods used in
[
3
]
and in this paper. In this paper, we specifically use the lower bound on
c
in the proofs of
Lemmas

4.16
and
6.1
, and it is also needed to apply
Theorem

2.1
.
Roughly, a large
c
is needed to ensure the existence of the strong
4
-core and to show that the
mantle
, what remains when we delete the strong
4
-core, is roughly distributed like a sub-critical random graph.
Heuristically, there should be some threshold
c^{*}
at which the strong
4
-core emerges, and above which the mantle should have such a sub-critical distribution. It would be interesting to determine the value of
c^{*}
, which computational experiments suggest is
\approx 9.2
, and more generally the threshold for the existence of strong a
k
-core for general
k\in\mathbb{N}
. We note that, in the case
k=4
, the conjectured threshold is much larger than the corresponding threshold for the
4
-core, which is known to be
\approx 5.15
, and instead lies between the threshold for the
6
-core (
\approx 8.37
) and the threshold for the
7
-core (
\approx 9.88
).

Appendix A
Proof of Proposition
1.3

This proof is most easily understood when read after reading the rest of the paper, as it uses some results and ideas from the proof of Theorem
1.2
, and we give less detail where arguments are similar to earlier parts of the paper.

We first note that for
\lambda(n)\to\infty
, a classic result of Bollobás
[
10
]
and Komlós and Szemerédi
[
30
]
implies that whp
G(n,p)
is Hamiltonian, and so
n=L(G)
, dealing with
(3)
. Let us suppose then, in what follows, that
\lambda(n)
is bounded above by a constant and thus
c=\log n+\log\log n+\lambda(n)\leq 2\log n
.

Let
V(G)=S\cup P\cup R
be the partition given by
Section

2.1
and let
\widetilde{L}
be defined as in
Theorem

2.1
, so that
\mathbb{P}\left[\widetilde{L}(G)\neq L(G)\right]=O\left(n^{-2}\right)
, and so it suffices to show that
\widetilde{L}(G)
satisfies
Proposition

1.3
in place of
L(G)
. Moreover, note that by
Observation

4.1
, a component containing
i
red vertices has at most
3i
purple vertices and a similar argument as in
Lemma

4.15
shows that, for
c=O(\log n)
, whp all components of
G[P\cup R]
have order at most
\log^{4}n
.

Let us first consider the case where
\lambda(n)\rightarrow\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
. In this case, we note that a simple first moment calculation, as in (
18
), implies that whp there are no components of
G[P\cup R]
containing more than one red vertex.
The probability that there is some component of
G[P\cup R]
containing more than one red vertex is at most

\displaystyle\sum_{i\geq 2}^{4\log^{4}n}\sum_{j=0}^{3i}\binom{n}{j+i}\binom{j+i}{i}(j+i)^{j+i-2}p^{j+i-1}(1-p)^{i(n-j-i)}=o(1).

However, if a component
K
contains a single red vertex
v
, then
\mathrm{uc}(G[K],P\cap K)
is equal to
0
if
d(v)\geq 2
, and
1
if
d(v)\leq 1
. On the other hand, every vertex of degree
\leq 1
lies in
R
and hence whp

\widetilde{L}(G)=n-n_{0}(G)-n_{1}(G),

where
n_{i}(G)
is the number of vertices of degree
i
in
G
. It follows that
\widetilde{L}(G)-(n-n_{0}(G)-n_{1}(G))\overset{p}{\to}0
, as
n\rightarrow\infty
. Hence, standard results on the distribution of
n_{0}(G)+n_{1}(G)
, see for example
[
18
, Section 3]
,
imply that
(2)
holds.

The case where
\lambda(n)\rightarrow-\infty
and
c\rightarrow\infty
is slightly more delicate, but follows a similar idea. We write

\displaystyle\widetilde{L}(G)

\displaystyle=(\widetilde{L}(G)-n_{0}(G)-n_{1}(G))+n_{0}(G)+n_{1}(G)

\displaystyle\eqqcolon Y+n_{0}(G)+n_{1}(G).

Since, by standard results,
n_{1}(G)
satisfies a central limit theorem in this regime of
p
, it will be sufficient to show that

(57)

\displaystyle\max\{\mathbb{V}\left[n_{0}(G)\right],\mathbb{V}\left[Y\right]\}=o\left(\mathbb{V}\left[n_{1}(G)\right]\right).

Indeed, note that if we assume
57
, then by Chebyshev’s inequality, we have as
n\rightarrow\infty
,

\displaystyle\frac{\widetilde{L}(G)-\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{L}(G)\right]}{\sqrt{\mathbb{V}\left[n_{1}(G)\right]}}-\frac{n_{1}(G)-\mathbb{E}\left[n_{1}(G)\right]}{\sqrt{\mathbb{V}\left[n_{1}(G)\right]}}=\frac{Y-\mathbb{E}\left[Y\right]}{\sqrt{\mathbb{V}\left[n_{1}(G)\right]}}+\frac{n_{0}(G)-\mathbb{E}\left[n_{0}(G)\right]}{\sqrt{\mathbb{V}\left[n_{1}(G)\right]}}\overset{p}{\to}0.

This implies that as
n\rightarrow\infty
,

\displaystyle\frac{\widetilde{L}(G)-\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{L}(G)\right]}{\sqrt{\mathbb{V}\left[n_{1}(G)\right]}}\quad\overset{d}{\to}\quad\mathcal{N}(0,1).

Since
57
and
Theorem

3.1
imply that
\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{L}(G)\right]=(1+o(1))\mathbb{V}\left[n_{1}(G)\right]
, we have by Slutsky’s theorem (
Theorem

3.2
), that as
n\rightarrow\infty
,

\displaystyle\frac{\widetilde{L}(G)-\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{L}(G)\right]}{\sqrt{\mathbb{V}\left[\widetilde{L}(G)\right]}}\quad\overset{d}{\to}\quad\mathcal{N}(0,1).

Thus it suffices to show
57
.

Again by standard results, the variances of
n_{0}(G)
and
n_{1}(G)
are known to satisfy
